It's during weeks 2–4 of antidepressant therapy — energy comes back before mood does, and suddenly a patient who was too depleted to act on suicidal thoughts now has the fuel. Know ECT too: it's for treatment-resistant depression, it's safe and effective (not the horror show from movies), and the nursing care is straightforward — NPO before, informed consent, and monitor afterward for confusion, headache, and short-term memory changes.

**PRACTICE:**
A patient started on sertraline 3 weeks ago reports having "more energy" but still feeling "hopeless." Which concern should the nurse prioritize?
A. The medication is not working and should be changed
B. The patient may be at increased risk for suicide due to improved energy with persistent hopelessness
C. The patient needs the dose increased immediately
D. The patient's improvement indicates the medication is effective

**Answer: B.** When energy improves before mood does, the patient may have the energy to act on suicidal ideation. This is the highest-risk period. The nurse should assess for suicidal ideation immediately. Option A is premature — SSRIs can take 4–6 weeks. Option C requires provider evaluation, not nurse-driven dose changes. Option D is partially true but misses the critical safety concern. *Why B works:* The disconnect between improved energy and persistent hopelessness is a well-documented risk pattern.

---

## Mental Health Disorders: Anxiety Disorders
**Psychosocial Integrity** · **Exam Weight: 9%** · Connects to: Therapeutic Communication, Crisis Intervention

**WHAT:** Anxiety disorders involve excessive, persistent worry or fear that is disproportionate to the actual situation and causes significant distress or functional impairment. Includes generalized anxiety disorder (GAD), panic disorder, phobias, OCD, and PTSD.

**WHY IT MATTERS:** Anxiety is the most commonly experienced psychiatric symptom — you'll encounter it in virtually every clinical setting, not just psychiatric units. Hospitalized patients are anxious. Surgical patients are anxious. Understanding anxiety helps you help every patient.

**RECOGNIZE:**
- **Mild anxiety:** Increased alertness, enhanced learning — actually beneficial. Use this for patient teaching.
- **Moderate anxiety:** Narrowed perceptual field, selective attention, may need redirection
- **Severe anxiety:** Very narrow focus, difficulty processing information, physical symptoms intensify (tachycardia, diaphoresis, nausea, tremors, chest tightness)
- **Panic:** Complete perceptual disorganization, inability to function, feeling of impending doom, terror. Can mimic cardiac events (chest pain, shortness of breath, palpitations)

**ACT:**
1. For mild–moderate: therapeutic communication, teach relaxation techniques, provide information (reduces anxiety of the unknown)
2. For severe anxiety: remain calm, stay with the patient, use short simple statements, reduce stimulation, do NOT try to teach — they can't learn in this state
3. For panic: ensure safety, stay with the patient, calm presence, breathe WITH them, reduce environmental stimulation, medications as ordered
4. Teach long-term management: cognitive-behavioral strategies, relaxation techniques, regular exercise, adequate sleep, limiting caffeine and alcohol

**MEDS:**
- Benzodiazepines (lorazepam, alprazolam, diazepam): Short-term, acute anxiety relief. *Why?* Enhance GABA activity for rapid calming effect. Risk of dependence with long-term use — not intended as a long-term solution. Do not combine with alcohol or other CNS depressants.
- SSRIs/SNRIs: First-line for long-term management. *Why?* They address the underlying neurotransmitter imbalance without the dependence risk of benzodiazepines.
- Buspirone: Non-sedating anxiolytic for chronic anxiety. *Why?* No dependence risk, but takes 2–4 weeks for effect. Not useful for acute anxiety.

**CONNECT:**
→ See Section 4: Therapeutic Communication — communication techniques for anxious patients
→ See Section 4: Coping Mechanisms — anxiety drives coping behaviors
→ See Section 5 (Physiological Integrity): Cardiac — differentiating panic from cardiac events

**TEST TIP:** The NCLEX tests anxiety levels and appropriate nursing interventions for each level. Key concept: a patient in severe anxiety or panic CANNOT learn. Don't try to teach — first reduce the anxiety to a moderate level, then educate. Also: benzodiazepines + alcohol = respiratory depression. And buspirone takes weeks to work — if the question presents acute panic, benzodiazepines are the pharmacological answer, not buspirone.

**PRACTICE:**
A patient in the emergency department reports sudden onset chest tightness, palpitations, and a feeling of "impending doom." Cardiac workup is negative. The patient is hyperventilating. The nurse's best initial action is:
A. Administer oxygen via nasal cannula
B. Stay with the patient, speak calmly, and guide controlled breathing
C. Obtain a psychiatric consultation immediately
D. Teach the patient about the physiology of panic attacks

**Answer: B.** This is a panic attack. The immediate intervention is presence, calm communication, and guided breathing. The patient cannot learn right now (Option D is inappropriate during panic). Oxygen (Option A) may not be indicated if SpO2 is normal — the issue is hyperventilation, not hypoxia. Psychiatric consultation (Option C) may be appropriate but is not the immediate action. *Why B works:* A calm, supportive presence with guided breathing addresses the immediate crisis.

---

## Mental Health Disorders: Bipolar Disorder
**Psychosocial Integrity** · **Exam Weight: 9%** · Connects to: Depression, Pharmacology, Safety

**WHAT:** Bipolar disorder is a mood disorder characterized by episodes of mania (or hypomania) alternating with episodes of depression. During manic episodes, patients demonstrate elevated or irritable mood, increased energy, decreased need for sleep, grandiosity, pressured speech, impulsivity, and poor judgment.

**WHY IT MATTERS:** Manic patients are at risk for self-harm through impulsive behavior — spending sprees, risky sexual activity, aggressive confrontations, and refusal of treatment. They may also be unable to attend to basic needs (nutrition, sleep, hygiene) due to hyperactivity.

**RECOGNIZE:**
- **Mania:** Elevated/expansive/irritable mood, grandiosity, flight of ideas, pressured speech, decreased sleep (sometimes going days without rest), psychomotor agitation, distractibility, involvement in risky activities, possible psychotic features (delusions of grandeur)
- **Hypomania:** Similar but less severe, no psychotic features, minimal functional impairment
- **Depressive episodes:** Same as major depression (see Depression section)

**ACT:**
1. **Safety first** — the manic patient's impaired judgment is the primary concern
2. Provide a low-stimulation environment — reduce noise, lighting, social interactions
3. Offer high-calorie, portable foods (the manic patient won't sit down to eat) — "walk-and-eat" snacks
4. Monitor sleep — sleep deprivation worsens mania
5. Set firm, consistent limits on behavior — matter-of-fact, non-confrontational
6. Redirect — don't argue. You won't win an argument with a manic patient, and escalation is dangerous.
7. Monitor medication adherence — patients often stop medications during mania because they "feel great"
8. Maintain a structured routine with minimal choices

**MEDS:**
- Lithium: Classic mood stabilizer, gold standard for bipolar maintenance. *Why?* Stabilizes mood by modulating neurotransmitter activity. **Critical: narrow therapeutic index.** Therapeutic level: 0.6–1.2 mEq/L (maintenance). Monitor levels regularly. Toxicity signs: GI symptoms (nausea, vomiting, diarrhea) → neurological symptoms (tremor, ataxia, slurred speech) → severe toxicity (seizures, coma). Dehydration and sodium depletion increase lithium levels. Ensure adequate fluid and sodium intake.
- Valproic acid (Depakote): Mood stabilizer/anticonvulsant. *Why?* Stabilizes mood, particularly useful for rapid-cycling bipolar. Monitor liver function and CBC. Therapeutic level: 50–100 mcg/mL.
- Carbamazepine (Tegretol): Alternative mood stabilizer. *Why?* Reduces mood cycling. Monitor blood levels and CBC (risk of agranulocytosis).
- Atypical antipsychotics (olanzapine, quetiapine, aripiprazole): Used for acute mania and maintenance. *Why?* Address psychotic features and mood stabilization. Monitor for metabolic syndrome (weight gain, hyperglycemia, hyperlipidemia).

**CONNECT:**
→ See Section 4: Depression — the depressive phase of bipolar disorder
→ See Section 4: Crisis Intervention — acute mania may require crisis management
→ See Section 5 (Physiological Integrity): Pharmacology — lithium monitoring details

**TEST TIP:** Lithium is the NCLEX's favorite medication to test — treat it like a best friend you need to know everything about. Therapeutic range: 0.6–1.2 mEq/L. Early toxicity signs: GI symptoms and coarse tremor. What spikes levels: dehydration, sodium depletion, NSAIDs. The classic scenario they'll throw at you: patient on lithium starts vomiting and having diarrhea. You hold the lithium, push fluids, and check the level. For manic behavior questions, the answer is almost always limit-setting and managing the environment — not restraints, not confrontation.

**PRACTICE:**
A nurse is caring for a patient on lithium who reports nausea, vomiting, and coarse hand tremors. The patient's most recent lithium level is 1.8 mEq/L. The nurse should:
A. Administer the next scheduled lithium dose and recheck the level in 12 hours
B. Hold the lithium, ensure hydration, and notify the provider immediately
C. Encourage the patient to increase sodium intake and continue the medication
D. Administer an antiemetic and continue monitoring

**Answer: B.** A lithium level of 1.8 mEq/L exceeds the therapeutic range (0.6–1.2) and the symptoms (nausea, vomiting, tremor) indicate lithium toxicity. Hold the medication immediately, hydrate, and notify the provider for further management. Giving more lithium (A) or continuing it (C, D) would worsen toxicity. *Why B works:* Lithium toxicity can rapidly progress to life-threatening levels — immediate intervention is essential.

---

## Mental Health Disorders: Schizophrenia
**Psychosocial Integrity** · **Exam Weight: 9%** · Connects to: Therapeutic Communication, Safety, Pharmacology

**WHAT:** Schizophrenia is a chronic, severe mental health disorder characterized by disturbances in thought, perception, emotion, and behavior. It involves positive symptoms (additions to normal experience: hallucinations, delusions, disorganized thinking) and negative symptoms (subtractions from normal functioning: flat affect, social withdrawal, avolition, poverty of speech).

**WHY IT MATTERS:** A patient with schizophrenia is hearing things you can't hear and believing things that aren't real — and your response in that moment matters enormously. The NCLEX tests whether you can tell delusions from hallucinations, respond without reinforcing or arguing, and manage antipsychotics without letting the side effects become their own emergency.

**RECOGNIZE:**
- **Positive symptoms:** Hallucinations (auditory most common — hearing voices), delusions (fixed false beliefs — persecutory, grandiose, referential), disorganized speech (loose associations, tangentiality, word salad), disorganized or catatonic behavior
- **Negative symptoms:** Flat or blunted affect, alogia (poverty of speech), avolition (lack of motivation), anhedonia, social withdrawal — these are often more debilitating long-term and harder to treat
- **Cognitive symptoms:** Impaired attention, working memory deficits, difficulty with executive function

**ACT:**
1. **Safety assessment:** Assess for harm to self or others — command hallucinations (voices telling the patient to do something) require immediate safety evaluation
2. **Do not argue with delusions or hallucinations.** You won't convince the patient they're not real. Instead, acknowledge the patient's experience without reinforcing the delusion: "I understand that the voices feel very real to you. I don't hear them, but I can see they're upsetting you."
3. Present reality without confrontation: "I see a shadow on the wall, not a person."
4. Use concrete, simple, direct communication — avoid abstractions, metaphors, or lengthy explanations
5. Reduce environmental stimulation during psychotic episodes
6. Promote medication adherence — explain benefits in concrete terms
7. Assist with ADLs as needed — negative symptoms can impair self-care
8. Build trust through consistency and predictability

**MEDS:**
- Typical (first-generation) antipsychotics (haloperidol, chlorpromazine): Primarily treat positive symptoms. *Why?* Block dopamine D2 receptors. Major risk: extrapyramidal symptoms (EPS) — dystonia, akathisia, pseudoparkinsonism, tardive dyskinesia (late-onset, potentially irreversible involuntary movements).
- Atypical (second-generation) antipsychotics (risperidone, olanzapine, quetiapine, clozapine, aripiprazole): Treat both positive and negative symptoms. *Why?* Act on both dopamine and serotonin receptors. Lower EPS risk but higher metabolic risk (weight gain, diabetes, hyperlipidemia).
- **Clozapine:** Reserved for treatment-resistant schizophrenia. *Why?* Most effective antipsychotic but carries risk of agranulocytosis (severe drop in white blood cells). Requires regular blood monitoring (ANC monitoring) — no refills without current blood work.
- **Neuroleptic malignant syndrome (NMS):** Life-threatening reaction to antipsychotics. Signs: high fever (hyperthermia >104°F), severe muscle rigidity ("lead pipe"), altered mental status, autonomic instability (tachycardia, labile BP, diaphoresis), elevated creatine kinase (CK). This is a medical emergency — stop the antipsychotic immediately, cool the patient, supportive care.

**CONNECT:**
→ See Section 4: Therapeutic Communication — specific techniques for psychotic patients
→ See Section 4: Crisis Intervention — managing acute psychosis
→ See Section 5 (Physiological Integrity): Pharmacology — antipsychotic monitoring

**TEST TIP:** When the NCLEX presents a patient hearing voices, the first thing to assess is what the voices are saying — command hallucinations (telling the patient to harm themselves or others) are a safety priority. "Tell me what the voices are saying" is the correct assessment question. Don't argue, don't agree — acknowledge and redirect. For medication questions: typical antipsychotics = EPS risk (think: haloperidol and dystonia); atypical antipsychotics = metabolic risk (think: olanzapine and weight gain); clozapine = agranulocytosis (think: blood work). NMS is tested frequently — fever + rigidity + altered mental status = stop the drug, emergency.

**PRACTICE:**
A patient on haloperidol suddenly develops a temperature of 105°F, severe muscle rigidity, and altered consciousness. The nurse should:
A. Administer acetaminophen and continue monitoring vital signs
B. Hold the haloperidol, call a rapid response, and begin cooling measures
C. Administer an anticholinergic medication for extrapyramidal symptoms
D. Reassure the patient and increase fluid intake

**Answer: B.** These are the hallmark signs of neuroleptic malignant syndrome — a medical emergency. Immediate actions: stop the causative medication, call for emergency response, initiate cooling. NMS can be fatal without intervention. Option A doesn't address the cause. Option C treats EPS but NMS is beyond EPS. Option D is dangerously inadequate. *Why B works:* NMS is a life-threatening emergency requiring immediate drug discontinuation and medical intervention.

---

## Substance Use Disorders
**Psychosocial Integrity** · **Exam Weight: 9%** · Connects to: Pharmacology, Crisis Intervention, Legal Issues

**WHAT:** Substance use disorders (SUDs) involve the problematic use of substances (alcohol, opioids, stimulants, sedatives, and others) leading to clinically significant impairment, health consequences, and inability to control use despite negative outcomes.

**WHY IT MATTERS:** SUDs affect patients across all healthcare settings — not just psychiatric or addiction treatment centers. The nurse must recognize intoxication and withdrawal, manage these safely, and approach patients with clinical objectivity and without judgment.

**MECHANISM:**

**Depressant Withdrawal (Alcohol, Benzodiazepines):**
Depressants suppress the central nervous system. When they're removed, the CNS rebounds — think of it as the brain becoming overexcited without its usual brake. Withdrawal is the *opposite* of intoxication: stimulation, agitation, seizures, potentially death.
- **Alcohol withdrawal timeline:** Begins 6–24 hours after last drink. Tremors, anxiety, nausea, tachycardia. Peak: 24–72 hours. Delirium tremens (DTs) may develop 48–72+ hours after cessation: severe confusion, hallucinations (often visual — "seeing bugs"), profound autonomic instability, seizures. DTs can be fatal.
- **Benzodiazepine withdrawal:** Similar to alcohol — seizure risk. Requires gradual tapering, not abrupt cessation.

**Stimulant Effects (Cocaine, Amphetamines):**
- **Intoxication:** Euphoria, hyperactivity, tachycardia, hypertension, dilated pupils, hyperthermia, agitation, paranoia. Risk: cardiac arrhythmia, stroke, seizures.
- **Withdrawal:** "Crash" — fatigue, depression, increased appetite, hypersomnia. Not typically life-threatening but psychologically distressing.

**Opioid Effects (Heroin, Prescription Opioids, Fentanyl):**
- **Intoxication/Overdose:** Respiratory depression (primary concern), pinpoint pupils (miosis), altered consciousness, hypotension. Respiratory arrest is the cause of death.
- **Withdrawal:** Extremely uncomfortable but generally not life-threatening: muscle aches, lacrimation (tearing), rhinorrhea (runny nose), yawning, diarrhea, nausea, vomiting, piloerection ("goosebumps"), anxiety. Looks and feels like a severe flu.

**RECOGNIZE:**
- Alcohol intoxication: slurred speech, impaired coordination, altered judgment, possible respiratory depression at high levels
- Alcohol withdrawal: tremors, anxiety, diaphoresis, tachycardia → hallucinations → seizures → DTs
- Opioid overdose: pinpoint pupils + respiratory depression + decreased LOC = give naloxone
- Stimulant intoxication: dilated pupils, hypertension, tachycardia, agitation, paranoia

**ACT:**
1. Assess: current substance use, last use, history of withdrawal, previous withdrawal complications (seizures, DTs)
2. Monitor withdrawal using standardized tools (CIWA-Ar for alcohol, COWS for opioids)
3. Ensure safety during withdrawal — particularly seizure precautions for alcohol/benzodiazepine withdrawal
4. Maintain hydration and nutrition
5. Administer medications as ordered for withdrawal management
6. Use therapeutic communication — nonjudgmental, empathetic, direct
7. Provide information about treatment resources when the patient is receptive
8. Educate about overdose prevention and naloxone availability

**MEDS:**
- Benzodiazepines (chlordiazepoxide, lorazepam, diazepam): First-line for alcohol withdrawal. *Why?* They work on the same GABA receptors as alcohol, preventing the CNS overexcitement that causes seizures and DTs. Administer based on symptom severity (CIWA protocol).
- Naloxone (Narcan): Opioid antagonist for overdose reversal. *Why?* Competitively blocks opioid receptors, reversing respiratory depression. Onset is rapid (1–2 minutes IV). Monitor for re-sedation — naloxone's duration may be shorter than the opioid's, requiring repeat doses.
- Methadone and buprenorphine: Medication-assisted treatment (MAT) for opioid use disorder. *Why?* They reduce cravings and prevent withdrawal, supporting recovery without the euphoria and risks of illicit opioid use.
- Disulfiram (Antabuse): Alcohol deterrent. *Why?* Causes severe nausea, vomiting, and other unpleasant effects when alcohol is consumed, creating a deterrent effect. Patient must be fully informed and consenting.
- Naltrexone: Reduces alcohol cravings and blocks opioid effects. *Why?* Antagonist that reduces the rewarding effects of alcohol and opioids.

**CONNECT:**
→ See Section 4: Crisis Intervention — overdose is a crisis situation
→ See Section 4: Therapeutic Communication — nonjudgmental approach essential
→ See Section 1 (Management of Care): Legal Issues — mandatory reporting in some situations (impaired providers)
→ See Section 5 (Physiological Integrity): Pharmacology — naloxone, methadone details

**TEST TIP:** The NCLEX consistently tests two key concepts: (1) alcohol withdrawal CAN kill — seizures and DTs are medical emergencies requiring benzodiazepines, and (2) opioid overdose requires naloxone immediately when respiratory depression is present. A classic question: patient found unresponsive with pinpoint pupils and a respiratory rate of 6 — what's your first action? Administer naloxone. Also know that naloxone may precipitate acute withdrawal in opioid-dependent patients — this is uncomfortable but not dangerous (respiratory depression is dangerous). Approach all SUD questions clinically, not morally.

**PRACTICE:**
A patient admitted for alcohol detoxification begins showing tremors, diaphoresis, and a heart rate of 110 bpm 24 hours after the last drink. The CIWA-Ar score is 18. The nurse should:
A. Continue monitoring and reassess in 4 hours
B. Administer a benzodiazepine as prescribed by the withdrawal protocol
C. Apply physical restraints in case of seizure activity
D. Encourage oral fluids and provide a calm environment only

**Answer: B.** A CIWA-Ar score of 18 indicates moderate to severe withdrawal requiring pharmacological intervention. Benzodiazepines are the standard treatment to prevent progression to seizures and DTs. Option A delays necessary treatment. Option C — restraints don't prevent seizures and are inappropriate here. Option D addresses comfort but doesn't treat the withdrawal pharmacologically. *Why B works:* Symptom-triggered benzodiazepine therapy based on CIWA scoring is the evidence-based approach to alcohol withdrawal management.

---

## Grief & Loss and End-of-Life Care
**Psychosocial Integrity** · **Exam Weight: 9%** · Connects to: Therapeutic Communication, Ethical Practice, Advance Directives, Cultural Considerations

**WHAT:** Grief is the emotional, psychological, and physical response to loss. End-of-life care focuses on providing comfort, dignity, and support to patients who are dying and their families. These topics are intertwined — grief accompanies dying for both the patient and those who love them.

**WHY IT MATTERS:** Every nurse will care for dying patients and grieving families. The NCLEX tests your ability to provide compassionate, competent care during these profoundly human experiences — and to recognize when grief becomes complicated or pathological.

**MECHANISM:**

**Kübler-Ross Stages of Grief:**
These stages are NOT linear, not universal, and not required. They're a framework for understanding common grief responses:
1. **Denial:** "This can't be happening." A protective buffer against overwhelming news.
2. **Anger:** "Why me?" / "This isn't fair." Directed at self, others, providers, or the world.
3. **Bargaining:** "If only I had..." / "Maybe if I pray harder..." Attempting to negotiate for a different outcome.
4. **Depression:** Deep sadness as the reality of loss settles in. Quiet grief, withdrawal.
5. **Acceptance:** Not happiness — but acknowledgment of reality. May involve planning, saying goodbye, finding peace.

**Types of Grief:**
- **Anticipatory grief:** Grieving before the loss occurs — common in terminal diagnoses
- **Normal grief:** Follows a loss, involves a range of emotions, resolves over time (months to years)
- **Complicated grief:** Persistent, unresolved grief that significantly impairs functioning beyond 12 months. May require professional intervention.
- **Disenfranchised grief:** Grief that isn't socially acknowledged (loss of a pet, ex-partner, miscarriage, grief of LGBTQ+ partners in unsupportive environments)

**End-of-Life Care Priorities:**
- **Comfort:** Pain management (do NOT undertreat pain in dying patients), symptom management (nausea, dyspnea, anxiety, agitation)
- **Dignity:** Respect autonomy, maintain privacy, honor wishes
- **Support:** Emotional support for patient and family. Allow time for visits, rituals, spiritual care.
- **Communication:** Honest, compassionate, clear. Address questions directly. Allow silence.
- **Physical signs of approaching death:** Decreased consciousness, mottled skin, irregular respirations (Cheyne-Stokes), decreased urine output, cool extremities, "death rattle" (accumulation of secretions in the throat — position change and suctioning may help; anticholinergics to reduce secretions)

**RECOGNIZE:**
- Normal grief responses: crying, anger, sleep disturbances, appetite changes, preoccupation with the loss, social withdrawal — these are expected and don't require "treatment," just support
- Complicated grief indicators: inability to function in daily life beyond 12 months, persistent feeling that life is meaningless, intense yearning that doesn't diminish, inability to accept the reality of the loss
- Signs of approaching death (for patient and family education)

**ACT:**
1. Be present. Sometimes the most therapeutic thing you can do is sit quietly with a dying patient or a grieving family.
2. Use therapeutic communication: acknowledge the pain, avoid clichés ("they're in a better place"), allow silence
3. Honor the advance directive and DNR/DNI status
4. Manage symptoms aggressively — dying patients deserve comfort
5. Support cultural and spiritual practices around death and dying
6. Provide post-mortem care with dignity and respect
7. Support the family: allow time with the body, provide information about next steps, offer chaplain or social worker
8. Debrief as a team — caring for dying patients affects nurses too

**MEDS:**
- Morphine: First-line for end-of-life pain and dyspnea. *Why?* Effective for both pain relief and the sensation of breathlessness. In end-of-life care, adequate pain relief is the priority — fear of hastening death should not prevent adequate dosing (principle of double effect).
- Atropine or glycopyrrolate: Reduce secretions ("death rattle"). *Why?* Anticholinergics decrease secretion production, improving comfort for the patient and reducing distress for the family.
- Anxiolytics (lorazepam): For end-of-life anxiety and agitation. *Why?* Reduces suffering from anxiety that may accompany the dying process.

**CONNECT:**
→ See Section 1 (Management of Care): Advance Directives — guiding end-of-life decisions
→ See Section 1 (Management of Care): Ethical Practice — ethical dimensions of end-of-life care
→ See Section 4: Cultural & Spiritual Considerations — death rituals and practices
→ See Section 4: Therapeutic Communication — communicating with dying patients and grieving families

**TEST TIP:** Grief questions on the NCLEX have one golden rule: acknowledge the feeling, don't fix it. "I can see this is really painful for you" beats "They lived a long life" every single time. For end-of-life care, pain management is *the* priority — you do NOT withhold morphine from a dying patient because you're worried about respiratory depression. Comfort is the goal, period. One more: organ donation conversations get initiated by trained personnel, not by you at the bedside, unless your facility policy says otherwise.

**PRACTICE:**
A family member of a dying patient says to the nurse, "I just can't believe this is happening. He was fine last month." The nurse's best response is:
A. "The doctors did everything they could."
B. "It must be very difficult to see such a rapid change in his condition."
C. "Try to stay strong for the rest of the family."
D. "Would you like me to explain what's happening medically?"

**Answer: B.** This response acknowledges the family member's experience (the shock of rapid decline) and validates their feelings. Option A is a platitude that doesn't address the emotion. Option C dismisses the person's right to grieve by telling them to suppress their feelings. Option D might be appropriate later but doesn't address the emotional need being expressed right now. *Why B works:* It reflects the emotion and opens space for the family member to continue expressing their feelings.

---

## Crisis Intervention
**Psychosocial Integrity** · **Exam Weight: 9%** · Connects to: Therapeutic Communication, Safety, Mental Health Disorders, Substance Use

**WHAT:** A crisis is a state of psychological disequilibrium in which a person's usual coping mechanisms are overwhelmed and they cannot resolve the situation with their existing resources. Crisis intervention is the focused, time-limited nursing response aimed at restoring the person to their pre-crisis level of functioning.

**WHY IT MATTERS:** Crisis situations — whether suicidal ideation, acute psychosis, domestic violence, or overwhelming anxiety — require immediate, skilled nursing response. This is not a time for committee meetings or lengthy assessments. It's a time for presence, safety, and stabilization.

**MECHANISM:**
Crisis phases:
1. **Pre-crisis:** Baseline functioning. Coping mechanisms are adequate.
2. **Impact:** The triggering event overwhelms coping. Anxiety escalates.
3. **Crisis:** Disorganization. Inability to problem-solve. Emotional flooding.
4. **Resolution:** With support, new coping is developed and equilibrium is restored.

**ACT:**
1. **Ensure safety** — this is always first. Assess for harm to self or others.
2. **Be present and calm** — your calm is therapeutic
3. **Use active listening** — let the person tell their story
4. **Assess the situation:** What happened? What resources does the person have? What coping has been tried?
5. **Focus on the immediate problem** — crisis intervention is not long-term therapy. Address the here and now.
6. **Help identify coping strategies** — what has worked before? What resources are available?
7. **Develop a simple, concrete plan** — achievable steps, not abstract goals
8. **Arrange follow-up** — crisis resolution requires continuity

**For Patients Expressing Suicidal Ideation:**
- Ask directly: "Are you thinking about hurting yourself?" / "Are you thinking about suicide?" (Asking does NOT increase risk — it opens a critical conversation)
- Assess: Do they have a plan? Do they have access to means? What is the lethality of the plan? What is the timeline?
- Higher risk indicators: specific plan, access to lethal means, prior attempts, social isolation, giving away possessions, sudden improvement in mood after a period of depression
- **Do not leave a suicidal patient alone.** Arrange for constant observation.
- Remove potential means of self-harm from the environment
- Contract-for-safety (verbal agreements not to harm themselves) should NOT be used as a sole intervention — they are not evidence-based safeguards

**CONNECT:**
→ See Section 4: Therapeutic Communication — crisis communication skills
→ See Section 4: Mental Health Disorders — crises may arise from psychiatric conditions
→ See Section 4: Substance Use — overdose as a crisis
→ See Section 2 (Safety): Restraints — when behavioral crisis requires physical intervention

**TEST TIP:** When the NCLEX presents a patient in crisis, the first action is always safety. If the patient is suicidal: assess lethality. If the patient is violent: ensure staff and patient safety. After safety is established, the next step is therapeutic presence and active listening. The NCLEX also tests whether you know to ask directly about suicide — any answer choice that avoids the question ("I'm sure you'll feel better") is wrong. Direct, compassionate inquiry is correct.

**PRACTICE:**
A patient tells the nurse, "I've been saving up my pills. I plan to take them all this Friday when my wife goes out of town." Which response indicates the highest level of risk?
A. The patient has thought about death before but never had a plan
B. The patient has a specific method, access to the means, and a set timeline
C. The patient reports feeling sad but denies suicidal thoughts
D. The patient is tearful but states they would never act on their thoughts

**Answer: B.** This patient has all three components of high lethality: a specific plan (pill overdose), access to means (saved pills), and a timeline (Friday). This is a psychiatric emergency requiring immediate intervention — constant observation, removal of means, provider notification, likely inpatient psychiatric evaluation. *Why B is highest risk:* Plan + means + timeline is the highest-risk combination for completed suicide.

---

## Abuse & Neglect
**Psychosocial Integrity** · **Exam Weight: 9%** · Connects to: Legal Issues (Mandatory Reporting), Documentation, Safety

**WHAT:** Abuse and neglect include physical, emotional, sexual, and financial abuse of children, intimate partners, and vulnerable adults (elderly, disabled), as well as neglect (failure to provide necessary care, food, shelter, or medical treatment).

**WHY IT MATTERS:** You're a mandated reporter — every state, no exceptions. Miss the signs of abuse or neglect, fail to report, and you're not just making a clinical error — you're breaking the law and failing a patient who can't protect themselves. The NCLEX tests whether you can spot the indicators, assess without leading, document objectively, and report through the right channels.

**RECOGNIZE:**

**Physical Abuse Indicators:**
- Injuries inconsistent with the reported mechanism (e.g., spiral fractures in a non-mobile infant)
- Injuries in various stages of healing (suggesting ongoing abuse)
- Patterned injuries (belt marks, cigarette burns, bite marks)
- Delay in seeking treatment
- Injuries in unusual locations (inner arms, back, buttocks, genitals)

**Child Abuse Red Flags:**
- Excessive fearfulness or withdrawal
- Inappropriate sexual knowledge for age
- Frequent absences from school
- Parent/caregiver provides inconsistent explanations or minimizes injuries

**Intimate Partner Violence Indicators:**
- Frequent ED visits for "accidents"
- Partner who answers all questions, won't leave the room
- Injuries during pregnancy
- Patient is withdrawn, avoids eye contact, appears fearful
- Delayed prenatal care

**Elder Abuse Indicators:**
- Unexplained injuries, poor hygiene, weight loss, dehydration
- Caregiver who is dismissive, controlling, or speaks for the patient
- Financial exploitation: unexplained withdrawals, changes to legal documents
- Pressure sores or contractures suggesting neglect

**ACT:**
1. Interview the patient ALONE — separate from the suspected abuser. This is essential.
2. Use a nonjudgmental, direct approach: "I'm asking because I want to make sure you're safe."
3. Use screening tools (HITS scale, Danger Assessment for intimate partner violence)
4. Document findings objectively: exact location, size, shape, and color of injuries. Use the patient's own words in quotes. Photograph with consent per facility policy.
5. Report to the appropriate authorities — you are required by law to report suspected (not confirmed) abuse. You do not need proof. You do not need the patient's permission.
6. Develop a safety plan with the patient — especially for intimate partner violence (safe place to go, emergency numbers, important documents)
7. Provide referrals: domestic violence hotlines, shelters, adult protective services, child protective services, social work
8. Do NOT confront the suspected abuser — this can escalate danger

**CONNECT:**
→ See Section 1 (Management of Care): Legal Issues — mandatory reporting requirements
→ See Section 1 (Management of Care): Documentation — objective documentation of abuse findings
→ See Section 4: Therapeutic Communication — communicating with abuse survivors
→ See Section 3 (Health Promotion): Prenatal Care — screening during pregnancy

**TEST TIP:** The NCLEX tests mandatory reporting consistently. Key points: (1) you report *suspected* abuse — you don't need to confirm it, (2) the patient's consent is not required for reporting, (3) HIPAA allows disclosure for mandatory reporting, (4) interview the patient alone, (5) document objectively without drawing conclusions ("patient states 'he hit me'" — not "patient appears to be a domestic violence victim"). If the question asks about an elderly patient with unexplained bruises and a controlling caregiver, the correct answer involves assessing the patient alone and reporting.

**PRACTICE:**
During a home visit, a nurse notices an elderly patient has unexplained bruises on both upper arms and appears withdrawn. The patient's adult child answers all questions for the patient and refuses to leave the room. The nurse's most important action is:
A. Ask the adult child about the bruises in a supportive manner
B. Document the bruises and plan to reassess on the next visit
C. Find a reason to speak with the patient alone and assess for abuse
D. Report the findings to adult protective services immediately

**Answer: C.** The nurse needs to interview the patient alone to assess for abuse — the controlling caregiver's presence prevents honest disclosure. While reporting may be necessary (and likely will be), the nurse should first assess the situation by speaking with the patient privately. Option A asks the suspected abuser. Option B delays a time-sensitive assessment. Option D may be appropriate after the assessment but is premature without patient interview. *Why C works:* You can't assess for abuse with the suspected abuser in the room. Separating them is the critical first step.

---

## Coping Mechanisms
**Psychosocial Integrity** · **Exam Weight: 9%** · Connects to: Therapeutic Communication, Mental Health Disorders, All clinical sections

**WHAT:** Coping mechanisms are the psychological strategies people use to manage stress, anxiety, and emotional pain. They can be adaptive (healthy) or maladaptive (unhealthy). Defense mechanisms are a specific category of unconscious coping.

**WHY IT MATTERS:** Everyone copes — the question is whether they're coping in ways that help or ways that dig the hole deeper. If you can recognize defense mechanisms and spot maladaptive patterns, you can gently steer patients toward strategies that actually work. The NCLEX wants to see that you know the difference and can support without judging.

**MECHANISM:**

**Common Defense Mechanisms:**
- **Denial:** Refusing to accept reality. Example: patient with a heart attack says "It's just heartburn." Often the first response to bad news.
- **Projection:** Attributing your own unacceptable feelings to someone else. Example: "The nurse is anxious about my surgery" (when the patient is the anxious one).
- **Displacement:** Redirecting emotions to a safer target. Example: patient angry at their diagnosis yells at the nurse instead.
- **Rationalization:** Making excuses to justify unacceptable behavior. Example: "I drink because my job is so stressful."
- **Regression:** Reverting to an earlier developmental stage. Example: a 6-year-old who was toilet-trained starts wetting the bed during hospitalization. Expected in children under stress.
- **Sublimation:** Channeling unacceptable feelings into socially acceptable activities. Example: exercising when angry. This is an *adaptive* defense mechanism.
- **Repression:** Unconsciously pushing uncomfortable thoughts or memories out of awareness. Not the same as suppression (which is conscious).
- **Suppression:** Consciously choosing not to think about something right now. Adaptive when temporary.
- **Compensation:** Excelling in one area to offset deficits in another. Example: a student struggling academically becomes an outstanding athlete.

**Adaptive vs. Maladaptive Coping:**
- **Adaptive:** Problem-solving, seeking social support, exercise, humor, relaxation techniques, spiritual practices, journaling, therapy
- **Maladaptive:** Substance use, self-harm, social isolation, aggression, excessive denial, avoidance of necessary treatment

**ACT:**
1. Assess the patient's usual coping strategies — what has worked before?
2. Recognize defense mechanisms without confronting them aggressively — denial in the immediate aftermath of bad news is normal and protective
3. Support adaptive coping: "It sounds like talking to your sister has been really helpful. Would you like to call her?"
4. Gently redirect maladaptive coping: "I notice you've been drinking more since the diagnosis. I'm concerned about your health. Can we talk about other ways to manage what you're feeling?"
5. Teach new coping skills when the patient is receptive (not during crisis)
6. Refer to support services: counseling, support groups, chaplain, social work

**CONNECT:**
→ See Section 4: Therapeutic Communication — how to discuss coping strategies
→ See Section 4: Mental Health Disorders — maladaptive coping and psychiatric conditions
→ See Section 4: Substance Use — substance use as maladaptive coping
→ See Section 4: Crisis Intervention — when coping fails entirely

**TEST TIP:** The NCLEX loves to present a scenario and ask "which defense mechanism is the patient demonstrating?" Know the key defense mechanisms by example. Also: regression in a hospitalized child is expected and doesn't require intervention beyond support. The correct nursing response to a defense mechanism is usually to acknowledge it without challenging it directly — especially in the acute phase.

**PRACTICE:**
A patient recently diagnosed with terminal cancer tells the nurse, "The lab must have mixed up my results. I feel perfectly fine." Which defense mechanism is the patient demonstrating?
A. Projection
B. Rationalization
C. Denial
D. Displacement

**Answer: C.** Denial — the patient is refusing to accept the reality of the diagnosis. This is a common initial response to overwhelming news and can be temporarily protective. The nurse should not confront the denial aggressively at this early stage but should ensure the patient has access to support as they process the diagnosis over time. *Why C works:* Rejection of a confirmed reality by insisting it can't be true is the hallmark of denial.

---

## Cultural & Spiritual Considerations
**Psychosocial Integrity** · **Exam Weight: 9%** · Connects to: Ethical Practice, Client Rights, End-of-Life, all care delivery

**WHAT:** Culturally and spiritually responsive care means recognizing, respecting, and integrating each patient's cultural background, religious beliefs, spiritual practices, and value system into their plan of care — without stereotyping or making assumptions.

**WHY IT MATTERS:** Culture and spirituality influence health beliefs, treatment decisions, communication preferences, dietary needs, end-of-life wishes, and family dynamics. The NCLEX tests whether you can provide inclusive, respectful care — and the 2026 test plan specifically adds content about supporting unbiased treatment regardless of culture, ethnicity, sexual orientation, gender identity, and gender expression.

**MECHANISM:**
- **Cultural humility** over cultural competence: You don't need to know everything about every culture. You need to approach each patient as an individual, ask respectful questions, and avoid assumptions.
- **Key assessment areas:** preferred language, family structure and decision-making patterns, dietary practices, religious/spiritual needs, health beliefs and practices, pain expression, views on death and dying, gender considerations, modesty needs
- **Avoid stereotyping:** Not all members of a group share the same beliefs or practices. Ask the individual patient what matters to them.

**RECOGNIZE:**
- When cultural or spiritual needs are not being met (e.g., dietary restrictions not accommodated, prayer times not respected, spiritual distress)
- When a patient's cultural beliefs conflict with the recommended treatment (e.g., refusal of blood products for religious reasons)
- Communication patterns influenced by culture (eye contact, personal space, directness vs. indirectness, family involvement in decision-making)
- Potential for bias in healthcare delivery — including unconscious bias

**ACT:**
1. Assess cultural and spiritual needs as part of the comprehensive assessment — ask, don't assume
2. Use professional interpreters for patients with limited English proficiency
3. Accommodate dietary restrictions (halal, kosher, vegetarian, fasting practices) within medical parameters
4. Respect religious practices: prayer times, ritual objects, clergy visits, modesty in examination
5. Support diverse family structures in care planning and visitation
6. When a patient's beliefs conflict with medical recommendations: respect autonomy, ensure informed consent, document, involve ethics committee if needed
7. Examine your own biases — self-awareness is the first step in providing unbiased care
8. Provide equitable care regardless of the patient's culture, ethnicity, sexual orientation, gender identity, or gender expression

**CONNECT:**
→ See Section 1 (Management of Care): Ethical Practice — autonomy and justice
→ See Section 1 (Management of Care): Client Rights — right to culturally responsive care
→ See Section 4: End-of-Life Care — death rituals and cultural practices
→ See Section 4: Therapeutic Communication — culturally sensitive communication

**TEST TIP:** The NCLEX will NOT test you on specific cultural facts ("People from X culture believe Y"). Instead, it tests the *process* of providing culturally sensitive care: ask the patient, don't assume, accommodate when possible, use interpreters, respect autonomy. If a question presents a conflict between a cultural practice and a medical recommendation, the correct answer respects the patient's right to choose while ensuring they're fully informed. The 2026 exam will likely include scenarios involving diverse patients (LGBTQ+, gender-diverse individuals, various religious backgrounds) — the correct answer always involves respectful, unbiased, patient-centered care.

**PRACTICE:**
A patient scheduled for surgery states that their religious beliefs prohibit blood transfusions. The nurse should:
A. Try to convince the patient that the transfusion may be necessary to save their life
B. Respect the patient's decision, ensure informed consent documentation reflects it, and notify the surgical team
C. Contact the hospital chaplain to discuss the religious belief with the patient
D. Document the refusal and proceed with surgical preparation without further discussion

**Answer: B.** Autonomy governs: the patient has the right to refuse blood products based on religious beliefs. The nurse's role is to ensure the decision is informed, document it, and communicate it to the care team so alternative plans can be made. Option A overrides autonomy. Option C assumes the patient hasn't thought this through. Option D doesn't ensure proper communication to the team. *Why B works:* Respect the decision, ensure it's documented, and make sure the entire team knows so they can plan accordingly.

---

## Community Support Services
**Psychosocial Integrity** · **Exam Weight: 9%** · Connects to: Interprofessional Collaboration, Discharge Planning, all psychosocial topics

**WHAT:** Community support services include the resources available outside the hospital to support patients' ongoing health, mental health, social, and practical needs — support groups, home health services, rehabilitation programs, financial assistance, housing resources, crisis hotlines, and community health organizations.

**WHY IT MATTERS:** Discharge is not the end of care — it's a transition. Connecting patients to appropriate community resources affects long-term outcomes, prevents readmissions, and supports recovery.

**ACT:**
1. Assess the patient's post-discharge needs early — don't wait until the day of discharge
2. Identify appropriate community resources based on the patient's specific needs
3. Provide referrals to: home health nursing, social services, support groups, mental health services, substance use treatment programs, financial assistance, meal delivery, transportation, housing resources
4. Educate patients and families about available resources and how to access them
5. Coordinate with social work and case management for complex discharge planning
6. Follow up to ensure connections to community services were successful

**CONNECT:**
→ See Section 1 (Management of Care): Interprofessional Collaboration — social work, case management referrals
→ See Section 4: Substance Use Disorders — treatment programs and support groups
→ See Section 4: Abuse & Neglect — shelters, protective services
→ See Section 4: Mental Health Disorders — community mental health services

**TEST TIP:** When the NCLEX asks which community resource fits, it's a matching game — don't overthink it. Substance use → treatment programs and support groups. Elderly patient living alone with functional limitations → home health services. Can't afford medications → social work or case management. Domestic violence → shelter and hotline information. Match the need to the service, move on.

**PRACTICE:**
A nurse is discharging a patient with a new diagnosis of type 2 diabetes who lives alone and has limited financial resources. Which referral is most important?
A. A personal trainer for exercise guidance
B. A social worker to assess for medication assistance programs and community resources
C. A dietitian for meal planning education
D. A diabetes support group in the community

**Answer: B.** The patient's most pressing barrier is financial — they need to be able to afford their medications and supplies. A social worker can connect them with assistance programs, community resources, and coordinate comprehensive support. While a dietitian (C) and support group (D) are valuable, they don't address the financial barrier that could prevent the patient from managing their condition at all. *Why B works:* Addressing financial barriers to care is the highest priority — the best education means nothing if the patient can't afford their insulin.

---

---

# SECTION 5: BASIC CARE AND COMFORT

Don't sleep on this section — "basic" doesn't mean "easy." This is where the rubber meets the road: nutrition, elimination, mobility, pain management, rest. These are the things patients actually *feel* every hour of every day, and the NCLEX knows that getting them wrong cascades into everything else.

**Exam Weight: 6–12% (midpoint 9%)** · The foundation of hands-on nursing. These questions test whether you can keep patients fed, mobile, clean, comfortable, and dignified. Sounds simple — until NCLEX asks you to pick between two "correct" interventions and rank by priority.

---

## NUTRITION AND THERAPEUTIC DIETS
**Basic Care and Comfort** · **Exam Weight: 9%** · Connects to: → See Section 8: Renal Conditions, Endocrine Disorders, Burns

**WHAT:** Nutrition management means assessing dietary needs, identifying deficiencies, and implementing therapeutic diets tailored to disease states. It includes enteral and parenteral feeding when the GI tract is compromised.

**WHY IT MATTERS:** Malnutrition slows healing, worsens infection risk, and complicates every other condition. NCLEX tests your ability to match the right diet to the right disease — and to catch when a patient's tray has something they shouldn't eat.

**MECHANISM:** Macronutrients (carbs, proteins, fats) fuel metabolism. Micronutrients (vitamins, minerals) enable enzymatic processes. Disease states alter requirements — renal failure means the kidneys can't clear potassium or phosphorus, so you restrict them. Burns massively increase metabolic demand, so you increase everything. The principle: adjust intake to match what the body can process and what it needs to heal.

**RECOGNIZE:**
- Protein deficiency: muscle wasting, poor wound healing, low albumin (<3.5 g/dL), edema (albumin holds fluid in vessels — low albumin = fluid leaks out)
- Vitamin deficiencies: B12/folate → megaloblastic anemia; C → poor wound healing, bleeding gums; D → bone loss; K → bleeding
- Dehydration: poor skin turgor, dry mucous membranes, concentrated urine, elevated BUN
- Overhydration: edema, weight gain, crackles, dilute lab values

**ACT — Therapeutic Diets by Condition:**

| Condition | Diet | Why? |
|-----------|------|------|
| Renal disease (pre-dialysis) | ↓Protein, ↓Na, ↓K, ↓phosphorus, fluid restriction | Kidneys can't excrete waste products of protein metabolism or excess electrolytes |
| Renal disease (on dialysis) | ↑Protein (dialysis removes it), still restrict Na/K/phos | Dialysis clears waste but also strips protein |
| Heart failure / HTN | ↓Na, ↓fat, fluid restriction | Sodium holds water → worsens fluid overload and BP |
| COPD | ↑Calorie, ↑fat, ↓carb, small frequent meals | Carb metabolism produces more CO₂ than fat metabolism — patients already retain CO₂ |
| Celiac disease | Strict gluten-free — avoid wheat, barley, rye (remember: avoid BROW — Barley, Rye, Oats, Wheat) | Gluten triggers autoimmune destruction of intestinal villi |
| Addison's disease | ↑Na, ↓K | Low aldosterone = sodium wasting and potassium retention |
| Cushing's syndrome | ↓Na, ↑K, ↑calcium | Excess aldosterone effect = sodium retention and potassium loss |
| Gout | ↓Purine foods (organ meats, shellfish, beer), ↑fluids | Purines metabolize to uric acid → crystal deposition in joints |
| Burns | ↑Protein, ↑calorie, ↑vitamin C | Massive tissue destruction demands protein for repair; vitamin C supports collagen synthesis |
| Dumping syndrome | ↓Simple carbs, ↓fiber, no fluids with meals, small frequent meals, lie down 30 min after eating | Hyperosmolar bolus enters jejunum → rapid fluid shift → cramping, diarrhea, vasomotor symptoms |
| Cirrhosis | ↓Na, ↓protein (if encephalopathy), no alcohol | Impaired liver can't metabolize ammonia from protein; sodium worsens ascites |
| Pancreatitis | NPO in acute phase → low-fat when resumed | Fat stimulates pancreatic enzyme release → worsens inflammation |
| Peptic ulcer | Avoid caffeine, alcohol, spicy foods; small frequent meals | These stimulate acid production and irritate the mucosa |
| Phenylketonuria (PKU) | ↓Phenylalanine (avoid aspartame, meats, dairy) | Missing enzyme can't convert phenylalanine → toxic accumulation → brain damage |
| Iron-deficiency anemia | ↑Iron-rich foods (red meat, spinach, beans); take with vitamin C; avoid dairy/antacids with iron supplements | Vitamin C enhances iron absorption; calcium blocks it |

**MEDS:**
- Multivitamins/supplements: Purpose — correct deficiencies · *Why?* Disease states and poor intake create gaps that food alone may not fill
- Iron supplements (ferrous sulfate): Purpose — treat iron-deficiency anemia · *Why?* Give on empty stomach with vitamin C for absorption; causes black/tarry stools (expected, not bleeding); constipation common
- Potassium supplements: Purpose — replace losses from diuretics · *Why?* Never give IV push (cardiac arrest risk); dilute and infuse slowly; burns going in — that's the potassium, not an infiltration (though check the site too)

**CONNECT:** → See Section 8: Electrolyte Imbalances (dietary management is the first intervention) · → See Section 6: Parenteral Nutrition · → See Section 8: Burns, Renal Conditions

**TEST TIP:** NCLEX loves diet questions disguised as "which food should the nurse remove from the tray?" Know your restricted foods cold. For renal patients, think about what those foods contain — bananas and oranges are high-potassium, not just "bad for kidneys." Also watch for questions about enteral feeding: verify tube placement before EVERY feeding (auscultation of air bolus or pH of aspirate <5), elevate HOB 30-45° during and 30 min after to prevent aspiration.

**PRACTICE:**
A patient with chronic kidney disease (pre-dialysis) selects the following menu: grilled chicken breast, baked potato with butter, orange juice, and chocolate cake. Which item should the nurse discuss with the patient?

A. Grilled chicken breast
B. Baked potato with butter
C. Orange juice
D. Chocolate cake

**Answer: C.** Orange juice is high in potassium, which must be restricted in CKD because the kidneys cannot adequately excrete it. The baked potato is also high in potassium (B is also concerning — on a real NCLEX, you might see a "select all" format). The chicken provides needed protein in moderate amounts. Chocolate cake contains phosphorus (also restricted), but the orange juice is the highest potassium offender on this tray.

---

## ELIMINATION
**Basic Care and Comfort** · **Exam Weight: 9%** · Connects to: → See Section 7: Lab Values · → See Section 8: Renal Conditions, GI Conditions

**WHAT:** Managing urinary and bowel elimination — assessing patterns, identifying dysfunction, implementing interventions, and managing devices (catheters, ostomies).

**WHY IT MATTERS:** What comes out of a patient tells you what's going wrong inside. Urine output drops? Think renal failure, shock, or obstruction. Stool changes character? You're looking at possible GI bleeding, infection, or malabsorption. The NCLEX expects you to connect the output to the underlying problem — because that connection is the difference between charting a number and catching a crisis.

**MECHANISM:**
- Urine: kidneys filter ~180 L/day, reabsorb most, produce 1-2 L urine. Normal output: 30 mL/hr minimum (0.5 mL/kg/hr). Below this = oliguria = kidneys struggling
- Stool: formed in the colon through water reabsorption. Normal: brown, formed, daily or every 2-3 days depending on patient's baseline

**RECOGNIZE:**
- Urinary retention: distended bladder, discomfort, dribbling overflow, bladder scan >300 mL
- UTI: frequency, urgency, dysuria, cloudy/foul-smelling urine, confusion in elderly (often the ONLY sign)
- GI bleeding: upper = coffee-ground emesis or melena (black tarry stool — blood digested by stomach acid); lower = bright red blood per rectum (hematochezia)
- Obstruction (bowel): absent bowel sounds, distention, vomiting (may be feculent), no flatus
- Diarrhea: assess for dehydration, electrolyte loss (especially potassium), skin breakdown

**ACT:**
1. Urinary catheter care: maintain closed system, keep bag below bladder level, secure tubing to prevent traction, perform perineal hygiene, do NOT irrigate unless ordered. Remove as soon as clinically able — every extra day increases infection risk
2. Bowel management: assess patterns first; increase fluids/fiber/activity for constipation before medications; digital rectal exam for impaction; monitor stool character post-GI surgery
3. Ostomy care: assess stoma (should be pink/red and moist — dusky/pale = report); measure stoma for proper appliance fit; empty pouch when 1/3 to 1/2 full; protect peristomal skin
4. Bladder training: scheduled voiding attempts, gradually increasing intervals, pelvic floor exercises (Kegels)

**MEDS:**
- Docusate (Colace): Purpose — stool softener · *Why?* Prevents straining; doesn't stimulate peristalsis; safe for cardiac patients who must avoid Valsalva
- Bisacodyl (Dulcolax): Purpose — stimulant laxative · *Why?* Stimulates peristalsis directly; use short-term; don't crush enteric-coated tablets
- Loperamide (Imodium): Purpose — antidiarrheal · *Why?* Slows GI motility; do NOT use in infectious diarrhea (you want pathogens OUT)
- Phenazopyridine (Pyridium): Purpose — urinary analgesic · *Why?* Turns urine orange/red — warn the patient so they don't panic; it treats the pain, not the infection

**CONNECT:** → See Section 8: Renal Conditions · → See Section 7: Diagnostic Testing (UA, stool cultures) · → See Section 8: GI Conditions

**TEST TIP:** "Which finding should the nurse report immediately?" — you'll see this phrasing constantly. For catheters, the red flag is decreased or absent output. For ostomies, it's a dusky or cyanotic stoma — that's ischemia, and it's urgent. Bowel obstruction? Priority is NPO plus NG tube decompression. And the post-op rule that always shows up: bowel sounds AND flatus before you advance that diet. Not one or the other — both.

**PRACTICE:**
A nurse is caring for a client 2 days post-colostomy creation. During assessment, the nurse notes the stoma is dark purple. What is the priority nursing action?

A. Document the finding as expected
B. Apply a warm compress to the stoma
C. Notify the healthcare provider immediately
D. Reposition the ostomy appliance

**Answer: C.** A dark purple (dusky/cyanotic) stoma indicates compromised blood supply and possible necrosis. This is an emergency requiring immediate provider notification and likely surgical intervention. A healthy stoma is pink to red and moist. This is not expected and warm compresses won't fix a vascular problem.

---

## PAIN MANAGEMENT
**Basic Care and Comfort** · **Exam Weight: 9%** · Connects to: → See Section 6: Pain Pharmacology · → See Section 4: Psychosocial Integrity

**WHAT:** Comprehensive pain assessment and management using both pharmacological and non-pharmacological interventions. Pain is what the patient says it is — the patient's self-report is the most reliable indicator of pain.

**WHY IT MATTERS:** Pain isn't just uncomfortable — it's actively harmful. Uncontrolled pain slows healing, drives up oxygen demand, keeps patients immobile (hello, DVT and pneumonia), and violates their rights. The NCLEX will push you to prioritize pain management while navigating the real tensions — respiratory depression, fall risk, and the bias that tells you a patient is "drug-seeking" when they're actually just hurting.

**MECHANISM:** Pain pathways: nociception (tissue damage activates nerve endings) → transmission (signals travel via A-delta and C fibers to spinal cord) → perception (brain interprets signal) → modulation (descending pathways can amplify or dampen signal). This matters because interventions target different points — NSAIDs block nociception, opioids modulate perception, distraction/relaxation target the brain's processing.

**RECOGNIZE:**
- Acute pain: sudden onset, identifiable cause, vital sign changes (↑HR, ↑BP, ↑RR, diaphoresis), guarding, grimacing
- Chronic pain: >3-6 months, vital signs may be NORMAL (the body adapts), behavioral cues more important
- Neuropathic pain: burning, tingling, "electric shock" quality — responds poorly to standard analgesics, may need gabapentin/pregabalin
- Cultural considerations: pain expression varies; some cultures are stoic, others are expressive — neither means more or less pain. Assess, don't assume

**ACT:**
1. Assess pain using standardized scale (numeric 0-10, FACES for children/cognitively impaired, FLACC for nonverbal/infants)
2. Believe the patient's report — it is the gold standard
3. Medicate as ordered — don't wait for pain to become severe (easier to prevent escalation than to chase pain)
4. Reassess after intervention (30 min for IV, 60 min for PO)
5. Non-pharmacological adjuncts (use WITH medication, not instead of):
   - Positioning and repositioning
   - Ice/heat therapy (ice for acute inflammation, heat for muscle spasm/chronic pain)
   - Distraction (guided imagery, music, conversation)
   - Relaxation techniques (deep breathing, progressive muscle relaxation)
   - TENS unit (transcutaneous electrical nerve stimulation)
   - Massage (avoid over fractures, clots, or open wounds)
   - Splinting incision before coughing

**MEDS:** → See Section 6: Pain Pharmacology for comprehensive drug coverage

**CONNECT:** → See Section 6: Pain Pharmacology (opioids, NSAIDs, adjuvants) · → See Section 4: Psychosocial (substance use, pain and addiction) · → See Section 1: Patient Rights

**TEST TIP:** NCLEX will NEVER punish you for treating pain — internalize that now. The answer is almost never "hold the pain med and reassess." Here's where they get you: (1) Picking assessment over medication when the patient's already been assessed and has an order — just give the med. (2) Confusing physiological dependence (totally expected with chronic opioid use) with addiction — they're not the same thing. (3) Withholding opioids from a dying patient because RR is 10 — comfort is the priority at end of life, full stop. (4) NGN format: you may need to drag pain interventions into priority order, so know your sequencing.

**PRACTICE:**
A postoperative client rates pain as 8/10. Morphine 2 mg IV is ordered PRN every 4 hours. The client received the last dose 5 hours ago. Respirations are 14/min. What should the nurse do first?

A. Offer a non-pharmacological intervention first
B. Administer morphine 2 mg IV as ordered
C. Contact the provider for a higher dose
D. Reassess pain in 30 minutes

**Answer: B.** The patient has pain (8/10), a valid order, adequate time since last dose (5 hours > 4 hour interval), and safe respiratory status (14/min is well above the hold threshold of typically 10-12). Administer the medication. Non-pharmacological measures are adjuncts, not replacements. Waiting 30 minutes with an 8/10 pain level is unnecessary delay.

---

## MOBILITY AND POSITIONING
**Basic Care and Comfort** · **Exam Weight: 9%** · Connects to: → See Section 8: Neurological Conditions, Cardiac Conditions, Respiratory Conditions

**WHAT:** Promoting safe movement, proper body mechanics, and therapeutic positioning based on the patient's condition. Positioning is one of the most heavily tested topics on NCLEX because the wrong position can kill a patient.

**WHY IT MATTERS:** Correct positioning prevents aspiration, reduces intracranial pressure, optimizes breathing, prevents skin breakdown, and maintains surgical repair integrity. NCLEX expects you to know the right position for 40+ conditions — and more importantly, WHY.

**MECHANISM:** Gravity is the operating principle. Elevating the head uses gravity to reduce venous return to the brain (lowering ICP) and move abdominal contents away from the diaphragm (improving ventilation). Lateral positioning uses gravity to drain secretions and prevent aspiration. Every position decision comes back to: where do we want blood/air/fluid to go or not go?

**ACT — Complete Positioning Reference:**

**Head and Neurological Conditions:**

| Condition | Position | Why? |
|-----------|----------|------|
| Hemorrhagic stroke | HOB elevated 30° | Promotes venous drainage from brain → reduces ICP |
| Ischemic stroke | HOB flat or low | Maximizes cerebral perfusion to ischemic tissue |
| Increased ICP | HOB 30°, head midline, avoid hip flexion | Promotes venous drainage; midline prevents jugular compression; hip flexion increases intra-abdominal → intracranial pressure |
| Post-craniotomy (supratentorial) | HOB 30°, do NOT position on operative side | Prevents pressure on surgical site; promotes venous drainage |
| Post-craniotomy (infratentorial/posterior fossa) | Flat, on either side but NOT on the back | Prevents pressure on brainstem |
| Seizures (active) | Side-lying (lateral), lower HOB | Maintains airway, allows secretion drainage; do NOT restrain or force anything into mouth |
| Post-lumbar puncture | Flat/supine for 4-8 hours | Reduces CSF leak from puncture site → prevents spinal headache |
| Meningitis | Side-lying, dim lights, quiet room | Comfort position; reduces stimulation that worsens headache |
| Autonomic dysreflexia | Sit UP immediately (elevate HOB first!) | Lowers blood pressure — this is a hypertensive emergency triggered by noxious stimulus below the spinal cord injury |

**Respiratory Conditions:**

| Condition | Position | Why? |
|-----------|----------|------|
| Asthma / acute dyspnea | Orthopneic (sitting upright, leaning forward on overbed table) | Maximizes lung expansion; accessory muscles work best in this position |
| COPD (chronic) | High Fowler's or tripod | Same principle — maximizes diaphragmatic excursion |
| Pneumothorax (pre-chest tube) | Fowler's or high Fowler's | Air rises — keeps free air in upper chest away from functioning lung tissue |
| Pneumonectomy | Operative side or back only, NOT good lung down | Fluid collects on operative side; positioning good lung down would impair the only functioning lung |
| Lobectomy | Good lung down (unaffected side dependent) | Promotes perfusion to the healthy lung (gravity pulls blood to dependent lung, matching ventilation) |
| Pulmonary edema | High Fowler's, legs dangling | Reduces venous return (preload) to the heart; maximizes lung expansion |
| Pleural effusion | Affected side down for comfort; sit upright for thoracentesis | Gravity keeps fluid on the affected side; upright positioning for the procedure allows access and drainage |
| Unilateral lung disease (general rule) | Good lung down | Maximizes gas exchange — gravity increases perfusion to the dependent (good) lung |
| Post-intubation/ventilator | HOB 30-45° | Reduces VAP (ventilator-associated pneumonia) risk |

**Cardiac and Vascular Conditions:**

| Condition | Position | Why? |
|-----------|----------|------|
| Heart failure / pulmonary edema | High Fowler's | Reduces preload; eases breathing |
| Shock (hypovolemic, neurogenic, septic) | Modified Trendelenburg (legs elevated 20°, trunk flat) | Promotes venous return to the heart without compromising breathing |
| Cardiac catheterization (femoral approach) | Post-procedure: supine, affected leg straight for 4-6 hours | Prevents bleeding from arterial puncture site; flexion increases bleed risk |
| Air embolism | Left lateral, head down (Trendelenburg) | Traps air in the right atrium (air rises to the highest point); prevents air from entering pulmonary vasculature |
| DVT / PE suspected | Bedrest, affected leg elevated | Prevents clot migration; elevation reduces edema |

**Gastrointestinal Conditions:**

| Condition | Position | Why? |
|-----------|----------|------|
| GERD / hiatal hernia | HOB elevated 30-45°; right side-lying after meals | Gravity keeps gastric contents in the stomach; right side promotes gastric emptying |
| Esophageal varices (bleeding) | Left side-lying | Prevents aspiration of blood |
| Liver biopsy (post-procedure) | Right side-lying, with pillow under puncture site | Compresses biopsy site against chest wall → tamponades bleeding |
| Paracentesis (during) | Upright, sitting at edge of bed | Gravity pools ascitic fluid for easier drainage |
| NG tube in place (feeding) | HOB 30-45° during and 30 min after feeding | Prevents aspiration |
| Bowel obstruction | NPO + position of comfort, usually semi-Fowler's | Promotes comfort; facilitates NG tube drainage |
| Appendectomy (post-op, ruptured) | Right side-lying or semi-Fowler's | Promotes drainage of peritoneal cavity if drain present; comfort |

**Obstetric and Pediatric Conditions:**

| Condition | Position | Why? |
|-----------|----------|------|
| Prolapsed umbilical cord | Knee-chest or Trendelenburg | Gravity lifts presenting part off the cord, restoring fetal blood flow |
| Placenta previa (active bleeding) | Left lateral | Reduces uterine pressure on vena cava; maximizes uteroplacental perfusion |
| Pregnant (general, >20 weeks) | Left lateral | Prevents vena cava compression by uterus → maintains cardiac output and fetal perfusion |
| Spina bifida (pre-repair) | Prone | Prevents pressure on the exposed neural sac |
| Cleft lip repair (post-op) | Supine or side-lying (NOT prone) | Prevents pressure on the surgical repair |
| Cleft palate repair (post-op) | Prone or side-lying | Promotes drainage of secretions; prevents aspiration |
| Pyloric stenosis (pre-op) | Upright or slightly right side-lying after feeding | Gravity assists gastric emptying through narrowed pylorus |
| Tonsillectomy (post-op) | Side-lying or prone | Promotes drainage of blood/secretions; prevents aspiration |

**Surgical and Orthopedic Conditions:**

| Condition | Position | Why? |
|-----------|----------|------|
| Post-thyroidectomy | Semi-Fowler's, support head/neck with sandbags | Reduces edema at surgical site; prevents flexion/hyperextension of neck that stresses suture line |
| Post-laryngectomy | Semi-Fowler's or Fowler's | Reduces edema; promotes drainage |
| Post-total hip replacement | Affected leg abducted (use abduction pillow), avoid >90° flexion, avoid internal rotation, avoid crossing legs | Prevents prosthesis dislocation — the ball can pop out of the new socket |
| Amputation (below knee, first 24h) | Elevate residual limb on pillow | Reduces edema |
| Amputation (BKA, after 24h) | Flat (no pillow under residual limb) | Prevents flexion contracture of the knee |
| Amputation (above knee) | Prone position periodically | Prevents hip flexion contracture |
| Cast (fresh) | Elevate above heart level on pillows | Reduces edema; do NOT rest on hard surface while drying (dents the cast) |
| Traction (skeletal/skin) | Maintain body alignment, weights hang freely, do NOT remove | Weights provide countertraction; removing disrupts fracture alignment |
| Compartment syndrome | Affected extremity at heart level — do NOT elevate | Elevation reduces arterial flow to already compromised tissue; need perfusion, not drainage |
| Burns (acute) | Elevate burned extremities; positioning depends on burn location to prevent contractures | Reduce edema; prevent scar contracture (e.g., neck burns — no pillow, hyperextend) |
| Eye surgery (post-op) | Operative side UP (do not lie on operative side) | Prevents pressure on the surgical eye |
| Detached retina (pre-op) | Position so the detachment is dependent (falls back against the wall of the eye) | Gravity helps reattach the retina |
| Ear surgery (post-op) | Operative side UP (do not lie on operative side) | Promotes drainage away from surgical site |

**Miscellaneous Critical Positions:**

| Condition | Position | Why? |
|-----------|----------|------|
| Tube feeding/PEG | HOB 30-45° during and 30 min after | Prevents aspiration |
| Peripheral IV insertion | Extremity level with heart initially | Facilitates venous filling for access |
| Central line insertion (subclavian) | Trendelenburg | Distends neck veins for easier access; prevents air embolism |
| Epidural/spinal anesthesia | Monitor for hypotension; avoid sitting upright too quickly | Sympathetic blockade → vasodilation → hypotension |
| Log rolling | Post-spinal surgery, spinal cord injury | Maintains spinal alignment; prevents rotation |

**CONNECT:** → See Section 8: Stroke, ICP, Cardiac, Respiratory conditions for deeper pathophysiology

**TEST TIP:** Position questions are NCLEX gold. The test loves "what position should the nurse place the client in?" Here is the cheat: think about what gravity does in each position. If you can reason through whether you want fluid/air/pressure to go UP or DOWN, you can derive most positioning answers. Two positions students always confuse: hemorrhagic stroke (UP — reduce ICP) vs. ischemic stroke (FLAT — maximize perfusion). Also: lobectomy (good lung DOWN) vs. pneumonectomy (operative side DOWN or back).

**PRACTICE:**
A nurse finds a client cyanotic and gasping after a central venous catheter was accidentally disconnected. What is the priority position?

A. High Fowler's
B. Left lateral with head lowered
C. Supine with legs elevated
D. Right lateral with head elevated

**Answer: B.** This presentation suggests an air embolism (air entered the open catheter). Left lateral, head-down (Durant's maneuver) traps the air in the right atrium, preventing it from entering the pulmonary vasculature. This is life-threatening — position first, then clamp the catheter and call for help.

---

## REST, SLEEP, AND PERSONAL HYGIENE
**Basic Care and Comfort** · **Exam Weight: 9%** · Connects to: → See Section 4: Psychosocial Integrity

**WHAT:** Promoting adequate rest and sleep, performing or assisting with personal hygiene, maintaining skin integrity, and preserving patient dignity during care.

**WHY IT MATTERS:** Sleep deprivation impairs immune function and wound healing, increases pain perception, and contributes to ICU delirium. Skin breakdown from poor hygiene or immobility is a nurse-sensitive outcome — meaning it reflects nursing care quality. Maintaining dignity during intimate care is both an ethical obligation and a 2026 test plan emphasis (NEW activity statement).

**RECOGNIZE:**
- Sleep deprivation: irritability, confusion, impaired cognition, vital sign instability
- Pressure injury risk factors: immobility, incontinence, malnutrition (low albumin), altered sensation, shearing forces
- Braden Scale: scores ≤18 = at risk; ≤12 = high risk. Assesses sensory perception, moisture, activity, mobility, nutrition, friction/shear

**ACT:**
1. Sleep promotion: cluster care activities, reduce nighttime noise/lights, maintain daytime routines, avoid caffeine after noon, address pain before bedtime
2. Skin care: reposition every 2 hours (minimum), use pressure-relieving surfaces, keep skin clean and dry, moisturize, protect bony prominences, manage incontinence promptly
3. Pressure injury staging: Stage 1 (non-blanchable redness, intact skin) → Stage 2 (partial thickness, blister/shallow crater) → Stage 3 (full thickness into subcutaneous, no bone/tendon visible) → Stage 4 (full thickness with exposed bone/tendon/muscle) → Unstageable (covered by slough/eschar — can't see the base)
4. Dignity during care (NEW 2026): close doors/curtains, drape properly, explain procedures, maintain communication, give choices when possible, be matter-of-fact but kind during intimate care

**CONNECT:** → See Section 1: Safe Care (fall prevention during toileting/bathing) · → See Section 4: Psychosocial (body image, dignity)

**TEST TIP:** The 2026 test plan added "maintain client dignity and privacy during care" as a distinct activity statement. Expect questions about draping, communication during care, and respecting patient preferences. For pressure injuries, know your staging — NCLEX loves "which stage is this?" with a description. Remember: you cannot REVERSE stage a pressure injury (a healing Stage 4 is still Stage 4, not Stage 3).

**PRACTICE:**
A nurse assesses a client's sacral area and notes a 2 cm area of non-blanchable redness with intact skin. What stage pressure injury is this?

A. Stage 1
B. Stage 2
C. Stage 3
D. Unstageable

**Answer: A.** Non-blanchable redness with intact skin is the hallmark of Stage 1 pressure injury. The skin hasn't broken yet, but the damage has begun. Interventions: reposition immediately, relieve pressure, assess contributing factors (nutrition, moisture, mobility).

---

## TUBES, DRAINS, AND CATHETERS
**Basic Care and Comfort** · **Exam Weight: 9%** · Connects to: → See Section 7: Monitoring Complications · → See Section 8: Respiratory, GI Conditions

**WHAT:** Managing the collection of devices that drain, decompress, feed, or monitor patients. Includes nasogastric tubes, chest tubes, urinary catheters, wound drains (JP, Hemovac, Penrose), T-tubes, and feeding tubes.

**WHY IT MATTERS:** Mismanagement kills. An unrecognized chest tube disconnection causes a tension pneumothorax. A blocked NG tube post-GI surgery leads to aspiration. Clamping a T-tube too early causes bile backup and organ damage. NCLEX wants to know you can manage these safely.

**RECOGNIZE and ACT — Device-Specific Management:**

**Nasogastric (NG) Tube:**
- Purposes: decompression (Salem sump — double lumen) or feeding (Dobhoff — smaller, weighted tip, placed in duodenum)
- Verify placement before each use: aspirate contents (pH <5 = gastric), X-ray is gold standard for initial placement
- Suction: low intermittent suction for Salem sump to protect mucosa; keep blue pigtail (air vent) open and above stomach level
- Irrigation: use normal saline, not water (water causes electrolyte imbalance)
- Monitor for: electrolyte imbalances (losing H+, K+, Na+, Cl- in gastric secretions → metabolic alkalosis)

**Feeding Tubes (PEG, G-tube, J-tube):**
- Verify placement before each feeding and with each shift
