- Check residual volumes as ordered (hold feeding if >250-500 mL per facility policy — indicates delayed gastric emptying)
- Flush with 30 mL water before and after each feeding/medication
- HOB 30-45° during and 30 min after feeding
- Medications: use liquid forms when available; crush only non-enteric-coated, non-extended-release medications; flush between each medication

**Chest Tubes:** → See Section 7: Chest Tubes for comprehensive coverage

**Urinary Catheters:**
- Maintain closed sterile system
- Secure tubing to prevent traction on urethra
- Keep bag below bladder level at all times (even during transport — pin to gown below bladder)
- Empty when 2/3 full
- Perineal care at least daily and after bowel movement
- Monitor I&O — report output <30 mL/hr
- Remove as soon as clinically indicated (CAUTI prevention)

**Wound Drains (Jackson-Pratt, Hemovac):**
- Maintain suction: JP — compress bulb; Hemovac — compress flat and close port
- Monitor character and amount of drainage
- Empty when half full (suction decreases as reservoir fills)
- Record output as part of I&O
- Document color, consistency, odor of drainage

**T-Tube (biliary drainage):**
- Placed after common bile duct surgery to maintain bile flow
- Keep drainage bag below level of gallbladder
- Expected output: 500 mL/day initially, decreasing over days
- Bile is golden brown — report green or blood-tinged drainage
- Clamp only as ordered (usually before meals to aid digestion; observe for distention, nausea, pain)
- Protect surrounding skin — bile is highly irritating

**CONNECT:** → See Section 7: Chest Tubes, Monitoring Complications · → See Section 5: Nutrition (enteral feeding) · → See Section 5: Elimination (catheter care)

**TEST TIP:** The game here is EXPECTED vs. REPORT — learn the pairs. Gentle bubbling in the suction control chamber of a chest tube? Expected. Continuous bubbling in the water seal chamber? Air leak — report it. Blood-tinged drainage from a fresh surgical drain? Expected. Bright red drainage that suddenly spikes? Report. When the question asks "which finding requires immediate intervention?" — they're testing whether you know the difference.

**PRACTICE:**
A nurse is caring for a client with a nasogastric tube connected to low intermittent suction. The client's latest lab results show pH 7.50, CO₂ 40 mmHg, and HCO₃ 30 mEq/L. What is the most likely cause?

A. Respiratory acidosis from hypoventilation
B. Metabolic alkalosis from loss of gastric acid
C. Respiratory alkalosis from hyperventilation
D. Metabolic acidosis from loss of bicarbonate

**Answer: B.** The NG tube is suctioning out gastric acid (HCl). Losing hydrogen ions causes the blood to become more alkaline. pH 7.50 = alkalotic. CO₂ is normal (not respiratory). HCO₃ 30 = elevated (metabolic). This is uncompensated metabolic alkalosis from NG suction losses. → See Section 7: ABG Analysis

---

# SECTION 6: PHARMACOLOGICAL AND PARENTERAL THERAPIES

This is the section students dread — and the one where pattern recognition saves you. You don't need to memorize every drug ever manufactured. You need systems: suffixes that decode drug classes, rights that prevent errors, and math that you can do under pressure. Build those systems here and pharm questions become manageable instead of terrifying.

**Exam Weight: 13–19% (midpoint 16%)** · This is the single highest-weighted subcategory in your four sections. NCLEX will test drug safety, calculations, administration, side effects, and your ability to catch errors before they reach the patient. Every medication question is a patient safety question.

---

## MEDICATION SAFETY — THE SIX RIGHTS
**Pharmacological Therapies** · **Exam Weight: 16%** · Connects to: → See Section 1: Safe Care

**WHAT:** The foundational framework for preventing medication errors: Right patient, Right drug, Right dose, Right route, Right time, Right documentation.

**WHY IT MATTERS:** Medication errors are a leading cause of preventable harm. NCLEX tests this constantly — not just as direct questions but embedded in clinical scenarios where you must catch the error.

**MECHANISM:** The six rights function as a checklist at the point of administration. But safety starts earlier: verify the order, check allergies, review interactions, assess the patient's current status (is this dose appropriate RIGHT NOW for THIS patient?), and educate.

**RECOGNIZE — Red Flags That Should Stop You:**
- Patient states "that's not what I usually take" or "that pill looks different" — STOP and verify
- Drug-allergy connection (e.g., patient allergic to PCN → question cephalosporin order due to cross-sensitivity)
- Dose doesn't make clinical sense (metoprolol 500 mg when standard is 25-200 mg)
- Lab value makes drug unsafe (K+ 5.8 → hold potassium supplement; INR 4.5 → hold warfarin)
- Vital sign makes drug unsafe (HR 54 → hold digoxin; BP 88/50 → hold antihypertensive)

**ACT:**
1. Always use two patient identifiers (name + DOB; NOT room number)
2. Check allergies before every first dose and periodically thereafter
3. Independent double-check for high-alert medications (insulin, heparin, chemotherapy, opioids, potassium IV)
4. Bar-code scanning when available — technology augments but doesn't replace clinical judgment
5. Educate the patient: what the drug is, why they're taking it, common side effects, when to report problems
6. Document administration immediately — if you didn't document it, it didn't happen

**CONNECT:** → See Section 1: Safe Care (error reporting, just culture)

**TEST TIP:** NCLEX will often describe a scenario where something is slightly wrong — the dose is borderline, the lab value is on the edge, the timing is off. Your job is to catch it. When in doubt: hold and verify. The safest answer on NCLEX is almost always "clarify with the provider" when something doesn't add up. A nurse who questions an order is competent. A nurse who blindly administers is dangerous.

**PRACTICE:**
A nurse prepares to administer digoxin 0.25 mg PO to a client. The apical pulse is 56 bpm. What should the nurse do?

A. Administer the medication as ordered
B. Hold the medication and notify the provider
C. Administer the medication and recheck the pulse in 1 hour
D. Cut the dose in half and administer 0.125 mg

**Answer: B.** Digoxin slows heart rate. The standard hold parameter is HR <60 bpm (apical, for one full minute). At 56 bpm, you hold the drug and notify the provider. You never independently modify a dose — that requires a new order.

---

## DRUG CALCULATIONS
**Pharmacological Therapies** · **Exam Weight: 16%** · Connects to: → See Section 1: Safe Care

**WHAT:** Mathematical calculations for safe medication dosing, including oral doses, injectable doses, IV flow rates, weight-based dosing, and reconstitution.

**WHY IT MATTERS:** A misplaced decimal kills. NCLEX will give you calculation problems and expect exact answers — these are not multiple-choice, they're fill-in-the-blank. No partial credit.

**ACT — Core Formulas:**

**Desired Over Have Method:**
Dose = (Desired / Have) × Vehicle
Example: Order: 250 mg PO. Available: 500 mg tablets. → (250/500) × 1 tablet = 0.5 tablets = ½ tablet

**IV Flow Rate (mL/hr):**
mL/hr = Total volume / Hours of infusion
Example: 1000 mL over 8 hours = 125 mL/hr

**IV Drip Rate (gtt/min):**
gtt/min = (Volume × Drop factor) / (Time in minutes)
Example: 1000 mL over 8 hours with 15 gtt/mL tubing = (1000 × 15) / 480 = 31.25 → 31 gtt/min

**Weight-Based Dosing:**
Dose = Weight (kg) × Dose per kg
Convert pounds to kg: divide by 2.2
Example: 176 lb patient, order heparin 80 units/kg. → 176 ÷ 2.2 = 80 kg → 80 × 80 = 6,400 units

**Reconstitution:**
Read the label. It tells you how much diluent to add and what concentration results.
Example: Vial says "Add 10 mL diluent for 250 mg/mL." Order: 500 mg. → 500/250 = 2 mL to administer

**IV Medication Infusion:**
If you have concentration and dose rate:
mL/hr = (Dose/hr) / Concentration
Example: Dopamine 5 mcg/kg/min for 70 kg patient. Available: 400 mg in 250 mL.
Dose: 5 × 70 = 350 mcg/min × 60 = 21,000 mcg/hr = 21 mg/hr
Concentration: 400 mg / 250 mL = 1.6 mg/mL
Rate: 21 / 1.6 = 13.125 → 13.1 mL/hr

**TEST TIP:** On calc questions: check your units, and always gut-check the answer. If you're calculating 10 tablets or 50 mL for an IM injection — you made an error, full stop. Where NCLEX gets you: forgetting to convert lb to kg, mixing up mg and mcg (1 mg = 1000 mcg), and botching drip rate rounding (round to the nearest whole drop).

**PRACTICE:**
A provider orders heparin 18 units/kg/hr IV for a client weighing 198 lbs. The available solution is heparin 25,000 units in 500 mL D5W. What rate should the nurse set the IV pump to? (Round to the nearest tenth.)

Calculation:
198 lb ÷ 2.2 = 90 kg
90 kg × 18 units/hr = 1,620 units/hr
Concentration: 25,000 units / 500 mL = 50 units/mL
Rate: 1,620 / 50 = **32.4 mL/hr**

---

## MEDICATION ADMINISTRATION ROUTES
**Pharmacological Therapies** · **Exam Weight: 16%** · Connects to: → See Section 1: Safe Care

**WHAT:** Selecting and safely using the correct route for medication delivery: oral, sublingual, buccal, topical, transdermal, ophthalmic, otic, nasal, inhaled, rectal, vaginal, intradermal, subcutaneous, intramuscular, and intravenous.

**WHY IT MATTERS:** Route isn't a minor detail — it determines onset, peak, duration, and bioavailability. Give an IM medication IV and you can cause cardiac arrest. Wrong route = wrong effect or a dead patient.

**ACT — Key Route Considerations:**

**Injection Sites and Techniques:**
- Intradermal (ID): inner forearm, 5-15° angle, 26-27 gauge, form a wheal (TB test, allergy testing). Do NOT aspirate. Do NOT massage.
- Subcutaneous (SubQ): abdomen (1 inch from umbilicus), upper arm, thigh. 45-90° angle depending on tissue. Do NOT aspirate for insulin or heparin. Rotate sites.
- Intramuscular (IM): vastus lateralis (preferred for infants), ventrogluteal (preferred for adults — safest, no major vessels/nerves), deltoid (small volumes ≤1 mL in adults). 90° angle. Z-track technique for medications that stain or irritate tissue (iron dextran).
- IV: fastest onset, used for emergencies and when precise blood levels needed. Regular insulin is the ONLY insulin given IV.

**Special Administration Rules:**
- Sublingual: under the tongue, do not eat/drink until dissolved. Nitroglycerin: up to 3 doses, 5 min apart. Call 911 if no relief after first dose (2015 AHA guideline change).
- Eye drops: pull down lower lid, drop into conjunctival sac, press on lacrimal duct (inner corner) for 1-2 min to prevent systemic absorption. Wait 5 min between different eye drops.
- Ear drops: adults — pull pinna UP and BACK. Children <3 years — pull pinna DOWN and BACK. Warm to body temperature.
- Inhaled medications: bronchodilator FIRST (opens airways), then corticosteroid. Rinse mouth after corticosteroid (prevents oral candidiasis — thrush).
- Transdermal patches: remove old patch first, rotate sites, apply to clean/dry/hairless skin, do NOT cut patches.
- Rectal suppositories: insert beyond internal sphincter, have patient retain 15-20 min.

**CONNECT:** → See Section 6: IV Therapy · → See Section 1: Safe Care (needle safety)

**TEST TIP:** The most commonly tested route question: "The nurse should administer this medication by which route?" Answer by thinking about onset needed and patient status. Can't swallow? Not oral. Need immediate effect? IV. Sustained release needed? Transdermal. Also heavily tested: injection site selection — ventrogluteal is ALWAYS the best IM site for adults.

**PRACTICE:**
A nurse is teaching a client about using a metered-dose inhaler (MDI) with two medications: albuterol and beclomethasone. In what order should the client use these inhalers?

A. Beclomethasone first, then albuterol
B. Albuterol first, then beclomethasone
C. Either order is acceptable
D. Mix them in a nebulizer and take together

**Answer: B.** Albuterol (bronchodilator) first — it opens the airways so the beclomethasone (corticosteroid) can reach deeper into the lungs for better anti-inflammatory effect. Rinse mouth after the corticosteroid to prevent thrush.

---

## IV THERAPY AND FLUID MANAGEMENT
**Pharmacological Therapies** · **Exam Weight: 16%** · Connects to: → See Section 8: Electrolyte Imbalances, Fluid Balance

**WHAT:** Administering intravenous fluids, monitoring fluid balance, managing IV access, and recognizing complications of IV therapy.

**WHY IT MATTERS:** IV fluids are medications — the wrong fluid or rate can cause fluid overload, electrolyte disturbances, or cellular damage. You must know which fluids go where, and what happens when they go wrong.

**MECHANISM:**
- **Isotonic solutions** (NS 0.9%, LR, D5W): same osmolarity as blood → fluid stays in the intravascular space → volume expansion. Use for: dehydration, blood loss, shock
- **Hypotonic solutions** (0.45% NS, D5W after dextrose metabolizes): lower osmolarity → fluid moves INTO cells. Use for: cellular dehydration, hypernatremia. Do NOT use in: increased ICP (worsens cerebral edema), burns, trauma
- **Hypertonic solutions** (3% NS, D10W, D5NS, D5LR): higher osmolarity → fluid moves OUT of cells into vasculature. Use for: severe hyponatremia, cerebral edema. Must infuse via central line (3% saline). Monitor closely for fluid overload

**Key Principle:** Water follows salt (osmosis). Fluids move from low concentration to high concentration across semipermeable membranes. If you put hypertonic fluid in the blood, water leaves cells and enters the blood. If you put hypotonic fluid in the blood, water leaves the blood and enters cells.

**RECOGNIZE — IV Complications:**

| Complication | Signs | Action |
|-------------|-------|--------|
| Infiltration | Cool, pale skin at site; edema; no blood return; decreased flow rate | Stop infusion, remove catheter, elevate extremity, warm compress |
| Extravasation | Same as infiltration but with vesicant drug → tissue necrosis | Stop infusion, aspirate residual drug if possible, apply antidote per protocol, do NOT flush line |
| Phlebitis | Red, warm, hard vein; pain; streak along vein | Stop infusion, remove catheter, warm compress, restart at new site |
| Air embolism | Sudden dyspnea, chest pain, hypotension, churning sound over heart | Clamp line, left side-lying with head down, call for help |
| Fluid overload | Dyspnea, crackles, JVD, peripheral edema, ↑BP, bounding pulse | Slow or stop infusion, elevate HOB, notify provider, administer diuretics as ordered |
| Catheter-related bloodstream infection | Fever, chills, redness/purulence at site, ↑WBC | Remove catheter, send tip for culture, blood cultures, antibiotics |
| Speed shock | Rapid onset flushing, irregular pulse, shock, cardiac arrest | Stop infusion, maintain IV access, call provider — caused by too-rapid medication administration |

**ACT:**
1. Verify order: fluid type, rate, duration, additives
2. Check compatibility before mixing or piggybacking medications
3. Monitor I&O strictly — compare to expected (minimum 30 mL/hr urine output)
4. Assess IV site at least every shift: check for infiltration, phlebitis, securement
5. Change tubing per facility policy (typically every 72-96 hours for continuous; every 24 hours for lipids/blood/TPN)
6. Flush peripheral IVs per policy to maintain patency

**MEDS:**
- Normal Saline (0.9% NaCl): Purpose — isotonic volume expansion · *Why?* Stays in the vascular space; compatible with blood products; used for most IV pushes
- Lactated Ringer's: Purpose — isotonic volume replacement with electrolytes · *Why?* Closest to plasma composition; preferred in burns/surgical losses. Do NOT use with blood products (calcium in LR causes clotting in the tubing)
- D5W: Purpose — isotonic in the bag but becomes hypotonic in the body · *Why?* Dextrose is metabolized quickly, leaving free water; provides minimal calories (170 kcal/L); can dilute electrolytes if over-infused

**CONNECT:** → See Section 8: Fluid & Electrolyte Imbalances · → See Section 6: Blood Products · → See Section 6: Parenteral Nutrition

**TEST TIP:** NCLEX loves: "which IV solution should the nurse question?" The answer is the one that will worsen the patient's condition — hypotonic fluids for a patient with increased ICP (cerebral edema worsens), isotonic saline for heart failure (volume overload worsens), LR with blood (causes clotting). Also know: D5W is isotonic in the bag but hypotonic once the dextrose is metabolized — it acts as free water in the body.

**PRACTICE:**
A nurse is preparing to administer an IV infusion to a client with increased intracranial pressure (ICP). Which IV solution should the nurse question?

A. 0.9% Normal Saline
B. 0.45% Normal Saline
C. 3% Hypertonic Saline
D. Lactated Ringer's

**Answer: B.** 0.45% NS is hypotonic — it would cause water to shift into brain cells, INCREASING ICP and worsening cerebral edema. For elevated ICP, use isotonic (NS) or hypertonic solutions (3% saline draws water OUT of swollen brain cells). This is exactly the kind of "which should the nurse question" item NCLEX loves.

---

## BLOOD PRODUCT ADMINISTRATION
**Pharmacological Therapies** · **Exam Weight: 16%** · Connects to: → See Section 8: Hematological Conditions

**WHAT:** Safely transfusing blood and blood products (packed RBCs, fresh frozen plasma, platelets, cryoprecipitate, albumin) and recognizing/managing transfusion reactions.

**WHY IT MATTERS:** Transfusion errors are among the most dangerous nursing errors. ABO incompatibility can be fatal within minutes. NCLEX wants to know you can prevent, recognize, and respond to reactions.

**ACT — Critical Safety Steps:**
1. Verify blood type and crossmatch with TWO nurses at the bedside (check: patient ID, blood bank label, blood type, Rh factor, expiration date, unit number)
2. Take baseline vitals before starting
3. Infuse with ONLY 0.9% NS — no other fluids, no medications through the same line. Use a Y-tubing with filter
4. Start SLOWLY (first 15 min at ~2 mL/min or per policy) — most fatal reactions occur in the first 15-50 mL
5. Stay with the patient for the first 15 minutes; recheck vitals at 15 min, then per policy
6. Infuse within 4 hours max (bacterial growth risk at room temperature)
7. Never put blood back in the refrigerator once warmed; never microwave or hot-water-heat blood

**RECOGNIZE — Transfusion Reactions:**

| Reaction | Timing | Signs | Priority Action |
|----------|--------|-------|----------------|
| Acute hemolytic | Minutes (first 50 mL) | Fever, chills, low back pain, hypotension, dark urine (hemoglobinuria), chest tightness | STOP immediately, NS to keep vein open, notify provider + blood bank, send tubing and bag to lab |
| Febrile non-hemolytic | Within 2 hours | Fever (>1°C rise), chills, headache, flushing | Stop transfusion, notify provider; may restart after assessment if mild |
| Allergic (mild) | During or shortly after | Urticaria (hives), itching, flushing | Stop transfusion, give antihistamine (diphenhydramine), may restart if symptoms resolve |
| Anaphylactic | Rapid onset | Dyspnea, wheezing, stridor, hypotension, angioedema | STOP immediately, epinephrine, maintain airway, CPR if needed |
| Transfusion-related acute lung injury (TRALI) | Within 6 hours | Acute respiratory distress, non-cardiogenic pulmonary edema, hypoxemia, fever | Stop transfusion, supportive care (O₂, possible intubation), notify provider |
| Fluid overload (TACO) | During or after | Dyspnea, crackles, JVD, HTN, tachycardia | Slow or stop, sit upright, diuretics, O₂ |

**Universal First Response to Any Reaction:** STOP the transfusion → Keep the IV open with NS → Stay with the patient → Notify provider and blood bank → Send blood bag and tubing to lab → Document everything.

**CONNECT:** → See Section 8: Hematological Conditions (indications for transfusion) · → See Section 7: Lab Values (H&H, type and screen)

**TEST TIP:** The single most important thing: STOP the transfusion first. Every reaction answer starts with stopping the blood. Then maintain the IV (you'll need access for emergency meds). The most dangerous reaction (acute hemolytic) happens fast and includes flank/low back pain — that's the kidneys protesting as they try to clear destroyed red blood cells. Also remember: type O negative is the universal donor for RBCs; type AB positive is the universal recipient.

**PRACTICE:**
A client receiving a blood transfusion develops low back pain, fever, and dark amber urine 10 minutes after the transfusion was started. What is the nurse's priority action?

A. Slow the transfusion rate and monitor
B. Administer diphenhydramine IV
C. Stop the transfusion and maintain IV access with NS
D. Obtain a urine specimen for analysis

**Answer: C.** Low back pain + fever + dark urine within minutes = acute hemolytic transfusion reaction. This is an emergency. Stop the blood immediately, keep the vein open with normal saline (you'll need it for emergency medications and fluids), then notify the provider and blood bank. Do not slow it — stop it. Diphenhydramine treats allergic reactions, not hemolytic reactions.

---

## DRUG CLASSIFICATIONS BY SUFFIX SYSTEM
**Pharmacological Therapies** · **Exam Weight: 16%** · Connects to: → See Section 8: Cardiac, Respiratory, Endocrine Conditions

**WHAT:** Organizing drugs by their name endings (suffixes) to rapidly identify drug class, mechanism, side effects, and nursing considerations — even for drugs you've never seen before.

**WHY IT MATTERS:** NCLEX *will* throw drug names you've never seen. That's by design. The suffix tells you the class, the class tells you the nursing implications — and that's how you answer the question without having memorized the drug. This is your pharmacology survival system. Build it now.

**ACT — Drug Classification Reference:**

### Beta-Blockers (-olol)
Drugs: metoprolol, atenolol, propranolol, carvedilol, labetalol
Action: Block beta-adrenergic receptors → ↓HR, ↓BP, ↓myocardial oxygen demand
Uses: HTN, angina, HF, MI, arrhythmias, migraine prophylaxis, performance anxiety
Side effects: bradycardia, hypotension, fatigue, bronchospasm (avoid in asthma — beta-2 blockade constricts airways), masks hypoglycemia symptoms (tachycardia) in diabetics, cold extremities
Nursing: Check HR before giving — hold if <60 bpm. Do NOT stop abruptly (rebound tachycardia/HTN). Teach patient to rise slowly (orthostatic hypotension).
Antidote: glucagon
*Why beta-blockers for heart failure?* Seems counterintuitive (they weaken contraction). Long-term, they reduce the heart's workload and prevent remodeling — started at LOW doses and titrated slowly.

### ACE Inhibitors (-pril)
Drugs: lisinopril, enalapril, captopril, ramipril, benazepril
Action: Inhibit angiotensin-converting enzyme → ↓angiotensin II → vasodilation + ↓aldosterone → ↓BP and ↓fluid retention
Uses: HTN, HF, diabetic nephropathy (renal protection), post-MI
Side effects: persistent dry cough (most common — from bradykinin accumulation), hyperkalemia (↓aldosterone = ↓K excretion), angioedema (rare but life-threatening — swelling of face/lips/tongue/airway), hypotension (especially first dose)
Nursing: Monitor K+ (no potassium supplements or K-sparing diuretics). Monitor renal function (BUN/creatinine). Cough is annoying but not dangerous — switch to ARB if intolerable. CONTRAINDICATED in pregnancy (teratogenic).
*Why the cough?* ACE breaks down bradykinin. Block ACE → bradykinin accumulates → irritates airway receptors.

### ARBs (-sartan)
Drugs: losartan, valsartan, irbesartan, candesartan
Action: Block angiotensin II receptors → same effect as ACE inhibitors WITHOUT the cough
Uses: same as ACE inhibitors; used when ACE inhibitor cough is intolerable
Side effects: hyperkalemia, hypotension, but NO cough (doesn't affect bradykinin). Still CONTRAINDICATED in pregnancy.
Nursing: same monitoring as ACE inhibitors (K+, renal function)

### Calcium Channel Blockers (-dipine and others)
Dihydropyridines (-dipine): amlodipine, nifedipine — primarily vasodilators
Non-dihydropyridines: verapamil, diltiazem — also slow HR (similar to beta-blockers)
Action: Block calcium entry into smooth muscle and cardiac cells → vasodilation, ↓HR (non-DHP), ↓contractility
Uses: HTN, angina, arrhythmias (verapamil/diltiazem for SVT)
Side effects: peripheral edema (vasodilation → fluid leaks), flushing, headache, constipation (especially verapamil), bradycardia (verapamil/diltiazem)
Nursing: Monitor HR and BP. Do NOT combine verapamil with beta-blockers (additive bradycardia → heart block). Avoid grapefruit juice (inhibits metabolism → increased drug levels).

### Statins (-statin)
Drugs: atorvastatin, simvastatin, rosuvastatin, pravastatin
Action: Inhibit HMG-CoA reductase → ↓cholesterol synthesis in liver
Uses: Hyperlipidemia, CAD prevention
Side effects: myopathy/rhabdomyolysis (muscle breakdown — report unexplained muscle pain), hepatotoxicity (monitor LFTs), ↑blood glucose
Nursing: Take in the evening (cholesterol synthesis peaks at night). Monitor for muscle pain/weakness. Check CK if muscle symptoms. Liver function at baseline and periodically. Avoid grapefruit with simvastatin/atorvastatin.
*Why the muscle damage?* Statins can be toxic to skeletal muscle mitochondria; risk increases with higher doses and certain drug interactions.

### Proton Pump Inhibitors (-prazole)
Drugs: omeprazole, pantoprazole, esomeprazole, lansoprazole
Action: Irreversibly block H+/K+ ATPase pump → ↓gastric acid secretion (most potent acid suppressors)
Uses: GERD, peptic ulcers, H. pylori (part of triple therapy), Zollinger-Ellison syndrome
Side effects: long-term use → ↓calcium/magnesium absorption (osteoporosis risk), ↓B12 absorption, C. difficile risk
Nursing: Take 30-60 min before meals (need an acid-free environment to activate). Do NOT crush enteric-coated forms.

### Benzodiazepines (-pam/-lam)
Drugs: lorazepam, diazepam, midazolam, alprazolam, clonazepam
Action: Enhance GABA activity → CNS depression → anxiolysis, sedation, anticonvulsant, muscle relaxation
Uses: anxiety, seizures, alcohol withdrawal, pre-procedure sedation, insomnia
Side effects: sedation, respiratory depression, dependence, paradoxical agitation (elderly), amnesia
Nursing: Monitor respiratory status. Fall precautions. Do NOT combine with opioids or alcohol (additive CNS depression). Taper slowly — abrupt discontinuation → seizures.
Antidote: flumazenil (but use cautiously — can precipitate seizures in dependent patients)

### Penicillins (-cillin) and Cephalosporins (cef-/ceph-)
Penicillins: amoxicillin, ampicillin, piperacillin
Cephalosporins: cephalexin, ceftriaxone, cefepime (1st–4th generation)
Action: Inhibit bacterial cell wall synthesis → bactericidal
Side effects: allergic reactions (rash to anaphylaxis), GI upset, superinfection (C. difficile)
Nursing: ALWAYS ask about penicillin allergy before giving either class. Cross-sensitivity between penicillins and cephalosporins exists (~1-2%). Monitor for anaphylaxis with first dose (have epinephrine available). Take full course.

### Additional Key Suffixes:
- **-mycin/-micin** (aminoglycosides: gentamicin, tobramycin): nephrotoxic AND ototoxic — monitor peak/trough levels, renal function, hearing
- **-floxacin** (fluoroquinolones: ciprofloxacin, levofloxacin): tendon rupture risk (especially Achilles), photosensitivity, avoid in children
- **-pine** (atypical antipsychotics: olanzapine, quetiapine, clozapine): metabolic syndrome (weight gain, hyperglycemia, dyslipidemia); clozapine requires weekly WBC monitoring (agranulocytosis risk)
- **-triptan** (sumatriptan, rizatriptan): serotonin agonists for migraines; avoid with MAOIs/SSRIs (serotonin syndrome risk)
- **-tidine** (H2 blockers: ranitidine [withdrawn], famotidine): reduce acid but less potent than PPIs

**CONNECT:** → See Section 8: Cardiac Conditions (beta-blockers, ACE-I, CCBs) · → See Section 8: Endocrine (insulin, thyroid meds)

**TEST TIP:** Mystery drug on your screen? Read the suffix FIRST — it's your lifeline. Even brand-new drugs follow naming conventions. See something ending in -olol? You already know: check heart rate, watch for bronchospasm, never stop it abruptly. You don't need to know the drug. You need to know the pattern.

**PRACTICE:**
A patient is newly prescribed enalapril. The nurse has not administered this specific drug before. Based on the drug suffix, which assessment is the nurse's priority before the first dose?

A. Check blood glucose level
B. Assess heart rate and rhythm
C. Check serum potassium and blood pressure
D. Monitor respiratory rate and oxygen saturation

**Answer: C.** The suffix -pril identifies ACE inhibitors, which lower blood pressure and can cause hyperkalemia. Before first dose, check baseline BP (to watch for hypotension) and potassium (ACE inhibitors retain potassium). You've never seen this exact drug, but the suffix tells you everything you need. *Why the suffix system matters:* NCLEX may use brand names or newer drugs. The suffix is your shortcut to safe practice.

---

## ANTIDOTES
**Pharmacological Therapies** · **Exam Weight: 16%** · Connects to: → See Section 8: Emergency Care

**WHAT:** Specific reversal agents for drug toxicities and overdoses. These are emergency medications — you need to know them cold.

| Drug/Toxin | Antidote | Key Point |
|-----------|----------|-----------|
| Warfarin (Coumadin) | Vitamin K (phytonadione) | Takes 24-48 hr for full effect; give FFP for immediate reversal of life-threatening bleeding |
| Heparin | Protamine sulfate | Give slow IV push; can cause hypotension and anaphylaxis; dose based on amount of heparin given |
| Opioids (morphine, fentanyl, etc.) | Naloxone (Narcan) | Short half-life — may need repeat doses; can precipitate acute withdrawal in dependent patients; monitor for re-sedation |
| Benzodiazepines | Flumazenil (Romazicon) | Use cautiously — can cause seizures in chronic benzo users or mixed overdoses; short acting |
| Beta-blockers | Glucagon | Bypasses beta receptors to increase HR and contractility via separate pathway |
| Digoxin | Digoxin immune Fab (Digibind) | For life-threatening toxicity (symptomatic bradycardia, hyperkalemia, ventricular arrhythmias) |
| Methotrexate | Leucovorin (folinic acid) | "Leucovorin rescue" — replaces folate that methotrexate blocks; timing is critical |
| Tricyclic antidepressants | Sodium bicarbonate | Counteracts cardiac sodium channel blockade; widening QRS is the danger sign |
| Acetaminophen (Tylenol) | N-acetylcysteine (Mucomyst/Acetadote) | Most effective within 8 hours of ingestion; smells terrible PO; liver transplant if too late |
| Magnesium sulfate (MgSO₄) | Calcium gluconate | MgSO₄ toxicity: absent DTRs, respiratory depression, cardiac arrest |
| Lead poisoning | Succimer (oral chelation) or CaEDTA (IV chelation) | Remove the source first; chelation binds the lead for excretion |
| Organophosphate poisoning | Atropine + pralidoxime (2-PAM) | Organophosphates = cholinergic crisis (SLUDGE: Salivation, Lacrimation, Urination, Defecation, GI distress, Emesis) |
| Iron | Deferoxamine | Urine turns red-orange (expected); for severe iron toxicity |

**TEST TIP:** NCLEX loves antidote matching. Memorize these pairs — they show up on almost every exam. The highest-yield ones: naloxone for opioids, protamine for heparin, vitamin K for warfarin, and calcium gluconate for mag sulfate. Also know naloxone's critical limitation: its half-life is shorter than most opioids, so the patient can re-sedate after it wears off. Keep monitoring.

**PRACTICE:**
A client on a heparin drip develops oozing from venipuncture sites, hematuria, and an aPTT of 120 seconds (therapeutic range: 46-70 seconds). The provider orders the antidote. Which medication should the nurse prepare?

A. Vitamin K
B. Protamine sulfate
C. Naloxone
D. Flumazenil

**Answer: B.** Protamine sulfate reverses heparin. The clinical picture — active bleeding with supratherapeutic aPTT — indicates heparin overdose. Vitamin K reverses warfarin (monitored by PT/INR, not aPTT). The heparin-protamine and warfarin-vitamin K pairings are among the most tested pharmacology facts on the NCLEX.

---

## NARROW THERAPEUTIC INDEX DRUGS
**Pharmacological Therapies** · **Exam Weight: 16%** · Connects to: → See Section 7: Lab Values

**WHAT:** Drugs where the difference between a therapeutic dose and a toxic dose is dangerously small. These require regular blood level monitoring.

| Drug | Therapeutic Level | Toxic Signs | Monitoring Notes |
|------|------------------|-------------|-----------------|
| Digoxin | 0.8–2.0 ng/mL | Nausea, vomiting, visual disturbances (yellow-green halos, blurred vision), bradycardia, arrhythmias | Check apical pulse ×1 min before giving — hold if <60. Check K+ (hypokalemia ↑toxicity). Check renal function. |
| Lithium | 0.8–1.5 mEq/L (maintenance) | Mild (1.5-2.0): N/V, diarrhea, tremor, drowsiness. Moderate (2.0-3.5): confusion, ataxia, ↑ tremor. Severe (>3.5): seizures, coma, death | Maintain consistent Na+ intake (lithium and sodium compete for reabsorption). Adequate hydration. Draw levels 8-12 hr post-dose. Avoid NSAIDs, ACE-I, diuretics (↑lithium levels). |
| Phenytoin (Dilantin) | 10–20 mcg/mL | Nystagmus (early), ataxia, slurred speech, confusion, gingival hyperplasia (chronic) | Monitor levels. Oral care for gum overgrowth. Many drug interactions. IV: give slowly, use NS only (precipitates in dextrose). |
| Theophylline | 10–20 mcg/mL | Tachycardia, arrhythmias, seizures, N/V | Avoid caffeine (same mechanism). Many drug interactions. |
| Warfarin | INR 2.0–3.0 | Bleeding (gums, nosebleeds, bruising, hematuria, dark stools) | Regular PT/INR monitoring. Consistent vitamin K intake (don't eliminate — keep consistent). Many drug-food interactions. |
| Vancomycin | Trough: 15–20 mcg/mL | "Red Man Syndrome" (rapid infusion → histamine release → flushing, rash — NOT true allergy); nephrotoxicity; ototoxicity | Infuse over ≥60 min. Monitor peak/trough levels. Monitor renal function and hearing. |
| Aminoglycosides (gentamicin) | Peak: 5–10; Trough: <2 mcg/mL | Nephrotoxicity (↑creatinine), ototoxicity (hearing loss, tinnitus, vertigo) | Monitor peak and trough. Assess hearing. Monitor renal function. |

**TEST TIP:** "The client's drug level is [number]. What should the nurse do?" Here's your framework: Above therapeutic range → hold the dose, notify the provider, watch for toxicity. Below range → give the dose, but flag it — the drug may not be working. And know your lab-drug pairs cold: digoxin level for digoxin, lithium level for lithium, PT/INR for warfarin, aPTT for heparin.

**PRACTICE:**
A patient taking lithium for bipolar disorder has a serum lithium level of 2.1 mEq/L (therapeutic range 0.6-1.2 mEq/L). The patient reports nausea, vomiting, coarse hand tremors, and drowsiness. What is the nurse's priority action?
A. Administer the next scheduled lithium dose and recheck the level in 4 hours
B. Hold the lithium, notify the provider, and monitor for worsening toxicity
C. Encourage the patient to increase sodium and fluid intake
D. Administer activated charcoal to reduce lithium absorption

**Answer: B.** A lithium level of 2.1 is in the toxic range. The symptoms (nausea, vomiting, coarse tremors, drowsiness) are consistent with lithium toxicity — these are more severe than the fine hand tremors seen at therapeutic levels. Hold the drug immediately, notify the provider (may need IV fluids, possible dialysis for severe toxicity), and monitor closely. Never give the next dose of a drug that's already at toxic levels. Increasing sodium/fluids helps prevent toxicity but won't treat an actively toxic level. Activated charcoal doesn't effectively bind lithium. *Why B works:* Supratherapeutic drug level + toxic symptoms = hold the drug, notify, and monitor.

---
## INSULIN MANAGEMENT
**Pharmacological Therapies** · **Exam Weight: 16%** · Connects to: → See Section 8: Diabetes, DKA

**WHAT:** Types of insulin, onset/peak/duration, administration, and management of hypo- and hyperglycemia.

**ACT — Insulin Types:**

| Type | Examples | Onset | Peak | Duration | Key Points |
|------|---------|-------|------|----------|------------|
| Rapid-acting | Lispro (Humalog), Aspart (Novolog), Glulisine (Apidra) | <15 min | 30-90 min | 3-5 hr | Give with meals (within 15 min of eating). Clear solution. |
| Short-acting (Regular) | Humulin R, Novolin R | 30-60 min | 2-4 hr | 5-8 hr | Give 30 min before meals. ONLY insulin given IV. Clear solution. Can be mixed with NPH. |
| Intermediate (NPH) | Humulin N, Novolin N | 1-4 hr | 6-12 hr | 12-18 hr | Cloudy — roll gently, do NOT shake. Peak is broad → hypoglycemia risk mid-cycle. |
| Long-acting | Glargine (Lantus), Detemir (Levemir) | 1-2 hr | No peak (peakless) | 20-24+ hr | Once daily, same time each day. Clear solution. Do NOT mix with other insulins. No peak = lower hypoglycemia risk. |

**Mixing Insulin:** When drawing up Regular + NPH in the same syringe:
- Draw up Regular (clear) FIRST, then NPH (cloudy) — "Clear before cloudy"
- *Why?* If NPH contaminates the Regular vial, it changes the Regular's action time. Regular is used for predictable dosing and IV use — contamination would be dangerous.

**Hypoglycemia (blood glucose <70 mg/dL):**
- Signs: shakiness, sweating, tachycardia, confusion, irritability, hunger, pallor, seizures, LOC
- Treatment (conscious): Rule of 15 — give 15 g fast-acting carbs (4 oz juice, glucose tablets), recheck in 15 min, repeat if still <70
- Treatment (unconscious): glucagon IM/SubQ, or dextrose 50% IV. Never give oral glucose to an unconscious patient (aspiration risk)
- *Why does it happen?* Too much insulin, missed meal, excess exercise, alcohol (blocks gluconeogenesis)

**Hyperglycemia (blood glucose >250 mg/dL):**
- Signs: polyuria, polydipsia, polyphagia (the 3 P's), blurred vision, Kussmaul breathing if DKA, fruity/acetone breath
- → See Section 8: DKA and HHS for emergency management

**CONNECT:** → See Section 8: Diabetes, DKA, HHS · → See Section 5: Nutrition (diabetic diet)

**TEST TIP:** Know your peaks — that's when hypoglycemia risk is highest. Rapid peaks in 30-90 min (watch after meals). NPH peaks around 6-12 hours (watch mid-afternoon or middle of night depending on when given). Long-acting has no peak (steady coverage). The most-tested insulin fact: Regular is the ONLY insulin given IV.

**PRACTICE:**
A nurse administers NPH insulin at 0800. At what time should the nurse most closely monitor for hypoglycemia?

A. 0830
B. 1000
C. 1400
D. 2000

**Answer: C.** NPH peaks at 6-12 hours after administration. 0800 + 6 hours = 1400 (2:00 PM). This is when the insulin effect is strongest and hypoglycemia risk is highest. The nurse should ensure the patient has eaten lunch and has a snack available.

---

## ANTICOAGULANT THERAPY
**Pharmacological Therapies** · **Exam Weight: 16%** · Connects to: → See Section 7: Lab Values · → See Section 8: Cardiac Conditions

**WHAT:** Heparin and warfarin — two anticoagulants with different mechanisms, monitoring, and reversal. NCLEX tests this topic relentlessly.

| Feature | Heparin | Warfarin (Coumadin) |
|---------|---------|---------------------|
| Route | IV (continuous drip) or SubQ | PO only |
| Onset | Immediate (IV) | 3-5 days (takes time to deplete existing clotting factors) |
| Lab monitoring | aPTT (activated partial thromboplastin time) | PT/INR (prothrombin time / International Normalized Ratio) |
| Therapeutic range | aPTT 1.5-2.5× control (typically 46-70 sec) | INR 2.0-3.0 (mechanical valve: 2.5-3.5) |
| Antidote | Protamine sulfate | Vitamin K (phytonadione) |
| Safe in pregnancy? | YES (does not cross placenta) | NO (teratogenic — causes birth defects) |
| Key interactions | Increased bleeding with NSAIDs, aspirin | MANY: vitamin K foods, antibiotics, NSAIDs, herbs (ginkgo, garlic, ginger) |

**Remember:** Heparin = Hospital (IV, fast acting, monitored by aPTT). Warfarin = Weeks to work (oral, slow onset, monitored by PT/INR).

**Patient Teaching — Warfarin:**
- Maintain consistent vitamin K intake (don't eliminate green leafy vegetables — keep intake STABLE)
- Avoid alcohol (increases bleeding risk and affects liver metabolism)
- Use soft toothbrush, electric razor
- Report: unusual bruising, blood in urine/stool, nosebleeds, bleeding gums, heavy menstruation
- Wear medical alert identification
- Inform all healthcare providers, including dentists

**CONNECT:** → See Section 6: Antidotes · → See Section 7: Lab Values · → See Section 8: DVT, PE, A-Fib

**PRACTICE:**
A client on warfarin has an INR of 4.8. The client denies any signs of bleeding. What should the nurse anticipate?

A. Continue the current warfarin dose
B. Hold warfarin and monitor INR
C. Administer vitamin K IV immediately
D. Administer protamine sulfate

**Answer: B.** INR 4.8 is supratherapeutic (target 2-3) but without active bleeding, the typical intervention is to hold the drug and recheck. Vitamin K would be given for active bleeding or significantly elevated INR. Protamine is for heparin, not warfarin. Continuing the dose would increase bleeding risk further.

---

## HIGH-YIELD INDIVIDUAL DRUGS
**Pharmacological Therapies** · **Exam Weight: 16%**

### Digoxin
- Therapeutic level: 0.8-2.0 ng/mL
- Check apical pulse for 1 full minute before administration → hold if <60 bpm (adult) or <70 bpm (child)
- Toxicity signs: anorexia, nausea/vomiting, visual disturbances (yellow-green halos), bradycardia, dysrhythmias
- Critical link: hypokalemia increases digoxin toxicity — always check K+ level. *Why?* Digoxin and potassium compete for the same binding sites on the cardiac cell. Low K+ = more digoxin binding = toxicity.
- Antidote: Digoxin immune Fab (Digibind)
- → See Section 8: Heart Failure

### MAOI Interactions
- MAOIs (phenelzine, tranylcypromine): inhibit monoamine oxidase → ↑norepinephrine, serotonin, dopamine
- TYRAMINE RESTRICTION: Aged/fermented foods (aged cheese, cured meats, sauerkraut, soy sauce, tap beer, red wine) contain tyramine, which is normally broken down by MAO. Without MAO → tyramine accumulates → massive norepinephrine release → hypertensive crisis
- Signs of hypertensive crisis: severe headache, neck stiffness, nausea, diaphoresis, BP >180/120
- Also avoid: decongestants (pseudoephedrine), meperidine, SSRIs, St. John's Wort (serotonin syndrome risk)

### TB Drugs — RIPE Therapy
- **R**ifampin: turns all body fluids red-orange (urine, tears, sweat — warn about stained contact lenses). Potent enzyme inducer → reduces effectiveness of many drugs including oral contraceptives.
- **I**soniazid (INH): peripheral neuropathy risk → supplement with vitamin B6 (pyridoxine). *Why?* INH interferes with B6 metabolism, and B6 is essential for nerve function. Hepatotoxic — monitor LFTs. Avoid alcohol.
- **P**yrazinamide: hepatotoxic, increases uric acid (may worsen gout)
- **E**thambutol: visual toxicity (optic neuritis) — baseline and monthly vision exams. Report any change in color vision or acuity. *Why?* It concentrates in retinal tissue.
- Treatment duration: minimum 6-9 months. Compliance is critical — DOT (directly observed therapy) for reliability.

### Herbal Supplement Interactions
- St. John's Wort: induces liver enzymes → reduces effectiveness of warfarin, digoxin, oral contraceptives, SSRIs, immunosuppressants. Also risk of serotonin syndrome with SSRIs/SNRIs.
- Ginkgo biloba: antiplatelet effect → increased bleeding risk with anticoagulants
- Garlic: same antiplatelet effect → increased bleeding
- Ginseng: can decrease warfarin effectiveness; may cause hypoglycemia
- Kava: hepatotoxic; additive CNS depression with sedatives
- Ephedra/Ma Huang: sympathomimetic → HTN, tachycardia, stroke risk (banned in US supplements but still encountered)
- *Why does this matter?* Patients often don't report herbal supplements as "medications." ALWAYS ask specifically.

**PRACTICE:**
A patient taking warfarin for atrial fibrillation tells the nurse she has started taking ginkgo biloba and St. John's Wort for mood support. Which is the nurse's greatest concern?

A. St. John's Wort will increase warfarin levels, causing toxicity
B. Ginkgo biloba will increase bleeding risk while St. John's Wort will decrease warfarin effectiveness
C. Both supplements will increase the anticoagulant effect of warfarin
D. Ginkgo biloba will decrease warfarin absorption

**Answer: B.** This is a two-hit problem going in opposite directions. Ginkgo has antiplatelet properties that ADD to warfarin's effect, increasing bleeding risk. Meanwhile, St. John's Wort induces liver enzymes that ACCELERATE warfarin metabolism, making it less effective — increasing clotting risk. The patient is simultaneously at higher risk for bleeding (ginkgo) and clot formation (subtherapeutic warfarin from St. John's Wort). Both supplements must be stopped and the provider notified. *Why B works:* The question tests whether you understand that two supplements can create opposite and equally dangerous problems at the same time.

---

## PARENTERAL NUTRITION (TPN/PPN)
**Pharmacological Therapies** · **Exam Weight: 16%** · Connects to: → See Section 5: Nutrition

**WHAT:** IV delivery of complete nutrition (dextrose, amino acids, lipids, electrolytes, vitamins) when the GI tract is non-functional.

**ACT:**
- TPN (total parenteral nutrition): via CENTRAL line only (hyperosmolar — would damage peripheral veins). Contains high-concentration dextrose (>10%).
- PPN (peripheral parenteral nutrition): via peripheral line. Lower dextrose concentration. Short-term use only.
- Monitor blood glucose every 4-6 hours — high dextrose content causes hyperglycemia. May need insulin coverage.
- Do NOT abruptly discontinue TPN — taper gradually. *Why?* Sudden stop → rebound hypoglycemia (the pancreas is still producing insulin for the high glucose load, but the glucose source is gone).
- Change tubing every 24 hours. Do NOT add medications to the TPN bag. Use an infusion pump (never gravity).
- Monitor for infection at central line site (TPN is an excellent growth medium for bacteria).
- If a bag is unavailable and the line must be maintained, hang D10W to prevent hypoglycemia while awaiting new bag.

**CONNECT:** → See Section 5: Nutrition · → See Section 6: IV Therapy · → See Section 7: Lab Values (glucose monitoring)

**TEST TIP:** If TPN must be stopped and a new bag isn't available, the answer is D10W — it prevents rebound hypoglycemia while you wait. Also: TPN goes through a central line only. If a question says "peripheral IV" and "TPN," that's the error.

**PRACTICE:**
A patient receiving TPN through a central line reports diaphoresis, shakiness, and confusion. The nurse discovers the TPN infusion was inadvertently stopped 45 minutes ago. What is the nurse's priority action?
A. Restart the TPN at the previously prescribed rate
B. Hang D10W and infuse at the previous TPN rate
C. Check the patient's blood glucose level
D. Notify the health care provider immediately

**Answer: C.** Assess first. The symptoms suggest hypoglycemia from rebound insulin effect after abrupt TPN cessation, but the nurse must confirm with a blood glucose level before intervening. After confirming hypoglycemia, hang D10W to bridge until TPN can be restarted. *Why C works:* Assessment before intervention — treat the confirmed problem, not the assumed one.

# SECTION 7: REDUCTION OF RISK POTENTIAL

Your job isn't just doing tasks — it's catching problems before they become emergencies. This section is about the monitoring, the lab interpretation, the moment you look at a number and know something's wrong. NCLEX loves testing whether you can connect a value on a screen to a patient in the bed.

**Exam Weight: 9–15% (midpoint 12%)** · This section is about catching problems before they become emergencies. It's the assessment-heavy section — lab values, diagnostic tests, monitoring, and the clinical reasoning that connects data to action.

---

## LAB VALUES AND CLINICAL SIGNIFICANCE
**Reduction of Risk Potential** · **Exam Weight: 12%** · Connects to: → See Section 8: All Physiological Adaptation topics

**WHAT:** Laboratory results don't exist in isolation — they tell a clinical story. NCLEX doesn't just test whether you know the normal range. It tests whether you know what to DO with an abnormal value.

**ACT — Complete Lab Value Reference with Clinical Significance:**

### Renal Function
| Lab | Normal | Clinical Significance |
|-----|--------|----------------------|
| BUN (Blood Urea Nitrogen) | 5-20 mg/dL | Elevated in: dehydration (↑BUN with normal creatinine), renal failure, GI bleeding (digested blood = protein load), high-protein diet. Decreased in: liver failure (can't make urea), malnutrition. |
| Creatinine | 0.6-1.3 mg/dL | Most reliable indicator of kidney function (not affected by diet/hydration like BUN). Rising creatinine = declining GFR = worsening renal function. |
| BUN:Creatinine Ratio | 10:1 to 20:1 | >20:1 suggests prerenal cause (dehydration, GI bleed). <10:1 suggests intrarenal damage or liver disease. |

### Glucose and Metabolic
| Lab | Normal | Clinical Significance |
|-----|--------|----------------------|
| Fasting glucose | 70-110 mg/dL | <70 = hypoglycemia (treat immediately). >126 on two occasions = diabetes diagnosis. Random >200 with symptoms = diabetes. |
| HgbA1c | <5.7% normal; <7% good diabetic control; >8% poor control | Reflects average glucose over 2-3 months. Not affected by daily fluctuations. Used to assess treatment compliance and adjust therapy. |

### Hematology
| Lab | Normal | Clinical Significance |
|-----|--------|----------------------|
| Hemoglobin | F: 12-15 g/dL; M: 14-16.5 g/dL | Oxygen-carrying capacity. Low = anemia (fatigue, pallor, tachycardia). Transfuse typically when <7 g/dL (varies by context). |
| Hematocrit | F: 35-47%; M: 42-52% | Percentage of blood that is RBCs. Rises with dehydration (hemoconcentration). Falls with overhydration or bleeding. Roughly 3× the hemoglobin value. |
| Platelets | 150,000-400,000/mm³ | <100,000 = thrombocytopenia (bleeding risk). <20,000 = spontaneous bleeding risk. >400,000 = thrombocytosis (clotting risk). Bleeding precautions when low. |
| WBC | 4,500-11,000/mm³ | >11,000 = leukocytosis (infection, inflammation, stress, steroids). <4,500 = leukopenia (immunosuppression → neutropenic precautions). |
| Neutrophils (ANC) | 1,800-7,800/mm³ | ANC <1,000 = neutropenic. ANC <500 = severe neutropenia (infection risk critical). Private room, no fresh flowers/fruit, strict hand hygiene. |

### Electrolytes
| Lab | Normal | Clinical Significance |
|-----|--------|----------------------|
| Potassium (K+) | 3.5-5.0 mEq/L | CRITICAL — both high and low can be fatal (cardiac arrhythmias). → See Section 8: Electrolytes |
| Sodium (Na+) | 135-145 mEq/L | Primary determinant of fluid balance (water follows sodium). → See Section 8: Electrolytes |
| Calcium (Ca²+) | 8.6-10.0 mg/dL (some sources 9-11) | Neuromuscular function, cardiac conduction, bone. Must interpret with albumin level (low albumin = falsely low calcium). → See Section 8: Electrolytes |
| Magnesium (Mg²+) | 1.6-2.6 mg/dL | Often overlooked. Low Mg = low Ca (can't fix calcium without fixing magnesium first). Affects neuromuscular and cardiac function. |
| Phosphorus (PO₄) | 2.7-4.5 mg/dL | INVERSE relationship with calcium (↑phos = ↓Ca). Elevated in renal failure (can't excrete it). |

### Coagulation
| Lab | Normal | Clinical Significance |
|-----|--------|----------------------|
| PT (Prothrombin Time) | 9.5-11.8 seconds | Measures extrinsic pathway. Monitored for: warfarin therapy. |
| INR | 1.0 (normal); 2.0-3.0 (warfarin); 2.5-3.5 (mechanical valve) | Standardized PT measurement. Therapeutic INR for warfarin = 2-3. Higher = bleeding risk. Lower = clotting risk. |
| aPTT | 20-36 seconds; therapeutic heparin: 46-70 seconds | Measures intrinsic pathway. Monitored for: heparin therapy. 1.5-2.5× control is therapeutic. |

### Drug Levels
| Lab | Therapeutic Level | Significance |
|-----|------------------|-------------|
| Digoxin | 0.8-2.0 ng/mL | See NTI drugs section |
| Lithium | 0.8-1.5 mEq/L | See NTI drugs section |
| Phenytoin | 10-20 mcg/mL | See NTI drugs section |
| Vancomycin trough | 15-20 mcg/mL | See NTI drugs section |

### Arterial Blood Gases
| Lab | Normal | Role |
|-----|--------|------|
| pH | 7.35-7.45 | Overall acid-base status. <7.35 = acidosis. >7.45 = alkalosis. |
| PaCO₂ | 35-45 mmHg | Respiratory component. CO₂ is an acid. Controlled by lungs. |
| HCO₃⁻ | 22-26 mEq/L | Metabolic component. Bicarbonate is a base. Controlled by kidneys. |
| PaO₂ | 80-100 mmHg | Oxygen level. <60 = respiratory failure. Does not determine acid-base status. |
| SaO₂ | >95% | Oxygen saturation. <90% = hypoxemia requiring intervention. |

**CONNECT:** → See Section 7: ABG Analysis · → See Section 6: Anticoagulants · → See Section 8: Electrolyte Imbalances

**TEST TIP:** NCLEX almost never asks "what's the normal potassium?" — that's too easy. Instead: "The client's K+ is 6.2. Which finding should the nurse expect?" (muscle weakness, peaked T waves, arrhythmias). Or: "INR is 4.2 and they're bleeding from the gums. What first?" (hold warfarin, apply pressure, notify provider). They give you the number — you have to know what it *means* at the bedside.

**PRACTICE:**
The nurse reviews morning labs for four patients. Which result requires the most immediate action?
A. Patient on furosemide: K+ 3.4 mEq/L
B. Patient on warfarin: INR 4.8, reporting bleeding gums
C. Patient with CKD: BUN 32 mg/dL
D. Patient post-op day 1: WBC 11,200/mm³

**Answer: B.** An INR of 4.8 is supratherapeutic (goal is typically 2-3) and the patient is actively bleeding — this combination requires immediate action: hold warfarin, apply pressure, notify the provider, and anticipate vitamin K administration. Option A is borderline low and needs monitoring/replacement but isn't immediately dangerous. Option C is expected in CKD. Option D is a mild post-operative stress response. *Why B works:* Active bleeding + supratherapeutic anticoagulation = immediate hemorrhage risk.*

---
## ABG ANALYSIS
**Reduction of Risk Potential** · **Exam Weight: 12%** · Connects to: → See Section 8: Respiratory Conditions

**WHAT:** Systematic interpretation of arterial blood gases to identify acid-base disturbances and guide treatment.

**MECHANISM — The ROME Method:**
- **R**espiratory = **O**pposite: In respiratory disorders, pH and CO₂ move in opposite directions
  - pH ↓ + CO₂ ↑ = respiratory acidosis (lungs retaining acid/CO₂)
  - pH ↑ + CO₂ ↓ = respiratory alkalosis (lungs blowing off too much CO₂)
- **M**etabolic = **E**qual: In metabolic disorders, pH and HCO₃ move in the same direction
  - pH ↓ + HCO₃ ↓ = metabolic acidosis (losing base or gaining acid)
  - pH ↑ + HCO₃ ↑ = metabolic alkalosis (losing acid or gaining base)

**Step-by-Step ABG Interpretation:**
1. Look at pH: <7.35 = acidosis; >7.45 = alkalosis; 7.35-7.45 = normal (but check if compensating)
2. Look at CO₂: <35 = alkalosis contribution; >45 = acidosis contribution
3. Look at HCO₃: <22 = acidosis contribution; >26 = alkalosis contribution
4. Match: which one (CO₂ or HCO₃) matches the direction of the pH? That's the primary disorder.
5. Check compensation: Is the other value abnormal too?
   - **Uncompensated:** pH abnormal + one other value abnormal (the body hasn't started compensating)
   - **Partially compensated:** pH abnormal + BOTH CO₂ and HCO₃ abnormal (compensating but not enough yet)
   - **Fully compensated:** pH NORMAL + both CO₂ and HCO₃ abnormal (compensation brought pH back to normal range)

**Common Clinical Causes:**

| Disorder | pH | CO₂ | HCO₃ | Common Causes |
|----------|-----|------|-------|---------------|
| Respiratory acidosis | ↓ | ↑ | Normal (or ↑ if compensating) | COPD, pneumonia, respiratory depression (opioids), airway obstruction, neuromuscular disease |
| Respiratory alkalosis | ↑ | ↓ | Normal (or ↓ if compensating) | Hyperventilation (anxiety, pain, fever), mechanical over-ventilation, PE, early salicylate poisoning |
| Metabolic acidosis | ↓ | Normal (or ↓ if compensating) | ↓ | DKA, lactic acidosis, renal failure, severe diarrhea (losing bicarb), salicylate poisoning |
| Metabolic alkalosis | ↑ | Normal (or ↑ if compensating) | ↑ | Vomiting, NG suction (losing HCl), excessive antacids, hypokalemia, diuretic use |

**Key Compensation Principle:** Lungs compensate fast (minutes to hours) by changing breathing rate. Kidneys compensate slowly (hours to days) by retaining or excreting HCO₃. So if you see metabolic acidosis with a low CO₂, the lungs are blowing off CO₂ to compensate (Kussmaul breathing in DKA is this — deep, rapid breathing is the lungs' attempt to lower CO₂ and raise pH).

**CONNECT:** → See Section 8: Respiratory Conditions, DKA · → See Section 5: Tubes (NG suction → metabolic alkalosis)

**TEST TIP:** They hand you blood gas numbers and expect you to interpret — so use ROME every single time. Practice this one: pH 7.28, CO₂ 55, HCO₃ 24. Walk it through — pH is acidotic (↓), CO₂ is acidotic (↑), HCO₃ is normal. Respiratory matches the pH direction (opposite). Uncompensated respiratory acidosis. ROME doesn't fail you if you actually use it.

**PRACTICE:**
A client's ABG results are: pH 7.50, PaCO₂ 48 mmHg, HCO₃ 36 mEq/L. How should the nurse interpret these results?

A. Respiratory alkalosis, uncompensated
B. Metabolic alkalosis, partially compensated
C. Respiratory acidosis, fully compensated
D. Mixed acid-base disorder

**Answer: B.** pH 7.50 = alkalotic (abnormal). HCO₃ 36 = elevated = metabolic alkalosis (equal — both pH and HCO₃ are up). CO₂ 48 = elevated = the lungs are retaining CO₂ to compensate (trying to add acid to bring pH down). Since pH is still abnormal, compensation is only partial. If pH were normal (7.35-7.45), it would be fully compensated.

---

## CHEST TUBES
**Reduction of Risk Potential** · **Exam Weight: 12%** · Connects to: → See Section 8: Respiratory Conditions · → See Section 5: Tubes/Drains

**WHAT:** Chest tubes drain air, blood, or fluid from the pleural space to restore negative pressure and allow lung re-expansion.

**WHY IT MATTERS:** Mismanagement of a chest tube system can cause tension pneumothorax (fatal) or allow the lung to re-collapse. NCLEX loves chest tube questions because they test critical thinking about closed-system mechanics.

**MECHANISM:** The pleural space normally has negative pressure, which keeps lungs expanded. When air (pneumothorax) or fluid (hemothorax, effusion) enters the pleural space, the lung collapses. The chest tube removes the air/fluid and the water-seal system prevents air from re-entering.

**RECOGNIZE — The Three-Chamber System:**
1. **Collection chamber:** Drainage collects here. Monitor amount, color, and rate. Mark levels hourly.
2. **Water-seal chamber:** Acts as a one-way valve. Contains 2 cm water.
   - Tidaling (fluid oscillates with breathing) = NORMAL — confirms the tube is patent and in the pleural space
   - Intermittent bubbling = NORMAL with pneumothorax (air is leaving the pleural space as intended)
   - Continuous bubbling = AIR LEAK — check all connections, check insertion site for subcutaneous emphysema, notify provider
   - No tidaling = may indicate lung re-expansion (good) OR tube occlusion/kink (bad) — assess the patient
3. **Suction control chamber:** Gentle, continuous bubbling = suction is at the prescribed level. Vigorous bubbling just means excess suction — doesn't improve drainage.

**ACT:**
1. Keep the system below chest level at all times (gravity drainage; prevents backflow)
2. Keep tubing free of kinks and dependent loops (fluid collects in loops and creates back-pressure)
3. NEVER clamp a chest tube (except momentarily when changing the system — clamping can cause tension pneumothorax)
4. If the system breaks or disconnects: submerge the tube end in sterile water (creates temporary water seal) — or, per some protocols, allow the tube to remain open briefly. DO NOT clamp.
5. If the tube accidentally pulls out: cover the site immediately with petroleum gauze (occlusive dressing taped on 3 sides — 3-sided allows air OUT but not IN, preventing tension pneumothorax)
6. Encourage deep breathing and coughing. Reposition frequently. Assess respiratory status continuously.
7. Prepare for removal: obtain CXR confirming lung re-expansion. Instruct patient to exhale and bear down (Valsalva) during removal. Apply occlusive dressing immediately.

**CONNECT:** → See Section 8: Pneumothorax, Hemothorax · → See Section 5: Tubes/Drains

**TEST TIP:** Three chest tube facts NCLEX tests repeatedly: (1) Continuous bubbling in the water-seal = air leak → report. (2) NEVER clamp a chest tube without a direct order. (3) If disconnected, submerge the end in sterile water. The trickiest question: "the chest tube has no tidaling." Ask yourself — has the lung re-expanded (check breath sounds, CXR) or is the tube blocked? Context determines the answer.

**PRACTICE:**
A nurse observes continuous bubbling in the water-seal chamber of a client's chest tube drainage system. What should the nurse do first?

A. Clamp the chest tube near the insertion site
B. Check all connections for looseness
C. Increase the suction level
D. Notify the provider immediately without assessment

**Answer: B.** Continuous bubbling = air leak. The first action is to systematically check the system from the patient outward — check all connections for tightness, check tubing for cracks, check the insertion site. If the leak persists after securing connections, then notify the provider. Never clamp as a first action (risk of tension pneumothorax). Increasing suction doesn't fix a leak.

---

## VENTILATOR MANAGEMENT
**Reduction of Risk Potential** · **Exam Weight: 12%** · Connects to: → See Section 8: Respiratory Conditions

**WHAT:** Monitoring patients on mechanical ventilation, responding to alarms, and preventing complications.

**RECOGNIZE — Alarm Interpretation:**

**High-Pressure Alarm** (pressure in circuit exceeds set limit):
- Causes: secretions in airway (most common), patient biting tube, bronchospasm, coughing, kinked tubing, displacement/migration of ET tube into right mainstem bronchus, pneumothorax, patient fighting the ventilator (dyssynchrony)
- Action: suction patient first (most likely cause), check tubing for kinks, assess tube placement, consider sedation if patient is fighting vent, listen for bilateral breath sounds

**Low-Pressure Alarm** (circuit pressure drops below baseline):
- Causes: disconnection from ventilator (most common), cuff leak on ET tube, leak in circuit/tubing
- Action: check connections first, assess cuff pressure, use manual resuscitation bag (Ambu bag) if patient is disconnected and reconnection takes time

**ACT — Ventilator Nursing Care:**
1. Verify settings match orders (mode, rate, FiO₂, tidal volume, PEEP)
2. HOB 30-45° at all times (prevents VAP)
3. Oral care with chlorhexidine every 2-4 hours (prevents VAP)
4. Suction PRN (when you hear secretions or high-pressure alarm) — not on a schedule
5. Monitor SpO₂ and ABGs per orders
6. Sedation vacation daily (per protocol) — assess readiness to wean
7. DVT prophylaxis and peptic ulcer prophylaxis (ventilated patients are at high risk for both)
8. Communicate with patient — they can't speak. Provide communication board, pen/paper, or picture board. Explain procedures. The patient is often alert and frightened.

**Weaning Criteria:** Patient must demonstrate: adequate oxygenation on low FiO₂ (<40%), hemodynamic stability, ability to breathe spontaneously, adequate cough/gag, alertness, and underlying condition resolving.

**CONNECT:** → See Section 8: Respiratory Failure, ARDS · → See Section 7: ABG Analysis

**TEST TIP:** "The ventilator alarm sounds. What does the nurse do first?" — depends on which alarm. High pressure → suction (clear the airway). Low pressure → check connections (find the disconnect). But here's the rule that overrides everything: if you can't fix the problem fast, disconnect the patient from the vent and bag them with an Ambu while you troubleshoot. Oxygenation first, machine second.

**PRACTICE:**
A mechanically ventilated patient's high-pressure alarm sounds. The nurse assesses coarse crackles over the trachea and the patient is coughing against the ventilator. Oxygen saturation is 89% and dropping. What should the nurse do first?
A. Increase the FiO₂ to 100%
B. Suction the endotracheal tube
C. Disconnect the patient and ventilate with an Ambu bag
D. Administer a prescribed PRN sedative

**Answer: B.** The high-pressure alarm + coarse crackles over the trachea = secretions obstructing the airway. Suctioning clears the obstruction — the cause. Increasing FiO₂ doesn't fix obstruction. Disconnecting to bag-ventilate is appropriate if you can't quickly identify the cause, but here the cause is clear. Sedation masks the problem. *Why B works:* Treat the identified cause of the alarm, not the downstream effect.

---
## EKG BASICS AND RHYTHM INTERPRETATION
**Reduction of Risk Potential** · **Exam Weight: 12%** · Connects to: → See Section 8: Cardiac Conditions

**WHAT:** Reading and interpreting cardiac rhythms to identify life-threatening arrhythmias and guide interventions.

**MECHANISM:** The cardiac conduction system: SA node (pacemaker, 60-100 bpm) → AV node (delays signal, 40-60 bpm backup rate) → Bundle of His → bundle branches → Purkinje fibers. Each part of the EKG tracing represents a phase: P wave (atrial depolarization), QRS complex (ventricular depolarization), T wave (ventricular repolarization).

**Heart Sound Auscultation Points:**
- **A**ortic: Right 2nd intercostal space (ICS), sternal border
- **P**ulmonic: Left 2nd ICS, sternal border
- **E**rb's Point: Left 3rd ICS, sternal border
- **T**ricuspid: Left lower sternal border, 4th ICS
- **M**itral: Left 5th ICS, midclavicular line (MCL)
- Memory aid: "**A**ll **P**atients **E**njoy **T**aking **M**edicine" (top to bottom)

**RECOGNIZE — Key Rhythms:**

| Rhythm | Rate | Regularity | P Waves | QRS | Clinical Significance |
|--------|------|-----------|---------|-----|----------------------|
| Normal Sinus (NSR) | 60-100 | Regular | Present, 1:1 with QRS | Narrow (<0.12s) | Normal — no intervention needed |
| Sinus Bradycardia | <60 | Regular | Present, 1:1 | Narrow | May be normal (athletes). Treat if symptomatic (atropine, pacing) |
| Sinus Tachycardia | >100 | Regular | Present, 1:1 | Narrow | Response to stimuli (fever, pain, anxiety, hypovolemia). Treat the CAUSE, not the rhythm |
| Atrial Fibrillation | Irregular (variable) | Irregularly irregular | No distinct P waves (fibrillatory baseline) | Narrow | Most common sustained arrhythmia. Risk: blood pools in atria → clots → stroke. Tx: rate control (beta-blockers/CCBs), anticoagulation, cardioversion |
| Atrial Flutter | Atrial: 250-350 | Regular or regularly irregular | "Sawtooth" pattern | Narrow | Similar to A-fib but organized. Tx: same principles |
| SVT | 150-250 | Regular | May be hidden in T waves | Narrow | Sudden onset/offset. Vagal maneuvers first (bear down, cold water on face). Adenosine (rapid IV push — warn patient of transient asystole/"impending doom" feeling) |
| PVCs | Varies | Irregular (premature beats) | None before PVC | Wide and bizarre | Occasional = often benign. Frequent, >6/min, couplets, runs, or multifocal = dangerous → may lead to V-tach. R-on-T phenomenon (PVC on T wave) → can trigger V-fib |
| Ventricular Tachycardia | 100-250 | Regular | Absent or dissociated | Wide | With pulse: stable → amiodarone or cardioversion; unstable → synchronized cardioversion. Pulseless → DEFIBRILLATE (treat as V-fib) |
| Ventricular Fibrillation | None (no organized rhythm) | Chaotic | None | No QRS — chaotic waves | NO cardiac output. IMMEDIATELY defibrillate + CPR. This is a shockable rhythm. |
| Asystole | None | Flat line | None | None | NO cardiac output. NOT shockable. CPR + epinephrine. Confirm in multiple leads (rule out loose lead). |
| Torsades de Pointes | Variable | Irregular | None distinct | Wide, "twisting" around baseline | Variant of V-Tach. Caused by prolonged QT interval. Tx: IV magnesium, overdrive pacing |

**Heart Blocks:**

| Block | PR Interval | Dropped Beats | Significance |
|-------|-------------|---------------|-------------|
| 1st Degree | Prolonged (>0.20s) but constant | None — all beats conducted | Usually benign. Monitor. |
| 2nd Degree Type I (Wenckebach) | Progressively lengthens until a QRS is dropped | Yes — cyclical pattern | Usually benign. Monitor. May resolve spontaneously. |
| 2nd Degree Type II (Mobitz II) | Fixed PR interval, but QRS randomly dropped | Yes — unpredictable | DANGEROUS. Can progress to 3rd degree without warning. Pacemaker needed. |
| 3rd Degree (Complete) | P and QRS completely independent | Atria and ventricles beat independently | DANGEROUS. No communication between atria and ventricles. Pacemaker required immediately. Wide QRS, slow ventricular rate. |

**Shockable vs. Non-Shockable Rhythms:**
- SHOCKABLE (defibrillation): V-Fib, Pulseless V-Tach
- NON-SHOCKABLE (CPR + epinephrine): Asystole, PEA (pulseless electrical activity)

**CONNECT:** → See Section 8: Cardiac Conditions (MI, HF) · → See Section 8: Electrolyte Imbalances (EKG changes)

**TEST TIP:** For rhythm identification on NCLEX: (1) Is there a pulse? No → start CPR. (2) Is it shockable? V-Fib or pulseless V-Tach → defibrillate. (3) Is it regular or irregular? Irregularly irregular = A-Fib. (4) Are P waves present? No = atrial origin problem. Wide QRS? = ventricular origin. NCLEX often shows you a rhythm strip and asks "what should the nurse do first?" — if there's no pulse, it's always CPR.

**PRACTICE:**
A nurse observes the following on a client's cardiac monitor: no discernible P waves, chaotic, irregular waveform, no identifiable QRS complexes. The client is unresponsive with no pulse. What should the nurse do first?

A. Administer atropine 1 mg IV push
B. Begin CPR and prepare for defibrillation
C. Administer epinephrine 1 mg IV push
D. Perform synchronized cardioversion

**Answer: B.** This is ventricular fibrillation (chaotic, no organized QRS, no pulse). V-fib is a shockable rhythm. Begin CPR immediately and prepare the defibrillator. Defibrillation is the definitive treatment. Epinephrine is given during CPR but after the first shock. Atropine is for bradycardia. Synchronized cardioversion is for organized rhythms with a pulse.

---

## DIAGNOSTIC TESTS AND PROCEDURES
**Reduction of Risk Potential** · **Exam Weight: 12%** · Connects to: → See Section 8: All organ system conditions

**WHAT:** Preparation and post-procedure care for common diagnostic tests. NCLEX focuses on what the nurse does BEFORE and AFTER, not the technical procedure itself.

**ACT — High-Yield Diagnostic Procedures:**

**Cardiac Catheterization:**
- Pre: assess allergies (contrast dye — iodine/shellfish allergy), mark pedal pulses, informed consent, NPO 6-8 hrs, assess renal function (contrast is nephrotoxic)
- Post (femoral approach): flat bedrest, affected leg straight and immobilized 4-6 hours. Assess insertion site for bleeding/hematoma every 15 min. Check distal pulses, circulation (warmth, color, sensation) of affected extremity. Monitor for retroperitoneal bleeding (back/flank pain, hypotension). FORCE FLUIDS to flush contrast dye and protect kidneys.
- Post (radial approach): pressure dressing or compression band. Can sit up sooner. Check radial pulse and hand perfusion.

**Lumbar Puncture (LP):**
- Pre: empty bladder. Position: side-lying with knees drawn up (fetal position) or sitting and leaning forward. This opens intervertebral spaces.
- Post: flat bed rest 4-8 hours (prevents spinal headache from CSF leak). Force fluids. Monitor for headache, neurological changes.
- Normal CSF: clear, colorless, glucose 60-80% of serum glucose, WBC <5, protein 15-45 mg/dL

**Paracentesis:**
- Pre: have patient void (prevent bladder puncture). Obtain baseline weight and abdominal girth.
- During: sitting position at edge of bed. Monitor vital signs.
- Post: monitor for hypovolemia (large fluid removal → fluid shift). Measure abdominal girth. Daily weights. Monitor albumin (protein lost in ascitic fluid).

**Thoracentesis:**
- Pre: position sitting upright, leaning forward on overbed table (spreads ribs for access).
- Post: CXR to check for pneumothorax. Monitor respiratory status. Assess insertion site for bleeding, crepitus (subcutaneous emphysema).

**Bronchoscopy:**
- Pre: NPO 6-8 hours. Remove dentures. Local anesthetic to throat → numbs gag reflex.
- Post: NPO until gag reflex returns (2-4 hours). Position semi-Fowler's. Monitor for laryngospasm, bronchospasm, bleeding, pneumothorax. Sore throat expected.

**Liver Biopsy:**
- Pre: verify normal clotting times (PT/INR). Position right side up, right arm over head.
- Post: position right side-lying with pillow under puncture site for 2 hours (applies pressure via body weight). Monitor for hemorrhage (liver is very vascular), peritonitis.

**Colonoscopy:**
- Pre: bowel prep (clear liquid diet + prep solution). Verify completion of prep.
- Post: monitor for perforation (abdominal distension, rigid abdomen, fever). Mild bloating and gas are normal. Resume diet after gag reflex returns if sedated.

**Contrast Dye Studies (CT with contrast, IVP, angiography):**
- Key risk: allergic reaction to iodine-based contrast and nephrotoxicity
- Pre: assess iodine/shellfish allergy, hold metformin 48 hours before AND after (risk of lactic acidosis with contrast + metformin + renal stress). Verify renal function (BUN/creatinine).
- Post: force fluids (2-3 L in 24 hours) to flush dye and protect kidneys. Monitor urine output. Resume metformin only after renal function confirmed normal at 48 hours.

**CONNECT:** → See Section 8: Specific organ conditions for why each test is ordered

**TEST TIP:** For procedure questions, focus on SAFETY: What can go wrong? How do you monitor for it? Three post-procedure priorities tested most: (1) Cardiac cath — check pulses and bleeding at site. (2) LP — flat bed rest and headache. (3) Liver biopsy — right side-lying and bleeding. Also remember: any procedure involving sedation → check gag reflex before oral intake.

**PRACTICE:**
A patient returns from a cardiac catheterization via the right femoral artery. One hour later, the nurse notes the right foot is cool and pale with a faintly palpable dorsalis pedis pulse. The left foot is warm and pink with a strong pulse. What is the nurse's priority action?
A. Elevate the right leg on two pillows
B. Apply warm blankets to both feet
C. Notify the health care provider immediately
D. Document the finding and reassess in 30 minutes

**Answer: C.** Cool, pale extremity with diminished pulse distal to the catheterization site indicates possible arterial occlusion from thrombus or hematoma — a limb-threatening emergency requiring immediate intervention. Elevation would further reduce arterial flow. Warming blankets mask the assessment finding. Waiting 30 minutes risks irreversible ischemia. *Why C works:* Asymmetric perfusion findings post-cath = vascular complication until proven otherwise.

---
## HEMODYNAMIC MONITORING
**Reduction of Risk Potential** · **Exam Weight: 12%** · Connects to: → See Section 8: Heart Failure, Shock

**WHAT:** Monitoring internal devices including arterial lines, central venous pressure (CVP), pulmonary artery catheters, and intracranial pressure monitors (NEW 2026 emphasis).

**RECOGNIZE — Key Values:**

| Parameter | Normal | Clinical Significance |
|-----------|--------|----------------------|
| CVP (Central Venous Pressure) | 2-8 mmHg | Reflects right heart preload. ↑CVP: fluid overload, right HF. ↓CVP: hypovolemia. |
| Pulmonary Artery Wedge Pressure (PAWP) | 8-12 mmHg | Reflects left heart preload. ↑PAWP: left HF, fluid overload. ↓PAWP: hypovolemia. |
| ICP (Intracranial Pressure) | 5-15 mmHg | >20 mmHg = elevated ICP → emergency. See Section 8: Neurological |
| Intrauterine Pressure Catheter (IUPC) | Resting tone 5-15 mmHg | Monitors contraction strength (NEW 2026). MVUs >200 Montevideo units = adequate labor. |

**ACT — Arterial Line Nursing Care:**
- Zero and level the transducer at the phlebostatic axis (4th ICS, mid-axillary line) every shift and with position changes
- Maintain continuous flush (pressurized NS at 300 mmHg)
- Never draw blood or infuse meds through a PA catheter
- Monitor insertion site for infection
- Set alarm limits appropriately — don't silence alarms
- If line disconnects: apply direct pressure immediately (arterial pressure = rapid blood loss)

**ICP Monitoring (NEW 2026 emphasis):**
- Normal ICP: 5-15 mmHg. Sustained >20 mmHg = notify provider
- Cerebral Perfusion Pressure (CPP) = MAP - ICP. Target CPP >60-70 mmHg
- Interventions for elevated ICP: HOB 30°, head midline, avoid hip flexion, hyperventilation (short-term to blow off CO₂ → cerebral vasoconstriction), mannitol (osmotic diuretic), hypertonic saline, CSF drainage via ventriculostomy
- Monitor for Cushing's triad (late sign of herniation): HTN with widening pulse pressure, bradycardia, irregular respirations — this is a neurological EMERGENCY

**CONNECT:** → See Section 8: Heart Failure, Shock, ICP/Neurological · → See Section 7: Lab Values

**TEST TIP:** The 2026 test plan specifically added monitoring of internal devices — ICP monitors, IUPCs — as a distinct activity. Translation: expect questions on what the nurse monitors, what's normal, and what demands immediate action. For hemodynamic monitoring, the high-yield concept is using the numbers to distinguish types of shock — CVP/PAWP low in hypovolemic vs. CVP/PAWP high in cardiogenic.

**PRACTICE:**
A patient in the ICU has the following hemodynamic values: CVP 2 mmHg (low), PAWP 4 mmHg (low), CO 3.2 L/min (low), SVR 1800 dynes (high). Which type of shock is most consistent with these findings?
A. Cardiogenic shock
B. Septic shock (warm phase)
C. Hypovolemic shock
D. Neurogenic shock

**Answer: C.** Low preload (CVP and PAWP both low) + low cardiac output + high SVR = classic hypovolemic shock. The body is vasoconstricting (high SVR) to compensate for low volume. Cardiogenic would show high CVP/PAWP (fluid backing up behind a failing heart). Warm septic shock shows low SVR (vasodilation). Neurogenic shows low SVR (loss of sympathetic tone). *Why C works:* The numbers tell a story — low in = low out, and the body is squeezing to compensate.

---

# SECTION 8: PHYSIOLOGICAL ADAPTATION

This is where it all converges — the section where your patient is actually sick and you need to know what's happening, what's coming next, and what to do about it. Cardiac, respiratory, neuro, endocrine, renal, GI, hematologic — every major system failure lives here. It's the densest section in the guide, and NCLEX pulls heavily from it.

**Exam Weight: 11–17% (midpoint 14%)** · The clinical heavyweight. This is where pathophysiology lives — every major body system, every critical condition, and the nursing interventions that keep patients alive. If Section 7 is about catching problems, Section 8 is about managing them.

---

## ELECTROLYTE IMBALANCES — A CONNECTED SYSTEM
**Physiological Adaptation** · **Exam Weight: 14%** · Connects to: → See Section 7: Lab Values · → See Section 6: IV Therapy

**WHAT:** Electrolytes regulate fluid balance, nerve transmission, muscle contraction, and cardiac rhythm. These 12 imbalances don't exist in isolation — they form a connected web where fixing one often requires addressing another.

**KEY RELATIONSHIPS (learn these first — they unlock the whole system):**
- **Phosphorus and calcium are INVERSE:** ↑Phosphorus → ↓Calcium (and vice versa). *Why?* They bind together — when one rises, it pulls the other down. This is why renal failure (can't excrete phosphorus → ↑phos) causes hypocalcemia.
