## CARD 7: INSULIN

### Types, Onset, Peak, Duration

| Type | Examples | Onset | Peak | Duration | Appearance |
|------|----------|-------|------|----------|------------|
| **Rapid-acting** | Lispro (Humalog), Aspart (NovoLog), Glulisine (Apidra) | <15 min | 30–60 min | 3 hrs | Clear |
| **Short-acting (Regular)** | Humulin R, Novolin R | 1 hr | 2 hrs | 4 hrs | Clear |
| **Intermediate (NPH)** | Humulin N, Novolin N | 4 hrs | 8 hrs | 12 hrs | **Cloudy** |
| **Long-acting** | Glargine (Lantus), Detemir (Levemir) | Slow (1–2 hrs) | **No peak** | 24 hrs | Clear |

### Critical Rules

- **Only Regular insulin can be given IV** — all others are SubQ only
- **Draw up clear before cloudy** (Regular before NPH) when mixing
- **Lantus (glargine): NEVER mix** with any other insulin
- **Rotate injection sites** — abdomen absorbs fastest → arms → thighs → buttocks
- **Hypoglycemia risk:** Greatest at peak time. Teach patients to eat at peak times.
- **Before meals:** Rapid-acting = give immediately before eating; Regular = give 30 min before
- **NPH + Regular:** Most common prescribed combination. Can be mixed in same syringe.

### Hypoglycemia vs. Hyperglycemia

| | **Hypoglycemia (<70 mg/dL)** | **Hyperglycemia (>300 mg/dL)** |
|---|---|---|
| Onset | Rapid (minutes) | Gradual (hours to days) |
| Signs | Cold, clammy, shaky, tachycardia, irritable, confused, hungry | Hot, dry, flushed, Kussmaul breathing, fruity breath, polyuria, polydipsia |
| Treatment | Conscious: 15g fast-acting carbs (4 oz juice, glucose tabs), recheck in 15 min · Unconscious: glucagon IM or D50 IV | Fluids, regular insulin, electrolyte monitoring, treat cause |
| Memory | "Cold and clammy, give some candy" | "Hot and dry, sugar high" |

→ See Section 6: Pharmacological Therapies and Section 8: Physiological Adaptation (DKA/HHS)

## CARD 8: CONVERSIONS & VITAL SIGNS

### Common Conversions

| Conversion | Value |
|------------|-------|
| 1 tsp | 5 mL |
| 1 tbsp | 15 mL |
| 1 oz | 30 mL |
| 1 cup | 8 oz = 240 mL |
| 1 pint | 2 cups = 16 oz = 480 mL |
| 1 quart | 2 pints = 32 oz = 960 mL |
| 1 kg | 2.2 lbs |
| 1 grain | 60 mg |
| °F → °C | (°F − 32) ÷ 1.8 |
| °C → °F | (°C × 1.8) + 32 |
| 1 g water | ≈ 1 mL (for diaper weights) |

### Vital Signs — Normal Adult

| Parameter | Normal Range | Concern |
|-----------|-------------|---------|
| **Temperature** | 98.6°F ± 1° (37°C ± 0.5°) | >100.4°F (38°C) = fever · >104°F (40°C) = hyperthermia emergency |
| **Heart Rate** | 60–100 bpm | <60 bradycardia · >100 tachycardia |
| **Respiratory Rate** | 12–20/min | <12 bradypnea (think opioids, ↑ICP) · >20 tachypnea |
| **Blood Pressure** | <120/80 mmHg | >130/80 Stage 1 HTN · >180/120 hypertensive crisis |
| **O₂ Saturation** | 95–100% | <94% supplement O₂ · <90% hypoxemia |
| **MAP** | 70–105 mmHg | Formula: (SBP + 2×DBP) ÷ 3 · <65 = organ hypoperfusion |
| **CVP** | 2–8 mmHg | ↑ Fluid overload, right HF · ↓ Hypovolemia |

### Vital Signs — Pediatric (Resting)

| Age | HR | RR | BP (systolic) |
|-----|-----|-----|---------------|
| Newborn | 120–160 | 30–60 | 60–80 |
| Infant (1 yr) | 100–150 | 24–40 | 80–100 |
| Toddler (2 yr) | 80–130 | 22–34 | 80–110 |
| Preschool (4 yr) | 80–120 | 20–30 | 80–110 |
| School age (8 yr) | 70–110 | 16–22 | 85–120 |
| Adolescent | 60–100 | 12–20 | 90–130 |

### Vital Signs — Obstetric

| Parameter | Normal |
|-----------|--------|
| **Fetal Heart Rate** | 110–160 bpm |
| FHR < 110 | Bradycardia — reposition, O₂, stop Pitocin, notify provider |
| FHR > 160 | Tachycardia — assess for fever, dehydration, meds |

## CARD 9: INFECTION CONTROL

### Standard Precautions (ALL patients, ALL the time)
- Hand hygiene (soap & water or alcohol-based if hands not visibly soiled)
- Gloves for body fluid contact
- Gown if splash/spray expected
- Mask + eye protection if splash/spray to face expected
- Sharps disposal in puncture-resistant containers
- Safe injection practices
- Respiratory hygiene/cough etiquette

### Transmission-Based Precautions

| Type | **Airborne** | **Droplet** | **Contact** |
|------|------------|-----------|-----------|
| **Room** | Private, negative pressure, door closed | Private or cohort, door can be open | Private or cohort |
| **Mask** | **N95 respirator** (or PAPR) — fit tested | Surgical mask within 3 feet (6 feet per some guidelines) | Standard mask for splash risk only |
| **Patient Transport** | Patient wears surgical mask | Patient wears surgical mask | Limit transport; cover wounds |
| **Key Organisms** | **M**easles, **T**uberculosis, **V**aricella (chickenpox), disseminated **Z**oster | **S**trep (GAS), **P**ertussis, **I**nfluenza, **D**iphtheria, **M**eningococcal meningitis, **M**umps, **R**ubella, **P**neumonia (some), COVID-19 | **MRSA**, **VRE**, **C. diff**, **Scabies**, **RSV**, **Herpes zoster** (localized), **Impetigo**, **Lice**, **Rotavirus** |
| **Mnemonic** | **"MTV"** + Zoster (disseminated) | **"SPIDERMAN"** | Think: things that live on surfaces/skin |

### PPE Don/Doff Order

| **Donning (putting ON)** | **Doffing (taking OFF)** |
|---|---|
| 1. Hand hygiene | 1. **Gloves** (most contaminated — remove first) |
| 2. Gown | 2. Goggles/face shield |
| 3. Mask/respirator | 3. Gown (pull from shoulders, roll inside out) |
| 4. Goggles/face shield | 4. Hand hygiene |
| 5. Gloves (last on) | 5. Mask/respirator (remove by straps — don't touch front) |
| | 6. **Hand hygiene** (always last) |

**Memory: "Go go, go go" — Gown-Goggles-Gloves on; Gloves-Goggles-Gown off** (simplified)

### Sterile Technique Key Rules
- 1-inch border of sterile field is contaminated
- Below waist level = contaminated
- Wet/damp = contaminated
- If you turn your back on it = contaminated
- Open sterile packages away from you (flap farthest from you opens first)
- Pour solutions from 6 inches above the field
- If in doubt, consider it contaminated

→ See Section 2: Safety & Infection Control for full details

---

## CARD 10: DELEGATION

### Scope of Practice

| **RN** | **LPN/LVN** | **UAP/CNA** |
|--------|-------------|-------------|
| Assessment (comprehensive & focused) | Focused assessment on STABLE patients | Vital signs on stable patients |
| Nursing diagnosis & care planning | Reinforce (not initiate) teaching | Bathing, grooming, dressing |
| Teaching (initial & complex) | Administer PO, IM, SubQ meds | Feeding (non-complex) |
| Evaluation of outcomes | Sterile dressing changes | Ambulation (stable patients) |
| IV push medications | Monitor IV flow rate (NOT initiate) | Intake & output measurement |
| Blood product administration | Urinary catheterization (stable) | Specimen collection |
| Unstable/complex patients | Stable, predictable patients | Routine, standard tasks |
| Triage & prioritization | Trach suctioning (established) | Turning & repositioning |
| Telephone orders from provider | Report changes to RN | Report observations to nurse |

### Five Rights of Delegation

1. **Right Task** — Can this task be delegated? (Is it within their scope?)
2. **Right Circumstance** — Is the patient stable enough? Is the situation predictable?
3. **Right Person** — Does this person have the training and competency?
4. **Right Direction** — Did you give clear, specific instructions?
5. **Right Supervision** — Are you available for questions, and will you follow up?

### Delegation Red Flags (NEVER Delegate)

- Assessment, evaluation, or clinical judgment to non-RN
- Unstable or unpredictable patient care to LPN or UAP
- Patient teaching (initial) to LPN or UAP
- Blood administration to LPN
- IV push medications to LPN
- Anything the person hasn't been trained to do

→ See Section 1: Management of Care for detailed delegation scenarios

---

## CARD 11: COMMON SIGNS & SYMPTOMS

| Condition | Hallmark Sign/Symptom |
|-----------|----------------------|
| Addison's disease | Bronze skin, hypotension, hyperkalemia, salt craving |
| Cushing's syndrome | Moon face, buffalo hump, truncal obesity, hyperglycemia, purple striae |
| Hypothyroidism | Cold intolerance, weight gain, bradycardia, fatigue, constipation |
| Hyperthyroidism | Heat intolerance, weight loss, tachycardia, exophthalmos, tremor |
| DKA | Kussmaul breathing, fruity breath, dehydration, hyperglycemia >300 |
| HHS | Extreme hyperglycemia (>600), no ketones, severe dehydration, altered LOC |
| Heart failure (left) | Dyspnea, crackles, orthopnea, pink frothy sputum, S3 gallop |
| Heart failure (right) | JVD, peripheral edema, hepatomegaly, weight gain, ascites |
| MI | Crushing chest pain radiating to jaw/left arm, diaphoresis, N/V |
| PE (pulmonary embolism) | Sudden dyspnea, pleuritic chest pain, tachycardia, anxiety |
| DVT | Unilateral leg swelling, warmth, redness, positive Homans' (unreliable but tested) |
| Pneumothorax | Sudden pleuritic chest pain, absent breath sounds on affected side, tracheal deviation (tension) |
| Pancreatitis | Epigastric pain radiating to back, elevated amylase/lipase, Turner/Cullen sign |
| Appendicitis | RLQ pain (McBurney's point), rebound tenderness, N/V, low-grade fever |
| Meningitis | Nuchal rigidity, Kernig's sign, Brudzinski's sign, photophobia, fever |
| ↑ ICP | Cushing's triad (HTN, bradycardia, irregular respirations), altered LOC, projectile vomiting |
| Myasthenia gravis | Progressive muscle weakness improving with rest, ptosis, diplopia |
| Multiple sclerosis | Relapsing-remitting weakness, visual changes, intention tremor, Uhthoff's phenomenon (heat worsens) |
| Parkinson's disease | Resting tremor ("pill rolling"), bradykinesia, rigidity, shuffling gait |
| Guillain-Barré | Ascending paralysis (starts in legs), follows URI/GI infection |
| Pyloric stenosis | Projectile vomiting (non-bilious), olive-shaped mass, visible peristalsis, hungry infant |
| Intussusception | Currant jelly stools, sausage-shaped mass, colicky pain in infant |
| Epiglottitis | Tripod position, drooling, dysphagia, "cherry red" epiglottis, NO tongue blade |
| Croup | Barking (seal-like) cough, stridor, steeple sign on X-ray |
| Kawasaki disease | Strawberry tongue, high fever >5 days, conjunctivitis, desquamation of hands/feet |
| Tetralogy of Fallot | Tet spells (cyanosis), squatting position, boot-shaped heart on X-ray |
| Preeclampsia | HTN (>140/90 after 20 wks), proteinuria, edema, headache, visual changes |
| Eclampsia | Seizures in preeclamptic patient |
| Abruptio placentae | Painful, dark red vaginal bleeding, rigid/board-like abdomen, fetal distress |
| Placenta previa | Painless, bright red vaginal bleeding (no vaginal exam until ultrasound) |
| Pheochromocytoma | Severe episodic HTN, headache, diaphoresis, tachycardia |

→ See Sections 3–8 for full condition details

---

## CARD 12: THERAPEUTIC DIETS

| Condition | Diet | Key Points |
|-----------|------|------------|
| **Heart failure / HTN** | Low sodium (<2g/day) | No canned soups, processed meats, chips; read all labels |
| **Renal failure (pre-dialysis)** | Low protein, low K⁺, low Na⁺, low PO₄, fluid restriction | Limit bananas, oranges, tomatoes, potatoes |
| **Renal failure (on dialysis)** | Higher protein, low K⁺, low PO₄, fluid restriction | Protein needs increase with dialysis |
| **Celiac disease** | Gluten-free | No wheat, rye, barley, oats (unless certified); rice/corn OK |
| **Crohn's / IBD (flare)** | Low residue/low fiber | No raw fruits/vegetables, whole grains, seeds, nuts |
| **Diverticulitis (acute)** | Clear liquids → low fiber | High fiber only when NOT in acute phase |
| **Diverticulosis (maintenance)** | High fiber | Prevent flares with fiber + fluids |
| **GERD / Hiatal hernia** | Small frequent meals, avoid spicy/acidic/fatty, no eating 3 hrs before bed | Elevate HOB |
| **Hepatic encephalopathy** | Low protein | Reduces ammonia production |
| **Cirrhosis** | Low sodium, possibly low protein | Depends on ammonia levels |
| **Dumping syndrome** | Low carb, high protein/fat, small frequent meals, no fluids with meals | Lie down 20–30 min after eating |
| **Pancreatitis (acute)** | NPO → clear liquids → low fat | No alcohol ever |
| **Cholecystitis** | Low fat | Avoid fried foods, dairy fat |
| **DM Type 1** | Consistent carb counting, regular meals | Match insulin to carbs |
| **DM Type 2** | Balanced, calorie-controlled, limit refined carbs | Weight management key |
| **Gout** | Low purine | Avoid organ meats, shellfish, beer, red meat |
| **Osteoporosis** | High calcium, vitamin D | Dairy, fortified foods, weight-bearing exercise |
| **Iron-deficiency anemia** | High iron + vitamin C (enhances absorption) | Avoid calcium/antacids with iron (inhibit absorption) |
| **PKU** | Low phenylalanine | No aspartame (NutraSweet), limited protein |
| **Nephrotic syndrome** | Low sodium, adequate protein | Monitor albumin, manage edema |
| **Burns (major)** | High calorie, high protein | Metabolic demands massively increased |
| **Post-tonsillectomy** | Clear cool liquids → soft foods | NO red/brown fluids (mask bleeding), no straws, no crunchy foods |
| **Cleft palate (infant)** | Breck feeder, slow flow, upright position | Burp frequently, assess for aspiration |
| **MAO inhibitor therapy** | No tyramine | No aged cheese, wine, beer, smoked meats, soy sauce, avocados, bananas |
| **Warfarin therapy** | Consistent vitamin K intake | Don't eliminate green leafy vegetables — keep intake consistent |
| **Potassium-wasting diuretics** | Increase K⁺ foods | Bananas, oranges, potatoes, avocados, spinach |

→ See Section 5: Basic Care & Comfort for nutrition details

---

## CARD 13: DRUG SUFFIXES

| Suffix | Drug Class | Key Nursing Implication |
|--------|-----------|------------------------|
| **-olol** | Beta blockers (atenolol, metoprolol, propranolol) | Monitor HR (hold if <60), BP, mask hypoglycemia signs in diabetics |
| **-pril** | ACE inhibitors (lisinopril, enalapril, captopril) | Dry cough (common SE), monitor K⁺ (↑), monitor renal function, angioedema risk |
| **-sartan** | ARBs (losartan, valsartan, irbesartan) | Similar to ACE inhibitors but NO cough. Monitor K⁺ and renal function |
| **-dipine** | Calcium channel blockers — dihydropyridines (amlodipine, nifedipine) | Peripheral vasodilation, edema, reflex tachycardia, avoid grapefruit |
| **-statin** | HMG-CoA reductase inhibitors (atorvastatin, simvastatin) | Take at bedtime (peak cholesterol synthesis at night), monitor LFTs, report muscle pain (rhabdomyolysis) |
| **-prazole** | Proton pump inhibitors (omeprazole, pantoprazole) | Take 30 min before meals, long-term use → ↓ Mg²⁺, Ca²⁺ absorption, fracture risk |
| **-tidine** | H2 blockers (famotidine, ranitidine) | Less potent than PPIs, fewer long-term risks |
| **-azepam / -zolam** | Benzodiazepines (diazepam, lorazepam, alprazolam, midazolam) | CNS depression, fall risk, respiratory depression, antidote = flumazenil |
| **-triptan** | Serotonin receptor agonists (sumatriptan) | For migraines, contraindicated with MAOIs, serotonin syndrome risk |
| **-floxacin** | Fluoroquinolones (ciprofloxacin, levofloxacin) | Tendon rupture risk, photosensitivity, avoid in pregnancy/children, no antacids within 2 hrs |
| **-mycin / -micin** | Aminoglycosides (gentamicin, tobramycin, amikacin) | Nephrotoxicity + ototoxicity, monitor trough levels, peak/trough timing |
| **-cillin** | Penicillins (amoxicillin, ampicillin) | Allergy/anaphylaxis risk, cross-reactivity with cephalosporins (slight) |
| **-cephalosporin → -cef** | Cephalosporins (cefazolin, ceftriaxone, cephalexin) | Ask about penicillin allergy, avoid alcohol (disulfiram reaction with some) |
| **-vir** | Antivirals (acyclovir, valacyclovir, oseltamivir) | Hydration important (renal clearance), start early for effectiveness |
| **-azole** | Antifungals (fluconazole, ketoconazole) | Hepatotoxicity, drug interactions (CYP450), monitor LFTs |
| **-sone / -lone / -olone** | Corticosteroids (prednisone, prednisolone, methylprednisolone) | Hyperglycemia, immunosuppression, don't stop abruptly (taper), Cushing's with long-term use |
| **-lam / -pam** | Anxiolytics/Sedatives | See benzodiazepines above |
| **-pine** | Atypical antipsychotics (olanzapine, quetiapine, clozapine) | Metabolic syndrome, weight gain, clozapine → agranulocytosis (monitor WBC) |
| **-done** | Opioids (methadone, hydrocodone, oxycodone) | Respiratory depression, constipation, antidote = naloxone |
| **-parin** | Anticoagulants — heparins (heparin, enoxaparin) | Monitor aPTT (unfractionated), no aPTT needed for LMWH, antidote = protamine |
| **-arin** | Anticoagulants — warfarin | Monitor PT/INR, antidote = vitamin K, consistent vitamin K diet |
| **-mab** | Monoclonal antibodies (rituximab, trastuzumab, infliximab) | Infusion reactions, immunosuppression, biologic — store properly |
| **-nib** | Kinase inhibitors (imatinib, erlotinib) | Oral chemo agents, monitor CBC, LFTs |
| **-ase** | Thrombolytics (alteplase, tenecteplase) | Dissolve clots, major bleeding risk, strict time windows, neuro checks q15min |

→ See Section 6: Pharmacological Therapies for complete drug profiles

---

## CARD 14: CARDIAC RHYTHMS

| Rhythm | Identification | Treatment |
|--------|---------------|-----------|
| **Normal Sinus Rhythm** | Regular, rate 60–100, P before every QRS | None needed |
| **Sinus Bradycardia** | Regular, rate <60, normal P waves | Atropine if symptomatic; pacemaker if persistent |
| **Sinus Tachycardia** | Regular, rate >100, normal P waves | Treat underlying cause (pain, fever, hypovolemia, anxiety) |
| **Atrial Fibrillation (A-fib)** | Irregularly irregular, no identifiable P waves, fibrillatory baseline | Rate control (diltiazem, beta blockers), anticoagulation (stroke risk), cardioversion |
| **Atrial Flutter** | Regular or irregular, "sawtooth" P waves, usually 300 atrial rate with block | Rate control, cardioversion, ablation |
| **SVT** | Very fast (150–250), regular, narrow QRS, P waves hidden | Vagal maneuvers → adenosine (rapid IV push + flush) → cardioversion |
| **1st Degree AV Block** | Regular, PR interval >0.20 sec, all P waves conducted | Usually benign, monitor |
| **2nd Degree Type I (Wenckebach)** | Progressive PR prolongation → dropped QRS → cycle repeats | Usually benign, monitor. Atropine if symptomatic |
| **2nd Degree Type II** | Constant PR interval with randomly dropped QRS | More serious. Pacemaker likely needed. Atropine, transcutaneous pacing |
| **3rd Degree (Complete) Heart Block** | P waves and QRS completely independent, regular but dissociated | Pacemaker required. Transcutaneous pacing as bridge. Do NOT give atropine for wide QRS |
| **PVC** | Wide, bizarre QRS without preceding P wave | Occasional: monitor · Frequent/symptomatic: amiodarone, lidocaine · >6/min, couplets, R-on-T: danger |
| **V-Tach (with pulse)** | Wide QRS, regular, rate 100–250, no P waves | Amiodarone or lidocaine, synchronized cardioversion |
| **V-Tach (pulseless)** | Same appearance, no pulse | **CPR + defibrillation** (shockable rhythm), epinephrine, amiodarone |
| **V-Fib** | Chaotic, no identifiable waves | **CPR + defibrillation** (shockable rhythm), epinephrine, amiodarone |
| **Asystole** | Flat line (confirm in 2 leads) | CPR, epinephrine — **NOT shockable**. Confirm in 2 leads (rule out loose lead) |
| **PEA** | Organized rhythm on monitor but NO pulse | CPR, epinephrine — **NOT shockable**. Treat H's & T's |
| **Torsades de Pointes** | Twisting QRS around baseline, polymorphic V-tach | IV magnesium, overdrive pacing, defibrillation if pulseless |

### Key EKG Intervals
- **PR interval:** 0.12–0.20 sec (3–5 small boxes)
- **QRS complex:** 0.06–0.10 sec (<3 small boxes = narrow = supraventricular origin)
- **QT interval:** <0.44 sec (prolonged QT → torsades risk)

→ See Section 8: Physiological Adaptation for full cardiac content

---

## CARD 15: STAGES OF LABOR & FETAL MONITORING

### Stages of Labor

| Stage | Phase | Dilation | Contractions | Duration | Nursing Care |
|-------|-------|----------|-------------|----------|-------------|
| **1st** | **Latent** | 0–6 cm | Mild, q5–30 min, 30–45 sec | Longest phase (varies widely) | Encourage ambulation, hydration, comfort measures, may go home if early |
| **1st** | **Active** | 6–8 cm | Moderate, q3–5 min, 40–60 sec | Average 3–6 hrs (nullipara) | Breathing techniques, positioning, pain management options, monitor FHR |
| **1st** | **Transition** | 8–10 cm | Strong, q2–3 min, 60–90 sec | 30 min – 2 hrs | Most intense. N/V common. Provide constant support. "I can't do this" is normal. |
| **2nd** | Pushing | 10 cm (complete) | Urge to push, q2–3 min | Up to 2 hrs (nullipara) | Coach pushing with contractions, monitor FHR with each push, position changes |
| **3rd** | Placenta delivery | — | — | 5–30 min | Assess placenta for completeness (3 vessels: 2 arteries, 1 vein), fundal massage |
| **4th** | Recovery | — | — | 1–2 hrs postpartum | VS q15min × 1hr, assess fundus (firm, midline, at/below umbilicus), lochia, bonding |

### VEAL CHOP — Fetal Heart Rate Patterns

| FHR Pattern | Cause | Memory |
|-------------|-------|--------|
| **V**ariable decelerations | **C**ord compression | Abrupt onset/offset, "V" shape. Reposition mother, check for prolapsed cord |
| **E**arly decelerations | **H**ead compression | Mirror contractions. Normal. No intervention needed |
| **A**ccelerations | **O**K (oxygenated) | Reassuring sign. Rise ≥15 bpm × ≥15 sec |
| **L**ate decelerations | **P**lacental insufficiency | Begin after contraction peaks, slow return. **CONCERNING** → reposition left side, O₂, stop Pitocin, IV fluids, notify provider |

### Fetal Heart Rate Essentials
- **Normal FHR:** 110–160 bpm
- **Moderate variability:** Reassuring (fluctuation 6–25 bpm)
- **Absent/minimal variability:** Concerning — may indicate fetal distress or sleep cycle
- **Prolonged deceleration:** FHR <110 for >2 min. Emergency — prepare for delivery

### Interventions for Non-Reassuring FHR
1. Reposition to left lateral (improves placental perfusion)
2. Administer O₂ via non-rebreather mask at 8–10 L/min
3. Stop oxytocin (Pitocin) if running
4. Increase IV fluids
5. Notify provider
6. Prepare for possible emergency cesarean delivery

→ See Section 3: Health Promotion & Maintenance for complete OB content

---

## CARD 16: EMERGENCY REFERENCE

### H's and T's (Reversible Causes of Cardiac Arrest)

| **H's** | **T's** |
|---------|---------|
| **H**ypovolemia | **T**ension pneumothorax |
| **H**ypoxia | **T**amponade (cardiac) |
| **H**ydrogen ion (acidosis) | **T**oxins (drug OD) |
| **H**ypo/Hyperkalemia | **T**hrombosis (coronary — MI) |
| **H**ypothermia | **T**hrombosis (pulmonary — PE) |

### Types of Shock

| Type | Cause | Signs | Priority Treatment |
|------|-------|-------|-------------------|
| **Hypovolemic** | Blood/fluid loss (hemorrhage, burns, dehydration) | ↓ BP, ↑ HR, ↓ CVP, cool/pale/clammy, ↓ UO | **Volume replacement** — NS, LR, blood products. Stop bleeding. |
| **Cardiogenic** | Pump failure (MI, severe HF, tamponade) | ↓ BP, ↑ HR, ↑ CVP/JVD, crackles, dyspnea | Treat cause. Vasopressors, inotropes (dobutamine). IABP. Avoid fluid overload. |
| **Distributive — Septic** | Widespread infection → vasodilation | EARLY: warm, flushed (warm shock) · LATE: cool, mottled (cold shock) | **Sepsis bundle:** cultures, antibiotics within 1 hr, 30 mL/kg crystalloid, vasopressors (norepinephrine), lactate monitoring |
| **Distributive — Anaphylactic** | Severe allergic reaction → vasodilation + airway edema | Urticaria, bronchospasm, angioedema, ↓ BP, stridor | **Epinephrine IM** (first-line), airway management, IV fluids, diphenhydramine, steroids |
| **Distributive — Neurogenic** | Loss of sympathetic tone (spinal cord injury) | ↓ BP, **↓ HR** (bradycardia — unique), warm/dry below level of injury | IV fluids, vasopressors (phenylephrine), atropine for bradycardia |
| **Obstructive** | Physical obstruction (PE, tension pneumo, tamponade) | ↓ BP, ↑ HR, JVD, muffled heart sounds (tamponade) | Treat obstruction: needle decompression (pneumo), pericardiocentesis (tamponade), thrombolytics (PE) |

### CPR Basics (Current AHA Guidelines)

| Component | Adult | Child (1–puberty) | Infant (<1 yr) |
|-----------|-------|-------------------|-----------------|
| **Compression depth** | ≥2 inches (5 cm) | ~2 inches (5 cm) | 1.5 inches (4 cm) |
| **Compression rate** | 100–120/min | 100–120/min | 100–120/min |
| **C:V ratio (1 rescuer)** | 30:2 | 30:2 | 30:2 |
| **C:V ratio (2 rescuers)** | 30:2 | 15:2 | 15:2 |
| **Hand placement** | 2 hands, lower half of sternum | 1 or 2 hands, lower half of sternum | 2 fingers or thumb-encircling technique |
| **AED** | Use immediately | Use pediatric pads/dose attenuator if available | Use pediatric system if available |

### Rapid Response / Code Blue Triggers

Activate rapid response for: RR <10 or >30, HR <40 or >130, SBP <90, O₂ Sat <90% despite O₂, acute mental status change, new-onset chest pain, seizure, acute significant bleeding, staff "gut feeling" that something is wrong.

### Emergency Medication Quick Reference

| Medication | Use | Key Points |
|------------|-----|------------|
| **Epinephrine** | Cardiac arrest, anaphylaxis | ACLS: 1 mg IV q3–5 min · Anaphylaxis: 0.3–0.5 mg IM (auto-injector) |
| **Amiodarone** | V-fib, pulseless V-tach | 300 mg IV push (first dose), 150 mg (second dose) |
| **Atropine** | Symptomatic bradycardia | 0.5–1 mg IV q3–5 min, max 3 mg |
| **Adenosine** | SVT | 6 mg rapid IV push + NS flush → 12 mg if needed. Use proximal IV site. |
| **Naloxone** | Opioid overdose | 0.4–2 mg IV/IM/IN, repeat q2–3 min. Short half-life — monitor for renarcotization |
| **D50 (Dextrose 50%)** | Severe hypoglycemia | 25–50 mL IV push. Recheck glucose q15 min |
| **Glucagon** | Hypoglycemia (no IV access) | 1 mg IM/SubQ. Position on side (causes N/V) |

### Fire Safety

**RACE** (in order):
- **R**escue anyone in immediate danger
- **A**ctivate the alarm
- **C**ontain the fire (close doors/windows)
- **E**xtinguish or **E**vacuate

**PASS** (fire extinguisher):
- **P**ull the pin
- **A**im at the base of the fire
- **S**queeze the handle
- **S**weep side to side

→ See Section 2: Safety & Infection Control and Section 8: Physiological Adaptation for complete emergency content

# SECTION 10: PRACTICE SCENARIOS — NGN Clinical Judgment Format

Reading about clinical judgment is one thing — doing it is another. These scenarios put you in the NGN format so you can practice the thinking NCLEX actually tests: recognizing cues, generating hypotheses, prioritizing actions, and evaluating outcomes. Work them like real test questions — no peeking at rationales until you've committed to an answer.

> **How to use these scenarios:** Work through each case as if you're at the bedside. Read the clinical information carefully, then answer the questions in order — they follow the NCJMM steps from Recognize Cues through Evaluate Outcomes. Cover the rationales until you've answered. These are designed to feel like the real NGN case studies on your exam.

---

## SCENARIO 1: Post-Operative Complications (Med-Surg)

### Clinical Setting
Medical-surgical unit, day shift.

### Patient Information
**Mr. James Whitfield, 58 years old.** Status post right hemicolectomy (colon resection) 18 hours ago for colon cancer. History of type 2 diabetes, hypertension, and obesity (BMI 34). Current medications include metformin (held post-op), lisinopril 10 mg daily, enoxaparin 40 mg SubQ daily, morphine PCA, and IV 0.9% NS at 125 mL/hr.

### Nurse's Notes — 0630
Patient resting in bed with eyes closed. Responds to name but appears drowsy. Abdominal dressing dry and intact with Jackson-Pratt drain in place — 50 mL serosanguineous drainage over last 8 hours. NG tube to low intermittent suction — 200 mL dark green drainage. Foley catheter draining clear amber urine. Patient denies pain, rates 2/10 "when I move." Has not ambulated since surgery. Sequential compression devices in place bilaterally.

### Vital Signs — 0630

| Parameter | Value |
|-----------|-------|
| Temperature | 100.8°F (38.2°C) |
| Heart Rate | 108 bpm |
| Blood Pressure | 98/62 mmHg |
| Respiratory Rate | 22/min |
| O₂ Saturation | 93% on room air |

### Lab Results (drawn at 0600)

| Test | Result | Reference |
|------|--------|-----------|
| WBC | 14,200/mm³ | 4,500–11,000 |
| Hemoglobin | 9.8 g/dL | 14–16.5 (male) |
| Hematocrit | 29% | 42–52% (male) |
| Platelets | 188,000/mm³ | 150,000–400,000 |
| Glucose | 198 mg/dL | 70–110 |
| Potassium | 3.2 mEq/L | 3.5–5.0 |
| BUN | 28 mg/dL | 5–20 |
| Creatinine | 1.1 mg/dL | 0.6–1.3 |

---

### Question 1 — Recognize Cues (Highlight Format)

**Instructions:** Highlight the assessment findings in the nurse's notes and vital signs that require follow-up.

**From the nurse's notes and vital signs, identify the findings that are of concern by selecting them below:**

- [ ] Patient responds to name but appears drowsy
- [ ] Jackson-Pratt drain: 50 mL serosanguineous drainage over 8 hours
- [ ] NG tube: 200 mL dark green drainage
- [ ] Patient denies pain, rates 2/10
- [ ] Has not ambulated since surgery
- [ ] Temperature 100.8°F (38.2°C)
- [ ] Heart rate 108 bpm
- [ ] Blood pressure 98/62 mmHg
- [ ] Respiratory rate 22/min
- [ ] O₂ saturation 93% on room air
- [ ] WBC 14,200/mm³
- [ ] Hemoglobin 9.8 g/dL
- [ ] Glucose 198 mg/dL
- [ ] Potassium 3.2 mEq/L
- [ ] BUN 28 mg/dL

**Correct Answers:** Patient appears drowsy | Has not ambulated since surgery | Temperature 100.8°F | Heart rate 108 bpm | Blood pressure 98/62 mmHg | O₂ saturation 93% on room air | WBC 14,200 | Hemoglobin 9.8 g/dL | Glucose 198 mg/dL | Potassium 3.2 mEq/L | BUN 28 mg/dL

**Rationale:**
- **Drowsiness** at 18 hrs post-op warrants attention — could indicate overmedication (morphine PCA), hypotension effects, or early sepsis.
- **Not ambulating** by 18 hours post-op increases risk for DVT/PE, pneumonia, and ileus.
- **Temp 100.8°F** — Low-grade fever is expected in the first 24 hrs ("surgical stress response"), but combined with elevated WBC and tachycardia, this cluster raises concern.
- **HR 108** — Tachycardia may indicate pain, hypovolemia, fever response, or early shock.
- **BP 98/62** — Hypotension in a patient with baseline hypertension is significant. Could indicate hypovolemia or early sepsis.
- **O₂ Sat 93%** — Below normal (95–100%). Consider atelectasis (most common post-op pulmonary complication), PE, or fluid overload.
- **WBC 14,200** — Elevated. Expected mild elevation post-op, but this combined with fever and tachycardia needs monitoring.
- **Hgb 9.8** — Below normal for a male. Expected drop post-surgery, but monitor for bleeding.
- **Glucose 198** — Elevated, likely due to surgical stress and held metformin. Needs insulin coverage.
- **K⁺ 3.2** — Low. NG suction loses potassium. Risk for cardiac arrhythmias, ileus, and muscle weakness.
- **BUN 28** — Mildly elevated. Consider dehydration or GI blood (swallowed blood from NG).

**Why not concerning:**
- JP drain output of 50 mL serosanguineous over 8 hours is normal post-abdominal surgery.
- NG drainage of 200 mL dark green is expected (bile and gastric contents).
- Pain rating of 2/10 is well-controlled, though in context of drowsiness, consider whether PCA overuse is contributing.
- Respiratory rate of 22 is mildly elevated but not critically abnormal — still warrants attention in context.
- Creatinine 1.1 is within normal limits.
- Platelets are normal.

---

### Question 2 — Analyze Cues (Matrix Format)

**Instructions:** For each assessment finding cluster, indicate the most likely underlying cause.

| Finding Cluster | Hypovolemia | Infection/Sepsis | Opioid Oversedation | Electrolyte Imbalance |
|----------------|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|
| Tachycardia + hypotension + low hemoglobin + elevated BUN | **X** | | | |
| Fever + elevated WBC + tachycardia | | **X** | | |
| Drowsiness + pain rating 2/10 + O₂ sat 93% | | | **X** | |
| Potassium 3.2 + NG suction + no ambulation | | | | **X** |

**Rationale:**
- **Hypovolemia cluster:** Tachycardia and hypotension are classic compensatory signs. Low Hgb suggests either surgical blood loss or hemodilution. Elevated BUN with normal creatinine suggests dehydration (prerenal azotemia). This patient's baseline BP is likely higher given his HTN history, making 98/62 more significant.
- **Infection/Sepsis cluster:** Fever + WBC + tachycardia = the early sepsis triad. At 18 hours post-abdominal surgery, consider surgical site infection (early for this, but not impossible), urinary tract infection (Foley in place), or pneumonia (not ambulating, low O₂ sat).
- **Opioid oversedation cluster:** Drowsiness + comfortable pain level + decreased O₂ sat suggests PCA may be providing more than needed. Morphine causes respiratory depression and sedation.
- **Electrolyte cluster:** NG suction depletes potassium, sodium, and chloride. K⁺ 3.2 increases risk for arrhythmias and contributes to ileus (which delays recovery). Lack of ambulation worsens ileus risk.

---

### Question 3 — Prioritize Hypotheses (Drag-and-Drop/Ranking Format)

**Instructions:** Rank the following concerns in order of priority (1 = address first, 4 = address last).

**Rank these nursing concerns from highest to lowest priority:**

| Priority | Concern |
|----------|---------|
| **1** | Hypotension and potential hypovolemia |
| **2** | Opioid oversedation with decreased oxygenation |
| **3** | Hypokalemia requiring replacement |
| **4** | Early ambulation for post-operative recovery |

**Rationale:**
- **Hypotension/hypovolemia is first** because inadequate perfusion threatens organ function. BP 98/62 with tachycardia indicates the body is compensating — if this continues without intervention, the patient could progress to shock. ABCs: circulation is at risk.
- **Opioid oversedation is second** because O₂ sat 93% + drowsiness indicates respiratory compromise. ABCs: breathing/oxygenation. Assess respiratory rate and depth, consider holding PCA, apply supplemental O₂.
- **Hypokalemia is third** because K⁺ 3.2 needs correction to prevent cardiac arrhythmias, but it's not immediately life-threatening at this level.
- **Ambulation is fourth** because it's essential for recovery but is not the immediate priority when the patient is hemodynamically unstable.

---

### Question 4 — Generate Solutions (SATA Format)

**Instructions:** Select all interventions that are appropriate for this patient at this time.

- [ ] A. Increase IV fluid rate and assess fluid status
- [ ] B. Hold the morphine PCA and reassess pain management
- [ ] C. Apply supplemental oxygen via nasal cannula
- [ ] D. Administer IV potassium chloride as ordered
- [ ] E. Notify the healthcare provider of vital sign changes
- [ ] F. Obtain a blood culture before starting antibiotics
- [ ] G. Administer a bolus of D5W for glucose management
- [ ] H. Encourage incentive spirometry every 1–2 hours
- [ ] I. Check most recent PCA usage history
- [ ] J. Assist patient to ambulate in the hallway immediately

**Correct Answers:** A, B, C, D, E, F, H, I

**Rationale:**
- **(A) Correct** — Fluid resuscitation addresses hypovolemia. Assess skin turgor, mucous membranes, urine output trend.
- **(B) Correct** — With drowsiness and O₂ sat 93%, the PCA should be held and pain reassessed. Consider non-opioid alternatives.
- **(C) Correct** — O₂ sat 93% warrants supplemental oxygen. Apply 2L NC, titrate to maintain >95%.
- **(D) Correct** — K⁺ 3.2 needs replacement. IV KCl per protocol (never IV push, always on pump, never >10 mEq/hr peripheral).
- **(E) Correct** — The combination of hypotension, tachycardia, fever, elevated WBC, and low O₂ sat requires provider notification.
- **(F) Correct** — If sepsis is suspected, blood cultures should be drawn BEFORE antibiotics are started.
- **(G) Incorrect** — D5W is not appropriate for glucose management. The patient has hyperglycemia; D5W would worsen it. Sliding scale insulin is appropriate.
- **(H) Correct** — Incentive spirometry prevents atelectasis (the most common cause of early post-op fever and low O₂ sat).
- **(I) Correct** — Review PCA history to determine if overuse is contributing to sedation. This is assessment before intervention.
- **(J) Incorrect** — Ambulation is important but NOT appropriate while the patient is hypotensive and hemodynamically unstable. Stabilize first.

---

### Question 5 — Take Action (Cloze/Drop-Down Format)

**Instructions:** Complete the following statements by selecting from the drop-down options.

**Statement 1:** The nurse should first address the patient's **[hypotension / hypokalemia / fever / hyperglycemia]** because **[it is the most life-threatening finding / it will improve the patient's pain / the provider ordered it first / it is causing the elevated WBC]**.

**Answer:** hypotension | it is the most life-threatening finding

**Statement 2:** Before notifying the provider, the nurse should **[administer IV potassium / obtain a full set of current vital signs and assess the surgical site / increase the PCA dose / transfer the patient to the ICU]** to provide a comprehensive report.

**Answer:** obtain a full set of current vital signs and assess the surgical site

**Statement 3:** The nurse should **[hold / continue / increase / discontinue]** the morphine PCA and apply **[a non-rebreather mask at 15 L/min / a nasal cannula at 2 L/min / a Venturi mask at 40% / no supplemental oxygen]** based on the current assessment.

**Answer:** hold | a nasal cannula at 2 L/min

**Rationale:**
- Hypotension takes priority because cardiovascular compromise threatens all organ systems. Using ABCs: adequate circulation is required before other issues can be effectively addressed.
- Before calling the provider, gather a complete picture — recheck vitals, assess the surgical site for bleeding or signs of infection, check JP drain output, review I&O, and assess urine output. This allows an SBAR report.
- Holding the PCA (not discontinuing — that's a provider order) is appropriate because the patient shows signs of oversedation. A nasal cannula at 2 L/min is appropriate for an O₂ sat of 93% — a non-rebreather at 15 L/min is for severe hypoxemia (O₂ sat <85% or acute respiratory distress).

---

### Question 6 — Evaluate Outcomes (Matrix Format)

**Instructions:** Two hours after interventions, the nurse reassesses. For each finding, indicate whether it suggests the patient is improving or requires escalation.

**Updated Assessment at 0830:**

| Finding | Improving | Requires Escalation |
|---------|:-:|:-:|
| BP 108/70 mmHg | **X** | |
| HR 96 bpm | **X** | |
| Temperature 101.4°F (38.6°C) | | **X** |
| O₂ sat 96% on 2L NC | **X** | |
| Patient alert, answering questions appropriately | **X** | |
| Urine output: 25 mL in last 2 hours | | **X** |
| Repeat K⁺: 3.4 mEq/L (after 20 mEq KCl) | **X** | |

**Rationale:**
- **BP 108/70** — Improving from 98/62. Still below his likely baseline with HTN, but trending in the right direction with fluid resuscitation.
- **HR 96** — Improving from 108. Tachycardia resolving as volume status improves.
- **Temp 101.4°F** — Worsening. Rising fever at 20 hours post-op, combined with elevated WBC, is concerning for developing infection. Blood culture results should be followed up. Provider should be notified if not already aware.
- **O₂ sat 96%** — Improving from 93%. Supplemental O₂ and holding PCA are working.
- **Alert and oriented** — Improving from drowsy. Holding the PCA has improved mental status.
- **UO 25 mL in 2 hrs** — Requires escalation. Normal is ≥30 mL/hr (≥60 mL in 2 hours). Output of 25 mL suggests continued hypovolemia or early renal compromise. Increase fluids, continue monitoring.
- **K⁺ 3.4** — Improving from 3.2. Moving toward normal range. Continue replacement per protocol.

---

## SCENARIO 2: Acute Heart Failure Exacerbation (Cardiac)

### Clinical Setting
Cardiac step-down unit, night shift.

### Patient Information
**Mrs. Dorothy Andersen, 72 years old.** Admitted for acute exacerbation of heart failure (HFrEF, EF 25%). History of atrial fibrillation, type 2 diabetes, and chronic kidney disease stage 3. Home medications: lisinopril 20 mg daily, carvedilol 12.5 mg BID, furosemide 40 mg PO daily, apixaban 5 mg BID, metformin 500 mg BID, and potassium chloride 20 mEq daily. Admitted weight: 176 lbs (80 kg). Dry weight per chart: 162 lbs (73.6 kg).

### Nurse's Notes — 2200
Patient sitting upright in bed, appears anxious and short of breath. States "I can't catch my breath when I lie flat." Using 2 pillows at home for the past week but tonight needs to sit straight up. Reports 3-day history of worsening ankle swelling and "my shoes won't fit." Has been eating canned soups and deli meats because she's been "too tired to cook." Bilateral crackles in lower lung fields. 3+ pitting edema bilateral lower extremities to mid-shin. Jugular venous distension noted at 45 degrees. Abdomen distended, patient reports feeling "bloated." Daily weight today: 182 lbs (82.7 kg) — a gain of 6 lbs (2.7 kg) since admission yesterday.

### Vital Signs — 2200

| Parameter | Value |
|-----------|-------|
| Temperature | 98.4°F (36.9°C) |
| Heart Rate | 112 bpm (irregular) |
| Blood Pressure | 158/94 mmHg |
| Respiratory Rate | 28/min |
| O₂ Saturation | 88% on room air |

### Lab Results

| Test | Result | Reference |
|------|--------|-----------|
| BNP | 1,840 pg/mL | <100 (normal), >400 (HF likely) |
| Potassium | 5.4 mEq/L | 3.5–5.0 |
| Sodium | 131 mEq/L | 135–145 |
| BUN | 42 mg/dL | 5–20 |
| Creatinine | 2.1 mg/dL | 0.6–1.3 |
| Digoxin level | 2.4 ng/mL | 0.8–2.0 |
| Glucose | 156 mg/dL | 70–110 |

---

### Question 1 — Recognize Cues (Highlight Format)

**Instructions:** From the data above, select ALL findings that indicate worsening heart failure or complications requiring immediate nursing attention.

- [ ] A. Patient sitting upright, anxious, short of breath
- [ ] B. Orthopnea (needs to sit straight up)
- [ ] C. 3-day history of worsening ankle swelling
- [ ] D. Has been eating canned soups and deli meats
- [ ] E. Bilateral crackles in lower lung fields
- [ ] F. 3+ pitting edema bilateral lower extremities
- [ ] G. JVD at 45 degrees
- [ ] H. Weight gain of 6 lbs since yesterday
- [ ] I. Heart rate 112, irregular
- [ ] J. Blood pressure 158/94
- [ ] K. Respiratory rate 28
- [ ] L. O₂ saturation 88%
- [ ] M. BNP 1,840 pg/mL
- [ ] N. Potassium 5.4 mEq/L
- [ ] O. Sodium 131 mEq/L
- [ ] P. Digoxin level 2.4 ng/mL
- [ ] Q. BUN 42, Creatinine 2.1

**Correct Answers:** All — A through Q

**Rationale:** Every single finding in this scenario is clinically significant. This is a patient in acute decompensated heart failure with multiple complications:

- **A, B, E, K, L** — Left-sided heart failure signs: dyspnea, orthopnea, crackles (fluid in lungs), tachypnea, hypoxemia. Pulmonary congestion is worsening.
- **C, F, G, H** — Right-sided heart failure signs: peripheral edema, JVD, abdominal distension, rapid weight gain (1 kg = ~1 L fluid; she's gained 2.7 kg = ~2.7 L fluid in one day).
- **D** — High sodium diet is a direct contributor to fluid retention and exacerbation.
- **I** — Tachycardia + irregular rhythm = uncontrolled atrial fibrillation, which worsens cardiac output.
- **J** — Hypertension increases afterload, further stressing the failing heart.
- **M** — BNP 1,840 confirms severe heart failure (>400 is diagnostic threshold).
- **N** — K⁺ 5.4 is above normal. With CKD, impaired renal excretion is likely. Risk for cardiac arrhythmias. Also concerning because she's on lisinopril (ACE inhibitor) and taking KCl supplement.
- **O** — Na⁺ 131 = dilutional hyponatremia from fluid overload. Common in severe HF.
- **P** — Digoxin 2.4 is TOXIC (therapeutic: 0.8–2.0). With impaired renal function, digoxin clearance is reduced. Hold digoxin, monitor for toxicity signs (N/V, visual halos, bradycardia — though A-fib masks the bradycardia).
- **Q** — BUN 42, Creatinine 2.1 = worsening renal function (cardiorenal syndrome). CKD baseline may be lower — compare to previous results.

---

### Question 2 — Analyze Cues (Cloze/Drop-Down Format)

**Instructions:** Complete the following analysis.

**Statement 1:** The patient's worsening symptoms are primarily caused by **[fluid overload / dehydration / medication non-compliance / pneumonia]** as evidenced by **[crackles, edema, JVD, and rapid weight gain / low blood pressure and dry skin / empty pill bottles / productive cough and fever]**.

**Answer:** fluid overload | crackles, edema, JVD, and rapid weight gain

**Statement 2:** The patient's dietary intake of canned soups and deli meats contributed to her exacerbation because these foods are **[high in sodium / high in potassium / low in protein / high in sugar]**, which causes **[fluid retention / hyperkalemia / muscle wasting / hyperglycemia]**.

**Answer:** high in sodium | fluid retention

**Statement 3:** The digoxin level of 2.4 ng/mL is **[therapeutic / subtherapeutic / toxic / not relevant]**, and the most likely contributing factor is **[decreased renal clearance / increased hepatic metabolism / dietary interaction / missed doses]**.

**Answer:** toxic | decreased renal clearance

**Rationale:**
- HF exacerbation is almost always fluid overload, triggered by dietary sodium excess, medication non-adherence, or worsening cardiac function. This patient has clear volume overload signs (crackles, edema, JVD, weight gain).
- Canned soups and deli meats are extremely high in sodium. One can of soup can contain 800–1,200 mg sodium. Heart failure patients should limit sodium to <2,000 mg/day. She's likely been consuming 3,000+ mg/day.
- Digoxin is renally cleared. With creatinine 2.1 (worsening CKD), digoxin accumulates → toxicity. This is a critical drug-disease interaction to recognize.

---

### Question 3 — Prioritize Hypotheses (Drag-and-Drop Format)

**Instructions:** Rank these patient problems from highest to lowest priority.

| Priority | Problem |
|----------|---------|
| **1** | Impaired gas exchange (O₂ sat 88%, crackles, dyspnea) |
| **2** | Digoxin toxicity (level 2.4, risk for fatal arrhythmia) |
| **3** | Excess fluid volume (6 lb weight gain, edema, JVD) |
| **4** | Hyperkalemia (K⁺ 5.4 with digoxin toxicity compounds risk) |
| **5** | Knowledge deficit regarding sodium-restricted diet |

**Rationale:**
- **Oxygenation is always first** — ABCs. O₂ sat 88% requires immediate intervention (supplemental O₂, positioning).
- **Digoxin toxicity is second** because toxic digoxin levels in a patient with A-fib and hyperkalemia create serious arrhythmia risk. Hypokalemia increases dig toxicity, but hyperkalemia with dig toxicity also increases cardiac risk. Hold digoxin immediately.
- **Fluid overload is third** because it's the root cause of the respiratory symptoms — IV diuretics will address both the fluid and the oxygenation over time.
- **Hyperkalemia is fourth** — K⁺ 5.4 is mildly elevated and concerning in the context of digoxin toxicity (potassium and digoxin compete for the same binding sites), but not at immediately life-threatening levels. Hold KCl supplement, reassess after diuresis.
- **Teaching is fifth** — essential for preventing readmission, but not the acute priority.

---

### Question 4 — Take Action (SATA Format)

**Instructions:** Select all appropriate immediate nursing actions.

- [ ] A. Elevate the head of bed to high Fowler's position
- [ ] B. Apply supplemental oxygen to maintain O₂ sat >95%
- [ ] C. Hold the digoxin and notify the provider of the toxic level
- [ ] D. Administer IV furosemide as ordered
- [ ] E. Hold the potassium chloride supplement
- [ ] F. Restrict oral fluids to 1,500 mL/day
- [ ] G. Administer a normal saline bolus of 500 mL
- [ ] H. Place patient on continuous cardiac monitoring
- [ ] I. Weigh patient daily at the same time
- [ ] J. Begin a low-sodium diet education session now
- [ ] K. Insert a Foley catheter to accurately monitor urine output
- [ ] L. Prepare to administer Digibind if provider orders it

**Correct Answers:** A, B, C, D, E, F, H, K, L

**Rationale:**
- **(A) Correct** — High Fowler's reduces preload, improves lung expansion, decreases work of breathing.
- **(B) Correct** — O₂ sat 88% needs supplemental O₂. Start with NC at 2–4 L/min, titrate up.
- **(C) Correct** — Digoxin level 2.4 is toxic. HOLD the drug and notify the provider immediately.
- **(D) Correct** — IV furosemide (not PO — IV is faster and more reliable in acute decompensation) to diurese excess fluid.
- **(E) Correct** — K⁺ is already 5.4 and patient is on KCl supplement. Hold it.
- **(F) Correct** — Fluid restriction is a core HF management strategy during acute exacerbation.
- **(G) Incorrect** — NS bolus would WORSEN fluid overload. This patient needs fluid removal, not addition.
- **(H) Correct** — With digoxin toxicity + hyperkalemia + A-fib, continuous telemetry is essential.
- **(I)** — Important but already being done. Not an immediate priority action at 2200. (Not wrong, but not an "immediate" action for this question.)
- **(J) Incorrect** — Patient is in acute respiratory distress. Teaching is important but should wait until she's stable and can focus.
- **(K) Correct** — Accurate I&O monitoring is critical during aggressive diuresis. A Foley allows precise measurement.
- **(L) Correct** — Digibind (digoxin immune fab) may be needed if the provider determines the toxicity warrants it. Having it prepared is appropriate.

---

### Question 5 — Evaluate Outcomes (Matrix Format)

**Instructions:** Four hours later, after IV furosemide 80 mg, supplemental O₂ at 4L NC, and holding digoxin, the nurse reassesses. Evaluate each finding.

| Finding at 0200 | Expected/Improving | Concerning/Unexpected |
|-----------------|:-:|:-:|
| O₂ sat 94% on 4L NC | **X** | |
| Urine output: 800 mL in 4 hours | **X** | |
| BP 138/82 | **X** | |
| HR 88, irregular | **X** | |
| Patient reports seeing yellow-green halos around lights | | **X** |
| RR 22 | **X** | |
| Crackles now in bases only (were in lower 1/3 bilaterally) | **X** | |
| Patient able to recline to 45 degrees without dyspnea | **X** | |

**Rationale:**
- Most findings show improvement: oxygenation improving, excellent urine output (diuresis working), BP decreasing toward target, HR improved, crackles resolving, tolerating lower position.
- **Visual halos are a classic sign of digoxin toxicity** and indicate the toxicity is producing symptoms despite holding the drug. Digoxin has a long half-life (36 hours, longer with renal impairment). This finding should be reported to the provider — Digibind may now be indicated.

→ See Section 8: Physiological Adaptation (Heart Failure) and Section 6: Pharmacological Therapies (Cardiac Glycosides)

---

## SCENARIO 3: Laboring Patient with Fetal Distress (OB)

### Clinical Setting
Labor and delivery unit, evening shift.

### Patient Information
**Ms. Aisha Johnson, 29 years old.** G2P1, 39 weeks 3 days gestation. Admitted in active labor. History of uncomplicated previous vaginal delivery. Current pregnancy uncomplicated. GBS negative. Membranes ruptured spontaneously 2 hours ago — clear amniotic fluid. Epidural placed 1 hour ago. Oxytocin (Pitocin) infusing at 8 mU/min.

### Nurse's Notes — 1830
Cervical exam: 7 cm dilated, 90% effaced, 0 station. Contractions every 3 minutes, lasting 60 seconds, moderate to strong by palpation. Patient reports good pain relief from epidural, rates pain 3/10. Fetal heart rate baseline 145 bpm with moderate variability. Occasional accelerations noted. No decelerations. Amniotic fluid clear.

### Updated Assessment — 1915 (45 minutes later)

**Fetal Heart Rate Tracing:**
- Baseline: 160 bpm (was 145)
- Variability: minimal (was moderate)
- Repetitive late decelerations following each contraction — FHR dips to 110 bpm, slow return to baseline 30 seconds after contraction ends
- No accelerations in the last 15 minutes

**Maternal Vital Signs:**

| Parameter | Value |
|-----------|-------|
| Temperature | 98.8°F |
| Heart Rate | 92 bpm |
| Blood Pressure | 100/58 mmHg (was 118/72 prior to epidural) |
| Respiratory Rate | 18/min |
| O₂ Saturation | 99% |

**Cervical Exam:** 8 cm, 100% effaced, +1 station. Contractions every 2 minutes, lasting 70 seconds, strong.

---

### Question 1 — Recognize Cues (Highlight Format)

**Instructions:** Identify the findings from the 1915 assessment that are concerning.

- [ ] A. FHR baseline increased from 145 to 160 bpm
- [ ] B. Variability changed from moderate to minimal
- [ ] C. Repetitive late decelerations to 110 bpm
- [ ] D. No accelerations in 15 minutes
- [ ] E. Maternal BP 100/58 (was 118/72)
- [ ] F. Contractions every 2 minutes, 70 seconds duration
- [ ] G. Cervix 8 cm, 100% effaced, +1 station
- [ ] H. Maternal HR 92 bpm

**Correct Answers:** A, B, C, D, E, F

**Rationale:**
- **(A)** FHR 160 is at the upper limit. Rising baseline can indicate fetal stress or compensatory response to decreased oxygenation.
- **(B)** Loss of variability from moderate to minimal is a key warning sign. Moderate variability is the best indicator of fetal well-being. Minimal variability may indicate fetal hypoxia, acidosis, or CNS depression.
- **(C)** Late decelerations are the most concerning deceleration pattern — they indicate **uteroplacental insufficiency**. Repetitive late decels are an ominous pattern. (VEAL CHOP: Late = Placental insufficiency)
- **(D)** Absent accelerations + minimal variability + late decels = non-reassuring tracing (Category III if variability is absent).
- **(E)** Maternal hypotension from the epidural can decrease uteroplacental perfusion, which may be causing or worsening the late decelerations.
- **(F)** Contractions every 2 minutes lasting 70 seconds is approaching tachysystole (≥5 contractions in 10 minutes). Excessive uterine activity reduces placental blood flow between contractions, contributing to fetal distress.
- **(G) Not concerning** — Normal labor progression. She's progressing appropriately.
- **(H) Not concerning** — Maternal HR 92 is normal.

---

### Question 2 — Analyze Cues (Cloze/Drop-Down Format)

**Instructions:** Complete the analysis.

**The fetal heart rate pattern of repetitive late decelerations with minimal variability most likely indicates **[cord compression / head compression / uteroplacental insufficiency / a normal fetal sleep cycle]**.

**Answer:** uteroplacental insufficiency

**The most likely contributing factor to this FHR pattern is **[epidural-related maternal hypotension reducing placental perfusion / maternal fever / fetal sleep / normal transition labor changes]**, compounded by **[tachysystole from oxytocin / maternal hyperventilation / fetal prematurity / dehydration]**.

**Answer:** epidural-related maternal hypotension reducing placental perfusion | tachysystole from oxytocin

**Rationale:**
Late decelerations are caused by decreased blood flow through the placenta (uteroplacental insufficiency). Two factors are combining here: (1) the epidural caused maternal hypotension (BP dropped from 118/72 to 100/58), reducing blood flow to the uterus, and (2) contractions are frequent and prolonged (approaching tachysystole), reducing the rest period between contractions when the placenta normally reoxygenates.

---

### Question 3 — Generate Solutions & Take Action (Ordered Response Format)

**Instructions:** Place the following nursing actions in the correct priority order.

| Priority | Action |
|----------|--------|
| **1** | Reposition the patient to left lateral side |
| **2** | Stop the oxytocin (Pitocin) infusion |
| **3** | Increase the IV fluid rate (bolus LR or NS) |
| **4** | Apply oxygen via non-rebreather mask at 8–10 L/min |
| **5** | Notify the healthcare provider with SBAR report |
| **6** | Prepare for possible emergency cesarean delivery |

**Rationale:**
- **Reposition first** — This is the fastest, easiest intervention. Left lateral position relieves aortic compression, improves venous return, and increases uterine blood flow. Takes seconds. Do it immediately while taking other actions.
- **Stop Pitocin second** — The contractions are too frequent, reducing placental perfusion. Stopping oxytocin reduces uterine activity and allows the placenta to reoxygenate.
- **IV fluid bolus third** — Addresses maternal hypotension. A bolus of LR or NS (500–1000 mL) increases blood volume and improves placental perfusion. This is also the treatment for epidural-induced hypotension.
- **Oxygen fourth** — While O₂ sat is 99% for mother, supplemental O₂ increases the oxygen available to the fetus through the placenta.
- **Notify provider fifth** — With interventions underway, call the provider. Use SBAR: Situation (repetitive late decels with minimal variability), Background (G2P1 at 8 cm, on Pitocin, epidural in place), Assessment (concern for uteroplacental insufficiency, likely related to hypotension and tachysystole), Recommendation (Pitocin stopped, repositioned, fluid bolus given, requesting orders).
- **Prepare for cesarean sixth** — If the FHR does not improve after intrauterine resuscitation, emergency cesarean delivery may be necessary. Preparing in advance saves critical minutes.

---

### Question 4 — Evaluate Outcomes (Matrix Format)

**Instructions:** Ten minutes after interventions (Pitocin stopped, repositioned to left lateral, 500 mL LR bolus, O₂ at 10 L/min), reassess the fetal tracing. Evaluate each finding.

| Finding | Reassuring | Non-Reassuring |
|---------|:-:|:-:|
| FHR baseline 148 bpm | **X** | |
| Variability returning to moderate | **X** | |
| One late deceleration in 10 minutes (was repetitive) | **X** (improving) | |
| Two accelerations noted | **X** | |
| Maternal BP 112/68 | **X** | |
| Contractions every 4 minutes, 50 seconds | **X** | |

**Rationale:**
The interventions are working. The FHR baseline has come down from 160 to 148 (normal range). Variability is returning to moderate — the single best indicator of fetal well-being. Late decelerations have nearly resolved (one isolated vs. repetitive). Accelerations have returned — a reassuring sign. Maternal BP improved with the fluid bolus. Contraction pattern has normalized after stopping Pitocin.

**Next steps:** Continue monitoring closely. The provider may restart oxytocin at a lower rate once the FHR tracing is consistently reassuring. Continue left lateral positioning. Continue IV fluids to maintain maternal BP. Document the entire episode thoroughly.

→ See Section 3: Health Promotion & Maintenance (Labor & Delivery) and Quick Reference Card 15 (Stages of Labor)

---

## SCENARIO 4: Pediatric Diabetic Ketoacidosis (Peds/Endocrine)

### Clinical Setting
Pediatric emergency department, then pediatric ICU.

### Patient Information
**Lily Chen, 8 years old, 25 kg.** Brought to the ED by her mother. Mom reports Lily has had increased thirst and frequent urination for the past 3 weeks, with a 7-pound weight loss. For the past 2 days, Lily has had nausea, vomiting, and abdominal pain. This morning, Lily's breathing became "fast and deep" and she became "confused and hard to wake up." No known medical history. No prior hospitalizations. No medications. Family history: maternal grandmother has type 2 diabetes.

### Emergency Department Assessment

**Vital Signs:**

| Parameter | Value | Pediatric Normal (age 8) |
|-----------|-------|-------------------------|
| Temperature | 97.8°F (36.6°C) | 98.6°F ± 1° |
| Heart Rate | 138 bpm | 70–110 |
| Blood Pressure | 88/52 mmHg | 85–120 systolic |
| Respiratory Rate | 32/min, deep | 16–22 |
| O₂ Saturation | 99% | 95–100% |

**Physical Exam:**
- Lethargic, responds to voice but slow to answer
- Eyes appear sunken, lips dry and cracked
- Mucous membranes dry, skin tenting present (>2 seconds)
- Breath has a fruity odor
- Deep, rapid breathing pattern (Kussmaul respirations)
- Abdomen soft, diffusely tender
- Capillary refill 3 seconds
- Weight: 25 kg

**Lab Results:**

| Test | Result | Reference |
|------|--------|-----------|
| Blood glucose | 486 mg/dL | 70–110 |
| pH | 7.18 | 7.35–7.45 |
| PaCO₂ | 22 mmHg | 35–45 |
| HCO₃ | 8 mEq/L | 22–26 |
| Potassium | 5.8 mEq/L | 3.5–5.0 |
| Sodium | 128 mEq/L | 135–145 |
| BUN | 32 mg/dL | 5–20 |
| Creatinine | 1.4 mg/dL | 0.3–0.7 (peds) |
| Serum ketones | Large positive | Negative |
| Urinalysis | Glucose 4+, ketones 3+ | Negative |
| WBC | 16,800/mm³ | 4,500–13,500 (peds) |

---

### Question 1 — Recognize Cues (Matrix Format)

**Instructions:** Categorize each finding as related to hyperglycemia, dehydration, or metabolic acidosis.

| Finding | Hyperglycemia | Dehydration | Metabolic Acidosis |
|---------|:-:|:-:|:-:|
| Glucose 486 mg/dL | **X** | | |
| Polydipsia and polyuria × 3 weeks | **X** | | |
| 7-pound weight loss | **X** | **X** | |
| Sunken eyes, dry mucous membranes, skin tenting | | **X** | |
| Capillary refill 3 seconds | | **X** | |
| Tachycardia (HR 138) | | **X** | **X** |
| Kussmaul respirations (deep, rapid) | | | **X** |
| Fruity breath odor | | | **X** |
| pH 7.18 | | | **X** |
| HCO₃ 8 | | | **X** |
| PaCO₂ 22 | | | **X** |
| Nausea, vomiting, abdominal pain | | **X** | **X** |
| Lethargy/altered mental status | | **X** | **X** |
| BUN 32, Creatinine 1.4 | | **X** | |

**Rationale:**
- **Hyperglycemia:** Glucose 486 confirms severe hyperglycemia. The 3-week history of polydipsia/polyuria represents the classic presentation of new-onset Type 1 DM — the body can't use glucose, so it spills into urine and draws water with it (osmotic diuresis). Weight loss occurs because the body breaks down fat and muscle for energy.
- **Dehydration:** Osmotic diuresis + vomiting = severe dehydration. Sunken eyes, dry mucous membranes, skin tenting, prolonged cap refill, and elevated BUN/creatinine all confirm significant volume depletion. Tachycardia is compensatory.
- **Metabolic acidosis:** pH 7.18 with HCO₃ 8 = severe metabolic acidosis. PaCO₂ 22 = respiratory compensation (Kussmaul breathing is the body's attempt to blow off CO₂ and raise pH). Fruity breath = acetone from ketone production. The body, unable to use glucose, breaks down fat → ketones → ketoacids → acidosis.

Note: Some findings overlap categories because DKA is a cascade — hyperglycemia causes dehydration, dehydration worsens acidosis, acidosis causes symptoms that worsen dehydration.

---

### Question 2 — Prioritize Hypotheses (Cloze/Drop-Down Format)

**Instructions:** Complete the clinical reasoning.

**The ABG results of pH 7.18, PaCO₂ 22, HCO₃ 8 indicate **[partially compensated metabolic acidosis / uncompensated respiratory alkalosis / fully compensated metabolic acidosis / uncompensated metabolic acidosis]**.

**Answer:** partially compensated metabolic acidosis

**The immediate life-threatening concern for this child is **[hyperglycemia / dehydration and circulatory compromise / diabetic teaching needs / weight loss]** because **[it can lead to shock and organ failure / blood glucose is dangerously high / the family needs to learn insulin administration / the child is underweight]**.

**Answer:** dehydration and circulatory compromise | it can lead to shock and organ failure

**Rationale:**
- ABG: pH is low (acidosis), HCO₃ is low (metabolic cause — ROME: M=E, both low), CO₂ is also low (lungs compensating by hyperventilating). Since pH has NOT returned to normal, it's partially compensated.
- While glucose 486 is dangerously high, the most immediate life threat is the dehydration. Lily has signs of moderate-to-severe dehydration (tachycardia, hypotension for age, prolonged cap refill, altered mental status). If circulating volume continues to drop, she'll progress to hypovolemic shock. Fluid replacement is priority #1 before aggressively addressing glucose.

---

### Question 3 — Generate Solutions (SATA Format)

**Instructions:** Select all appropriate initial interventions for this child.

- [ ] A. Establish two large-bore IV access sites
- [ ] B. Begin IV normal saline (0.9% NS) bolus 10–20 mL/kg
- [ ] C. Administer IV regular insulin bolus followed by continuous drip
- [ ] D. Begin continuous regular insulin drip at 0.1 units/kg/hr (no bolus)
- [ ] E. Place on continuous cardiac monitoring
- [ ] F. Insert a Foley catheter for strict I&O
- [ ] G. Administer IV sodium bicarbonate to correct acidosis
- [ ] H. Monitor blood glucose every hour
- [ ] I. Monitor potassium and replace when K⁺ drops below 5.0
- [ ] J. Perform neurological checks every hour
- [ ] K. Administer IV potassium chloride immediately
- [ ] L. Obtain a 12-lead EKG

**Correct Answers:** A, B, D, E, F, H, I, J, L

**Rationale:**
- **(A) Correct** — Two IV sites needed: one for fluids, one for insulin drip. Ensures access if one fails.
- **(B) Correct** — Initial fluid resuscitation with NS at 10–20 mL/kg over 1–2 hours. Restores circulating volume. Do NOT give too fast in pediatric DKA — risk of cerebral edema.
- **(C) Incorrect** — In pediatric DKA, an insulin BOLUS is generally NOT given because it increases the risk of cerebral edema. Start with a continuous drip only.
- **(D) Correct** — Continuous low-dose regular insulin drip at 0.1 units/kg/hr. Start AFTER initial fluid resuscitation (usually after the first hour of fluids). Goal: decrease glucose by 50–100 mg/dL per hour — NOT faster.
- **(E) Correct** — K⁺ 5.8 with acidosis = cardiac arrhythmia risk. As acidosis corrects, K⁺ will SHIFT INTO cells and drop rapidly. Continuous monitoring essential.
- **(F) Correct** — Strict I&O in a child with severe dehydration and altered mental status. Minimum UO goal: 1 mL/kg/hr in peds.
- **(G) Incorrect** — IV bicarb is generally NOT recommended in pediatric DKA unless pH <6.9 (per most guidelines). Bicarb can worsen hypokalemia and increase risk of cerebral edema.
- **(H) Correct** — Hourly glucose monitoring to ensure steady (not rapid) decline. When glucose reaches 250–300, add dextrose to IV fluids (D5 0.45% NS) to prevent hypoglycemia while continuing insulin to clear ketones.
- **(I) Correct** — K⁺ 5.8 is HIGH now, but this is falsely elevated due to acidosis (H⁺ moves into cells, K⁺ moves out). As insulin and fluids correct the acidosis, K⁺ will plummet. Begin K⁺ replacement when level drops below 5.0 and UO is confirmed.
- **(J) Correct** — Cerebral edema is the most dangerous complication of pediatric DKA treatment. Neuro checks q1h: assess for headache, irritability, decreased LOC, pupil changes, Cushing's triad.
- **(K) Incorrect** — K⁺ is currently 5.8. Giving potassium now could cause fatal cardiac arrhythmia. Replace only when it drops below 5.0.
- **(L) Correct** — With K⁺ 5.8, a baseline EKG assesses for peaked T waves, widened QRS, or other potassium-related changes.

---

### Question 4 — Evaluate Outcomes (Matrix Format)

**Instructions:** After 4 hours of treatment (NS fluids, insulin drip at 0.1 units/kg/hr started after first hour), evaluate the following findings.

| Finding at Hour 4 | Expected/On Track | Requires Intervention |
|-------------------|:-:|:-:|
| Glucose: 320 mg/dL (from 486) | **X** | |
| pH: 7.28 (from 7.18) | **X** | |
| K⁺: 4.2 (from 5.8) | **X** | |
| HCO₃: 14 (from 8) | **X** | |
| HR: 112 (from 138) | **X** | |
| BP: 96/60 (from 88/52) | **X** | |
| Urine output: 30 mL/hr | **X** | |
| Child complains of severe headache, becomes irritable | | **X** |

**Rationale:**
- Glucose decreased by ~166 mg/dL over ~3 hours of insulin (about 55 mg/dL/hr) — within the safe range of 50–100/hr. On track. When it reaches 250–300, add dextrose to IV fluids.
- pH improving from 7.18 to 7.28 — trend is correct, acidosis resolving.
- K⁺ dropped from 5.8 to 4.2 — expected as acidosis corrects. Now within normal range. Continue monitoring and begin K⁺ replacement in IV fluids to prevent hypokalemia.
- HCO₃ rising from 8 to 14 — metabolic acidosis correcting.
- HR and BP improving — dehydration correcting with fluid resuscitation.
- UO 30 mL/hr (1.2 mL/kg/hr) — adequate for a 25 kg child.
- **HEADACHE + IRRITABILITY = RED FLAG FOR CEREBRAL EDEMA.** This is the most feared complication of pediatric DKA treatment. Action: elevate HOB 30°, slow IV fluid rate, notify provider IMMEDIATELY, prepare IV mannitol or hypertonic saline (3%). Do NOT give anything that increases ICP. This is a medical emergency.

→ See Section 8: Physiological Adaptation (DKA) and Section 7: Reduction of Risk Potential (Pediatric Emergencies)

---

## SCENARIO 5: Suicidal Ideation (Mental Health)

### Clinical Setting
Emergency department psychiatric evaluation area, evening shift.

### Patient Information
**Mr. David Park, 34 years old.** Brought to the ED by his wife after she found him in the garage with the car running and a note on the kitchen table. He was conscious and cooperative when she found him (she arrived home within minutes of him starting the car). No physical injuries. No carbon monoxide poisoning symptoms. History of major depressive disorder diagnosed 2 years ago, currently prescribed sertraline 100 mg daily. Wife reports he stopped taking his medication about 6 weeks ago because he "felt fine." Recently lost his job 3 weeks ago due to company layoffs. No previous suicide attempts. No substance abuse history. No history of violence.

### Nurse's Assessment Notes — 1900
Patient sitting quietly in the exam room, making intermittent eye contact. Appears well-groomed. Speech is slow, monotone. Affect flat. States: "I don't see the point anymore. My family would be better off without me." When asked about the garage incident, he states: "I had a plan and I did it. My wife just happened to come home." Denies current homicidal ideation. Denies hallucinations or delusions. Oriented ×4. Denies substance use today. Last ate "a few bites of something yesterday." Reports sleeping 2–3 hours per night for the past 3 weeks.

### Risk Assessment Data

| Factor | Detail |
|--------|--------|
| Method | Attempted (car running in closed garage — lethal means) |
| Plan specificity | High (wrote a note, chose a method, had a time when wife would be away) |
| Lethality of attempt | High (carbon monoxide in enclosed space) |
| Rescue factors | Accidental (wife returned early) |
| Previous attempts | None |
| Psychiatric history | MDD, off medications × 6 weeks |
| Psychosocial stressors | Job loss, financial pressure |
| Protective factors | Married, wife involved, no substance abuse, cooperative |
| Current ideation | Ongoing ("I don't see the point") |
| Statements | "My family would be better off without me" (perceived burdensomeness) |

---

### Question 1 — Recognize Cues (Highlight Format)

**Instructions:** From the assessment, identify all findings that indicate HIGH suicide risk.

- [ ] A. Found in garage with car running and a note
- [ ] B. Stopped taking sertraline 6 weeks ago
- [ ] C. Recent job loss 3 weeks ago
- [ ] D. "I don't see the point anymore"
- [ ] E. "My family would be better off without me"
- [ ] F. "I had a plan and I did it"
- [ ] G. Flat affect, slow speech
- [ ] H. Wife found him within minutes (accidental rescue)
- [ ] I. Sleeping 2–3 hours per night × 3 weeks
- [ ] J. Intermittent eye contact
- [ ] K. Ate "a few bites" yesterday
- [ ] L. Denies substance use
- [ ] M. Cooperative with evaluation

**Correct Answers:** A, B, C, D, E, F, G, H, I, K

**Rationale:**
- **(A)** This was an actual attempt, not ideation. He took action using a lethal method and left a note — indicating intent to die and expectation of death.
- **(B)** Medication discontinuation is a major risk factor. Sertraline takes weeks to reach full effect. Abruptly stopping can cause rebound depression and destabilization.
- **(C)** Job loss is a significant psychosocial stressor. Unemployment is associated with increased suicide risk, particularly in men.
- **(D)** Hopelessness ("I don't see the point") is one of the strongest predictors of future suicide attempts — stronger even than depression severity.
- **(E)** Perceived burdensomeness ("better off without me") is a well-established risk factor. He genuinely believes his death would benefit his family.
- **(F)** He explicitly states he had a plan and executed it. This shows premeditation, intent, and follow-through.
- **(G)** Flat affect and psychomotor slowing indicate severe depression. Paradoxically, patients may be at HIGHEST risk when they shift from severe depression to slight improvement (they gain enough energy to act on plans).
- **(H)** Rescue was accidental — he did not call for help, did not leave the garage open, did not want to be found. This increases assessed lethality.
- **(I)** Severe insomnia (2–3 hrs/night) for 3 weeks indicates deteriorating mental health and is an independent risk factor for suicidal behavior.
- **(K)** Reduced appetite and minimal intake further indicate severe depression and possible neglect of self-care.
- **(L, M) Not risk indicators** — Denying substance use and being cooperative are actually protective factors, though they do not reduce the overall high risk from the other findings.
- **(J)** Intermittent eye contact alone is not a reliable risk indicator — it may reflect depression, cultural factors, or personality.

---

### Question 2 — Analyze Cues (Cloze/Drop-Down Format)

**Instructions:** Complete the clinical analysis.

**Statement 1:** This patient's suicide risk level is **[low / moderate / high / imminent]** because he **[expressed passive ideation only / has multiple risk factors but no plan / made an attempt with a lethal method and expresses ongoing hopelessness / has a history of multiple attempts]**.

**Answer:** high | made an attempt with a lethal method and expresses ongoing hopelessness

**Statement 2:** The statement "My family would be better off without me" reflects **[perceived burdensomeness / command hallucinations / rational problem-solving / attention-seeking behavior]**, which is a **[low / moderate / significant / irrelevant]** risk factor for future attempts.

**Answer:** perceived burdensomeness | significant

**Statement 3:** The fact that the patient stopped sertraline 6 weeks ago is clinically important because **[SSRIs are addictive / discontinuation can cause rebound worsening of depression / sertraline causes suicidal thoughts / the medication was not working]**.

**Answer:** discontinuation can cause rebound worsening of depression

**Rationale:**
- This patient's risk is high: recent lethal attempt, specific plan that was carried out, accidental rescue, ongoing hopelessness, perceived burdensomeness, medication non-adherence, and acute stressor. He requires inpatient psychiatric admission.
- Perceived burdensomeness is a core component of several suicidality models. When a person genuinely believes others would be better served by their death, they've overcome a major psychological barrier to self-harm.
- SSRI discontinuation after 6 weeks of stable use can cause discontinuation syndrome and, more importantly, allow the underlying depression to relapse or worsen. This likely contributed to the escalation from managed depression to acute crisis.

---

### Question 3 — Generate Solutions (SATA Format)

**Instructions:** Select all appropriate nursing interventions for this patient.

- [ ] A. Initiate one-to-one continuous observation (1:1 sitter)
- [ ] B. Remove all potentially harmful items from the environment (cords, sharps, belts, medications)
- [ ] C. Administer sertraline 100 mg immediately to restart his medication
- [ ] D. Place patient in a safe room (no exposed pipes, breakaway fixtures, shatterproof windows)
- [ ] E. Use a calm, direct, non-judgmental communication approach
- [ ] F. Establish a verbal "no-suicide contract" with the patient
- [ ] G. Allow the wife to stay at bedside without restriction
- [ ] H. Assess for access to lethal means at home (firearms, medications, etc.)
- [ ] I. Document the risk assessment thoroughly
- [ ] J. Notify the psychiatry team for evaluation and admission recommendation
- [ ] K. Allow the patient to use his cell phone freely
- [ ] L. Encourage the patient to express his feelings and listen without interrupting

**Correct Answers:** A, B, D, E, H, I, J, L

**Rationale:**
- **(A) Correct** — One-to-one observation is the standard of care for a patient who has made a recent suicide attempt. The patient should never be left alone.
- **(B) Correct** — Environmental safety is critical. Remove anything that could be used for self-harm: cords, IV tubing (coil and secure), belts, shoelaces, sharps, plastic bags. This is called "ligature-resistant" precautions.
- **(C) Incorrect** — Restarting sertraline is ultimately appropriate, but this is a provider order, not a nursing decision. Also, SSRIs have a "black box" warning — during the initial weeks of restarting, suicidal ideation may temporarily increase. The provider needs to make this decision with appropriate monitoring in place.
- **(D) Correct** — A safe/seclusion-ready room with environmental safeguards reduces means access.
- **(E) Correct** — Therapeutic communication is fundamental. Be direct (ask about suicidal thoughts openly — it does NOT increase risk), calm, and non-judgmental. Avoid platitudes ("Things will get better") or minimizing ("Others have it worse").
- **(F) Incorrect** — "No-suicide contracts" are NOT evidence-based and are NOT recommended. They may provide false reassurance to staff while doing nothing to reduce actual risk. Use collaborative safety planning instead (a different, evidence-based approach).
- **(G) Incorrect** — While family involvement is important, unrestricted visiting may not be appropriate in the acute phase. The wife may unknowingly bring in items, and the patient needs professional observation, not family substituting for clinical monitoring. Visits should be allowed but supervised.
- **(H) Correct** — Lethal means counseling is one of the most effective suicide prevention interventions. Ask about firearms, medications, and other means at home. Work with the wife to secure or remove them before discharge (whenever that occurs).
- **(I) Correct** — Thorough documentation of the risk assessment, interventions, and rationale protects the patient and the nurse. Document specific statements in quotes.
- **(J) Correct** — This patient needs psychiatry evaluation for likely inpatient admission. A voluntary admission is preferred if the patient is willing; involuntary commitment may be necessary if he refuses and meets criteria.
- **(K) Incorrect** — Free cell phone use is not automatically restricted, but in the acute phase, it should be assessed. Some facilities restrict phones because patients can use them to access means, say goodbye (part of a plan), or become distressed by messages. Follow facility policy.
- **(L) Correct** — Active listening is therapeutic. Let the patient express his feelings without judgment. Do not argue, debate, or try to talk him out of his feelings. Validate his pain while maintaining hope.

---

### Question 4 — Take Action (Ordered Response Format)

**Instructions:** Place these actions in the correct priority order for the first hour of care.

| Priority | Action |
|----------|--------|
| **1** | Ensure physical safety (safe room, environmental check, remove harmful items) |
| **2** | Initiate 1:1 continuous observation |
| **3** | Complete a thorough suicide risk assessment and document findings |
| **4** | Notify psychiatry for emergency evaluation |
| **5** | Facilitate therapeutic communication — allow patient to express feelings |
| **6** | Collaborate with wife for lethal means assessment at home |

**Rationale:**
- **Safety first — always.** Before anything therapeutic can happen, the environment must be safe. Check the room, remove items, ensure the patient cannot access means.
- **1:1 observation ensures continuous safety** while other assessments and interventions proceed.
- **Risk assessment** provides the clinical data needed for the psychiatry consult and drives the level of care decision.
- **Psychiatry notification** initiates the disposition process (admission vs. extended observation vs. crisis stabilization).
