- **Therapeutic communication** happens concurrently with assessment but is listed here as a distinct priority — the patient's emotional needs matter, and rapport building improves cooperation with treatment.
- **Means assessment at home** is essential before any eventual discharge planning and should happen as soon as the wife is available, even though discharge is not imminent.

→ See Section 4: Psychosocial Integrity (Crisis Intervention, Suicide Assessment)

---

## SCENARIO 6: Charge Nurse Delegation and Prioritization (Leadership)

### Clinical Setting
Medical-surgical unit, day shift. You are the charge nurse.

### Situation
It is 0700 and you are making assignments for the shift. Your team today consists of:
- **RN Maria** (5 years experience, med-surg certified)
- **RN Kevin** (new graduate, 3 months off orientation)
- **LPN Teresa** (10 years experience)
- **UAP/CNA Sam** (2 years experience)

You also need to prioritize which patients require assessment first.

### Patient Census

**Room 201 — Mr. Adams, 68 y/o.** Post-op day 1, total knee replacement. Vital signs stable overnight. PCA morphine for pain. Physical therapy scheduled at 0900. Needs vitals, assessment, AM care.

**Room 202 — Mrs. Bauer, 55 y/o.** Admitted last night with new-onset atrial fibrillation, rapid ventricular response (HR 142 on admission). Diltiazem drip initiated overnight. Last HR 98, irregular. Needs continuous monitoring, drip titration, and telemetry.

**Room 203 — Mr. Carter, 44 y/o.** Type 1 diabetes, admitted for cellulitis of left lower leg. IV antibiotics (vancomycin) q12h. On sliding scale insulin. Blood glucose at 0600 was 54 mg/dL — nurse gave juice, rechecked at 0615: 78 mg/dL. Breakfast arriving at 0730.

**Room 204 — Ms. Davis, 82 y/o.** CHF exacerbation, receiving IV furosemide. Strict I&O. Daily weight. K⁺ 3.3 last evening — KCl 20 mEq PO given. AM labs pending. Uses a walker, high fall risk. Needs AM care assistance.

**Room 205 — Mrs. Ellis, 32 y/o.** Admitted for observation after motor vehicle accident. CT scan negative. Cervical strain. Discharged today — needs discharge teaching, prescriptions, follow-up instructions.

**Room 206 — Mr. Foster, 71 y/o.** Receiving first unit of packed red blood cells (PRBCs) for anemia secondary to GI bleed. Transfusion started at 0630 by night shift RN. Needs 15-minute post-start vitals and continued monitoring.

---

### Question 1 — Recognize Cues (Matrix Format)

**Instructions:** For each patient, identify the acuity level and classify them as stable, potentially unstable, or unstable.

| Patient | Stable | Potentially Unstable | Unstable |
|---------|:-:|:-:|:-:|
| Room 201 — Mr. Adams (post-op knee, stable vitals) | **X** | | |
| Room 202 — Mrs. Bauer (new A-fib on diltiazem drip) | | **X** | |
| Room 203 — Mr. Carter (blood glucose 78 after hypoglycemic episode) | | **X** | |
| Room 204 — Ms. Davis (CHF, K⁺ 3.3, fall risk) | | **X** | |
| Room 205 — Mrs. Ellis (discharge today, stable) | **X** | | |
| Room 206 — Mr. Foster (active blood transfusion) | | **X** | |

**Rationale:**
- **Mr. Adams** — Post-op day 1 with stable vitals is a standard post-surgical patient. Stable.
- **Mrs. Bauer** — New A-fib on a continuous medication drip that may need titration. She could decompensate or develop complications from the arrhythmia or the medication. Potentially unstable.
- **Mr. Carter** — Had hypoglycemia this morning (54 mg/dL is dangerously low). Glucose recovered to 78, but he's on insulin and may fluctuate. Potentially unstable.
- **Ms. Davis** — CHF with hypokalemia (K⁺ 3.3 is below normal). On IV diuretics that deplete potassium. Fall risk. Potentially unstable.
- **Mrs. Ellis** — Observation patient cleared for discharge. CT negative. Stable.
- **Mr. Foster** — Active blood transfusion. Risk for transfusion reaction is highest in first 15 minutes and during the first unit. Potentially unstable.

---

### Question 2 — Prioritize Hypotheses (Ordered Response Format)

**Instructions:** In what order should the charge nurse assess patients at the start of shift? Rank from first to last.

| Priority | Patient | Rationale |
|----------|---------|-----------|
| **1** | Room 206 — Mr. Foster | Active transfusion started 30 min ago — needs first vital sign check NOW (15-min post-start). Transfusion reactions can be fatal and require immediate RN assessment. |
| **2** | Room 203 — Mr. Carter | Recent hypoglycemic episode. Glucose at 78 — needs breakfast and insulin management. Risk for another drop, especially on sliding scale. |
| **3** | Room 202 — Mrs. Bauer | On continuous diltiazem drip — needs assessment of rhythm, rate, and hemodynamic status. Drip may need titration. |
| **4** | Room 204 — Ms. Davis | Hypokalemia on loop diuretic. AM labs pending — needs results checked and K⁺ replacement evaluated. Fall risk needs to be addressed for morning activities. |
| **5** | Room 201 — Mr. Adams | Stable post-op patient. Routine assessment, pain management, pre-PT preparation. |
| **6** | Room 205 — Mrs. Ellis | Stable, being discharged. Teaching can happen after acute patients are assessed. |

---

### Question 3 — Generate Solutions (Extended Drag-and-Drop Format)

**Instructions:** Assign each patient to the most appropriate team member. Drag each patient to the correct staff member.

| Team Member | Assigned Patients | Rationale |
|-------------|------------------|-----------|
| **RN Maria** (experienced) | Room 202 — Mrs. Bauer (A-fib, drip) AND Room 206 — Mr. Foster (transfusion) | Most complex patients requiring experienced assessment. Diltiazem drip titration and blood transfusion monitoring require clinical judgment and experience. |
| **RN Kevin** (new grad) | Room 203 — Mr. Carter (DM, recent hypoglycemia) AND Room 205 — Mrs. Ellis (discharge) | Moderate complexity with learning opportunities. Charge nurse should check in on Kevin's glucose management and review his discharge teaching. |
| **LPN Teresa** (experienced LPN) | Room 201 — Mr. Adams (post-op knee) AND Room 204 — Ms. Davis (CHF) | Stable/predictable patients. Teresa can perform focused assessments, administer scheduled PO medications (KCl, other meds), and monitor I&O. The RN (charge nurse) performs the initial assessment and care plan, then LPN follows through. |
| **UAP Sam** | Assist all rooms | AM care for Mr. Adams and Ms. Davis. Vital signs on stable patients (201, 204, 205). Ambulation assistance. I&O collection. Accucheck on Mr. Carter at designated times. |

**Key delegation principles applied:**
- **RN only:** Blood transfusion monitoring, IV drip titration, initial assessments, discharge teaching, care of unstable patients
- **LPN scope:** Focused assessments on stable patients, PO/IM/SubQ medication administration, monitoring patients within established care plans
- **UAP scope:** Vital signs on stable patients, ADLs, I&O, accuchecks (collection, not interpretation)
- **New grad support:** Assign stable-to-moderate patients. Provide mentoring. Never assign the most complex patient to the newest nurse.

---

### Question 4 — Take Action (SATA Format)

**At 0815, UAP Sam reports to you: "Ms. Davis in room 204 tried to get up to the bathroom on her own. I found her holding onto the bed rail, saying she feels dizzy. Her blood pressure is 92/58."**

**Select all appropriate actions:**

- [ ] A. Instruct Sam to help Ms. Davis safely back to bed
- [ ] B. Go to room 204 immediately to assess the patient
- [ ] C. Ask Sam to recheck the blood pressure in 30 minutes
- [ ] D. Review the AM potassium level result
- [ ] E. Hold the scheduled furosemide dose until you assess the patient
- [ ] F. Delegate the assessment to LPN Teresa
- [ ] G. Notify the provider of the blood pressure and dizziness
- [ ] H. Instruct Sam to assist Ms. Davis to the bathroom since she needs to go
- [ ] I. Reassess fall risk and update the care plan
- [ ] J. Check the patient's urine output over the past 8 hours

**Correct Answers:** A, B, D, E, G, I, J

**Rationale:**
- **(A) Correct** — Immediate safety. Sam can safely assist her back to bed right now while you're on your way.
- **(B) Correct** — RN must assess. This is a change in condition — dizziness and hypotension in a patient on diuretics with known hypokalemia. Cannot delegate this assessment.
- **(C) Incorrect** — Waiting 30 minutes is inappropriate when a patient is hypotensive and dizzy. She could fall or lose consciousness.
- **(D) Correct** — The AM K⁺ result is critical. She was 3.3 last night — if it's still low or lower, this contributes to the clinical picture.
- **(E) Correct** — Furosemide would further decrease her blood volume and potassium. Hold until assessment is complete and provider is aware.
- **(F) Incorrect** — This is a change in condition requiring RN assessment, not a focused assessment an LPN can perform.
- **(G) Correct** — After your assessment, notify the provider. This represents a significant change from baseline.
- **(H) Incorrect** — She's hypotensive and dizzy — ambulating to the bathroom puts her at high fall risk. Offer a bedside commode or bedpan.
- **(I) Correct** — Fall risk has increased. Update the care plan: fall precautions, bed alarm, non-skid footwear, call light within reach, toileting schedule.
- **(J) Correct** — Urine output tells you how aggressively the diuretic is working. Excessive output without adequate fluid intake explains the hypotension.

→ See Section 1: Management of Care (Delegation, Prioritization, Assignment)

---

## SCENARIO 7: Polypharmacy and Drug Interactions (Pharmacology)

### Clinical Setting
Outpatient clinic, morning appointment.

### Patient Information
**Mrs. Helen O'Brien, 74 years old.** Presents for routine follow-up. Medical history: atrial fibrillation, type 2 diabetes, osteoarthritis, GERD, chronic pain (lumbar stenosis), insomnia, and depression. She sees three different providers (cardiologist, PCP, pain specialist) and uses two pharmacies.

### Current Medication List (brought in by patient)
1. Warfarin 5 mg daily (cardiologist)
2. Metformin 1000 mg BID (PCP)
3. Glipizide 5 mg BID (PCP)
4. Omeprazole 20 mg daily (PCP)
5. Naproxen 500 mg BID (pain specialist — started 2 weeks ago)
6. Acetaminophen 1000 mg TID (OTC, self-directed)
7. Sertraline 100 mg daily (PCP)
8. Zolpidem 10 mg at bedtime (PCP)
9. Amlodipine 5 mg daily (cardiologist)
10. Fish oil supplement 2000 mg daily (self-directed)
11. Ginkgo biloba (self-directed, "for memory")

### Assessment Findings
- Patient reports new bruising on arms and legs over the past 2 weeks
- "My stomach has been bothering me — burning feeling"
- INR drawn today: 4.8 (therapeutic range for A-fib: 2–3)
- Fasting glucose: 62 mg/dL (she skipped breakfast to come to the appointment)
- BP: 134/76 mmHg, HR: 72 (irregular), RR: 16
- Reports occasional dizziness in the morning
- States: "I don't understand why I need so many pills. Sometimes I forget which ones to take."

---

### Question 1 — Recognize Cues (Highlight Format)

**Instructions:** Identify ALL concerning findings and problematic medication combinations.

**Concerning assessment findings:**
- [x] New bruising × 2 weeks
- [x] GI burning
- [x] INR 4.8 (supratherapeutic)
- [x] Fasting glucose 62 mg/dL (hypoglycemia)
- [x] Morning dizziness
- [x] Expresses confusion about medication regimen

**Problematic medication combinations:**
- [x] Warfarin + Naproxen (NSAID)
- [x] Warfarin + Fish Oil
- [x] Warfarin + Ginkgo biloba
- [x] Warfarin + Sertraline (SSRI)
- [x] Naproxen + GI symptoms (NSAID gastropathy)
- [x] Glipizide + skipped meal → hypoglycemia
- [x] Zolpidem + age 74 → fall risk + dizziness
- [x] Acetaminophen 3000 mg/day (approaching max, especially with concurrent warfarin)

**All are correct** — every finding and combination listed is clinically significant.

**Rationale:**
- **INR 4.8** — Dangerously supratherapeutic. Warfarin's effect is being amplified by MULTIPLE interactions:
  - **Naproxen** (started 2 weeks ago — timing matches the bruising): NSAIDs inhibit platelet function AND increase warfarin levels by competing for protein binding sites. Also causes GI erosion → GI bleed risk.
  - **Fish oil** at high doses has antiplatelet effects, potentiating bleeding risk with warfarin.
  - **Ginkgo biloba** inhibits platelet aggregation — another additive bleeding risk.
  - **Sertraline** (SSRI) inhibits platelet function and can increase INR when combined with warfarin.
  - **Result:** Four medications/supplements are all amplifying warfarin's effect → INR 4.8 + bruising.
- **Glucose 62** — Hypoglycemia. Glipizide (sulfonylurea) stimulates insulin secretion regardless of blood glucose. Skipping a meal + glipizide = hypoglycemia. Morning dizziness may be recurrent hypoglycemic episodes.
- **Acetaminophen 3000 mg/day** — While technically below the 3,000–4,000 mg/day limit, acetaminophen at this dose can increase INR in patients on warfarin.
- **Zolpidem 10 mg in a 74-year-old** — The American Geriatric Society Beers Criteria list zolpidem as potentially inappropriate in older adults. Starting dose for elderly females should be 5 mg. Associated with falls, confusion, and next-morning sedation (explaining the dizziness).
- **Three providers, two pharmacies** — This is the root cause. No single provider has the full picture. No single pharmacist is catching all interactions.

---

### Question 2 — Analyze Cues (Cloze/Drop-Down Format)

**Statement 1:** The patient's INR of 4.8 is most likely caused by the addition of **[naproxen / metformin / amlodipine / omeprazole]** two weeks ago, which **[increases warfarin metabolism / decreases warfarin absorption / inhibits platelets and displaces warfarin from protein binding / has no interaction with warfarin]**, compounded by concurrent use of **[fish oil and ginkgo biloba / metformin and glipizide / amlodipine and omeprazole / zolpidem and sertraline]**.

**Answer:** naproxen | inhibits platelets and displaces warfarin from protein binding | fish oil and ginkgo biloba

**Statement 2:** The patient's fasting glucose of 62 mg/dL is best explained by **[taking glipizide without eating breakfast / metformin overdose / warfarin interaction / sertraline side effect]**.

**Answer:** taking glipizide without eating breakfast

**Statement 3:** The polypharmacy risk in this patient is elevated because she **[sees multiple providers and uses multiple pharmacies / takes too many generic medications / is elderly / has atrial fibrillation]**, which increases the likelihood of **[undetected drug interactions / insurance fraud / medication refusal / therapeutic compliance]**.

**Answer:** sees multiple providers and uses multiple pharmacies | undetected drug interactions

---

### Question 3 — Generate Solutions (SATA Format)

**Instructions:** Select all appropriate nursing interventions.

- [ ] A. Hold the naproxen and contact the prescribing provider
- [ ] B. Administer vitamin K immediately
- [ ] C. Notify the cardiologist of the INR 4.8 and current medication list
- [ ] D. Provide a snack with protein and carbohydrate for the glucose of 62
- [ ] E. Educate the patient about the interaction between NSAIDs and warfarin
- [ ] F. Recommend discontinuing fish oil and ginkgo biloba and discuss with the provider
- [ ] G. Suggest a single pharmacy for all prescriptions
- [ ] H. Create a medication reconciliation list for all providers
- [ ] I. Administer a warfarin dose increase to reach therapeutic INR faster
- [ ] J. Recommend the provider reassess zolpidem dose for this patient's age
- [ ] K. Educate the patient never to skip meals while on glipizide
- [ ] L. Recheck the glucose in 15 minutes after the snack

**Correct Answers:** A, C, D, E, F, G, H, J, K, L

**Rationale:**
- **(A) Correct** — Naproxen is the primary driver of the INR elevation and should be held pending provider review.
- **(B) Incorrect** — Vitamin K is for actively bleeding patients or severely elevated INR. At 4.8 with only bruising (no active hemorrhage), the provider will decide whether to give vitamin K or simply hold warfarin and monitor. This is not a nursing decision in the outpatient setting.
- **(C) Correct** — The cardiologist managing warfarin needs to know the INR and the full medication picture.
- **(D) Correct** — Glucose 62 needs treatment. A snack with protein + carbs provides quick sugar and sustained glucose maintenance.
- **(E) Correct** — Patient teaching is core nursing. She needs to understand WHY these interact so she can advocate for herself with all providers.
- **(F) Correct** — Both supplements increase bleeding risk with warfarin. Discuss with the provider for a coordinated recommendation.
- **(G) Correct** — A single pharmacy catches drug interactions that multiple pharmacies miss. This is a systems-level safety intervention.
- **(H) Correct** — Medication reconciliation across all providers is essential. Send the complete list to every provider.
- **(I) Incorrect** — The INR is already dangerously high. Increasing warfarin would worsen the situation dramatically.
- **(J) Correct** — Zolpidem 10 mg is inappropriate for a 74-year-old per Beers Criteria. Recommend provider review.
- **(K) Correct** — Sulfonylureas (glipizide) cause hypoglycemia if meals are skipped. This is a critical teaching point.
- **(L) Correct** — Standard hypoglycemia protocol: treat, wait 15 minutes, recheck.

---

### Question 4 — Evaluate Outcomes (Matrix Format)

**Instructions:** At a follow-up visit 2 weeks later, assess whether these outcomes indicate the plan is working.

| Finding | Goal Met | Needs Adjustment |
|---------|:-:|:-:|
| INR: 2.4 (naproxen discontinued, supplements stopped) | **X** | |
| Patient using a single pharmacy | **X** | |
| Fasting glucose: 98 mg/dL (eating before taking glipizide) | **X** | |
| Patient reports hip pain "is terrible without the naproxen" | | **X** |
| Zolpidem reduced to 5 mg, patient reports still having trouble sleeping | | **X** |
| All three providers have the complete medication list | **X** | |
| Patient has a pill organizer and medication schedule posted on refrigerator | **X** | |

**Rationale:**
- INR, glucose, pharmacy consolidation, provider communication, and medication management are all on track.
- Pain management needs re-evaluation — the patient needs an alternative to NSAIDs that doesn't interact with warfarin. Options: acetaminophen (at appropriate doses with INR monitoring), topical analgesics, physical therapy, or a non-NSAID prescription.
- Insomnia needs further workup — sleep hygiene education, possible referral, or alternative medication. Consider melatonin, trazodone (lower interaction risk), or CBT for insomnia.

→ See Section 6: Pharmacological Therapies (Drug Interactions, Anticoagulants)

---

## SCENARIO 8: Trauma Patient in the ED (Emergency)

### Clinical Setting
Emergency department, trauma bay.

### Patient Information
**Mr. Ryan Torres, 28 years old.** Brought in by EMS after a motorcycle accident. Was traveling approximately 40 mph, lost control, struck a guardrail. Was wearing a helmet. Found on the roadside, conscious but confused. GCS on scene: 13 (E3, V4, M6). C-spine immobilized by EMS. One large-bore IV placed in the field, NS running wide open.

### EMS Report
- Estimated blood loss at scene: moderate (road rash, open left femur fracture with visible bone, small laceration to forehead)
- Patient was agitated and diaphoretic on scene
- Breath sounds diminished on left side per EMS
- Flight time 12 minutes

### Initial Trauma Assessment — 2045

**Primary Survey (ABCDE):**

| Component | Finding |
|-----------|---------|
| **A — Airway** | Patent, speaking in short phrases, C-collar in place |
| **B — Breathing** | RR 28, labored. Breath sounds diminished on LEFT. Trachea midline. O₂ sat 91% on 15L NRB. Left chest wall tenderness with crepitus on palpation. |
| **C — Circulation** | HR 128, BP 86/54. Skin cool, pale, diaphoretic. Cap refill 4 seconds. Left femur open fracture with active bleeding — pressure dressing applied by EMS, blood soaking through. Second IV placed, NS bolus initiated. |
| **D — Disability** | GCS now 12 (E3, V3, M6 — verbal response has decreased). Pupils equal and reactive 3mm. Moving all extremities except guarding left leg. |
| **E — Exposure** | Road rash to bilateral arms. 4 cm forehead laceration. Open fracture left mid-femur with bone visible. Abdomen soft, mildly distended. Pelvis stable on compression. |

**Initial Labs (point-of-care):**

| Test | Result |
|------|--------|
| Hemoglobin | 9.2 g/dL |
| Lactate | 5.8 mmol/L (normal <2) |
| pH (VBG) | 7.28 |
| Base excess | -8 |

---

### Question 1 — Recognize Cues (Ordered Response — Rank Threats by Severity)

**Instructions:** Rank the following injuries/findings from most immediately life-threatening to least.

| Priority | Finding | Rationale |
|----------|---------|-----------|
| **1** | Diminished breath sounds on left with crepitus and O₂ sat 91% | Possible pneumothorax or hemothorax — if tension develops, it's immediately fatal without intervention. Breathing is compromised (the "B" in ABCDE). |
| **2** | Hemorrhagic shock (HR 128, BP 86/54, lactate 5.8, Hgb 9.2, active bleeding from femur fracture) | Class III hemorrhagic shock (tachycardia, hypotension, altered mental status, >30% blood volume loss). Without blood and fluid resuscitation, this progresses to cardiac arrest. |
| **3** | Declining GCS (13 → 12, verbal worsening) | Could indicate intracranial hemorrhage, worsening brain perfusion from shock, or both. Needs serial monitoring and CT scan. |
| **4** | Open femur fracture with active bleeding | Source of hemorrhage (femur fractures can lose 1–2 liters of blood into the thigh). Needs surgical fixation but also contributes to the shock picture. |
| **5** | Acidosis (pH 7.28, lactate 5.8, base excess -8) | Reflects inadequate tissue perfusion (lactic acidosis from shock). Will correct as volume is restored. A marker of severity, not a separate problem to treat independently. |

---

### Question 2 — Generate Solutions (SATA Format)

**Instructions:** Select all appropriate immediate interventions for this trauma patient.

- [ ] A. Prepare for chest tube insertion on the left side
- [ ] B. Initiate massive transfusion protocol (MTP)
- [ ] C. Obtain a portable chest X-ray STAT
- [ ] D. Apply a tourniquet to the left leg
- [ ] E. Type and crossmatch; send trauma labs
- [ ] F. Insert a Foley catheter for urine output monitoring
- [ ] G. Obtain a CT head, chest, abdomen, pelvis
- [ ] H. Administer IV morphine for pain
- [ ] I. Apply a traction splint to the left femur fracture
- [ ] J. Administer tranexamic acid (TXA) if within time window
- [ ] K. Maintain C-spine immobilization
- [ ] L. Perform a FAST exam (bedside ultrasound)
- [ ] M. Administer sodium bicarbonate to correct the acidosis

**Correct Answers:** A, B, C, E, F, G, I, J, K, L

**Rationale:**
- **(A) Correct** — Diminished breath sounds + crepitus = likely pneumothorax or hemothorax. Prepare for chest tube. If tension pneumothorax develops (tracheal deviation, hypotension worsening, JVD), needle decompression first (2nd intercostal space, midclavicular line), then chest tube.
- **(B) Correct** — With active hemorrhage, Hgb 9.2, lactate 5.8, and signs of Class III shock, MTP should be activated. MTP provides a balanced ratio of PRBCs, FFP, and platelets (typically 1:1:1).
- **(C) Correct** — Portable CXR confirms pneumo/hemothorax and guides chest tube placement. Do not delay life-saving intervention for imaging if tension pneumothorax is suspected.
- **(D) Incorrect** — A tourniquet is used for life-threatening extremity hemorrhage that cannot be controlled with direct pressure. The EMS pressure dressing is in place. An open femur fracture bleeds significantly, but a traction splint (see I) is the appropriate device — it reduces the fracture and tamponades bleeding in the thigh compartment.
- **(E) Correct** — Type and crossmatch is essential for blood product administration. Trauma labs (CBC, CMP, coags, lactate, type/screen) provide the complete picture.
- **(F) Correct** — Foley catheter monitors UO as a marker of perfusion. Goal: ≥0.5 mL/kg/hr (≥14 mL/hr for this patient if ~70 kg). Check for urethral blood before insertion (pelvic fracture can cause urethral injury — though pelvis was stable on exam).
- **(G) Correct** — Once stabilized enough for transport, CT of head, chest, abdomen, pelvis is the trauma imaging standard. The declining GCS makes head CT urgent.
- **(H) Incorrect** — IV morphine in a hypotensive, head-injured patient is dangerous. Opioids cause vasodilation (worsening hypotension) and respiratory depression. They also alter the neurological exam, making serial GCS unreliable. Pain management in trauma: small titrated doses of ketamine or fentanyl (shorter acting) may be considered by the provider once hemodynamically stabilized.
- **(I) Correct** — A traction splint (e.g., Hare or Sager splint) immobilizes the femur fracture, reduces pain, decreases muscle spasm, and helps tamponade bleeding within the thigh. Standard trauma intervention for femur fractures.
- **(J) Correct** — Tranexamic acid (TXA) reduces mortality in hemorrhagic trauma if given within 3 hours of injury. It's an antifibrinolytic — prevents clot breakdown.
- **(K) Correct** — C-spine immobilization is maintained until clinically or radiographically cleared. Mechanism of injury (motorcycle at 40 mph) warrants full precautions.
- **(L) Correct** — FAST (Focused Assessment with Sonography for Trauma) looks for free fluid in the abdomen, pelvis, and pericardium. His abdomen is mildly distended — FAST will determine if there's intra-abdominal hemorrhage requiring surgery.
- **(M) Incorrect** — Sodium bicarbonate does not treat lactic acidosis from hemorrhagic shock. The acidosis will correct when perfusion is restored with volume resuscitation. Giving bicarb can actually worsen intracellular acidosis.

---

### Question 3 — Evaluate Outcomes (Matrix Format)

**Instructions:** Thirty minutes after MTP initiation, chest tube placement (400 mL blood evacuated), traction splint application, and 2 units PRBCs, reassess.

| Finding at 2115 | Improving | Concerning |
|-----------------|:-:|:-:|
| BP 98/64 (from 86/54) | **X** | |
| HR 112 (from 128) | **X** | |
| O₂ sat 96% on 15L NRB (from 91%) | **X** | |
| Breath sounds present bilaterally (were diminished left) | **X** | |
| Chest tube output: 400 mL initial, now draining 150 mL/hr | | **X** |
| GCS 12 (unchanged) | | **X** |
| Lactate 4.2 (from 5.8) | **X** | |
| Urine output: 20 mL in first 30 min | **X** | |
| FAST exam: positive for free fluid in LUQ (Morrison's pouch) | | **X** |

**Rationale:**
- BP, HR, oxygenation, breath sounds, and lactate are all trending in the right direction with resuscitation.
- **Chest tube output 150 mL/hr is concerning** — if chest tube output exceeds 200 mL/hr for 2–3 hours (or >1,500 mL total initially), this may indicate ongoing intrathoracic hemorrhage requiring surgical intervention (thoracotomy).
- **GCS unchanged at 12** — Not improving despite improved perfusion. This raises concern for intracranial pathology. CT head becomes more urgent. Serial neuro checks must continue.
- **Positive FAST exam** — Free fluid in Morrison's pouch (between liver and right kidney) indicates intra-abdominal hemorrhage. With the mechanism and abdominal distension, this patient likely needs surgical exploration (exploratory laparotomy). Notify the surgical team.

→ See Section 2: Safety & Infection Control (Emergency Protocols) and Section 8: Physiological Adaptation (Shock)

---

## SCENARIO 9: Chronic Kidney Disease with Electrolyte Imbalances (Renal)

### Clinical Setting
Medical-surgical unit, day shift.

### Patient Information
**Mr. William Jackson, 63 years old.** Admitted for management of end-stage renal disease (CKD Stage 5, GFR 11 mL/min). Has been on hemodialysis MWF for 2 years via left AV fistula. Missed his last two dialysis sessions due to transportation issues. Medical history: type 2 diabetes (20 years), hypertension, peripheral neuropathy. Medications: insulin glargine 22 units at bedtime, insulin lispro sliding scale, sevelamer (phosphate binder) TID with meals, epoetin alfa weekly, calcitriol 0.25 mcg daily, amlodipine 10 mg daily, lisinopril 20 mg daily.

### Nurse's Assessment — 0730

**Subjective:**
- "My legs are swollen up like balloons"
- "I can barely breathe when I lie flat"
- "I've been having muscle cramps and my hands feel tingly"
- "I'm so tired I can barely move"
- "I haven't been able to get to dialysis — my ride fell through"
- Reports nausea, poor appetite × 3 days, metallic taste in mouth

**Objective:**

| Parameter | Value |
|-----------|-------|
| Temperature | 97.6°F (36.4°C) |
| Heart Rate | 96 bpm |
| Blood Pressure | 188/104 mmHg |
| Respiratory Rate | 24/min |
| O₂ Saturation | 90% on room air |
| Weight | 207 lbs (94 kg) — dry weight 192 lbs (87.3 kg) |

**Physical Exam:**
- Periorbital edema bilateral
- Bilateral crackles in lower lung fields
- 3+ pitting edema bilateral lower extremities to knees
- AV fistula left forearm: bruit and thrill present
- Skin: sallow, dry, excoriation marks from scratching (pruritus)
- Kussmaul-type respirations
- Asterixis present (flapping hand tremor)

**Lab Results:**

| Test | Result | Reference |
|------|--------|-----------|
| BUN | 98 mg/dL | 5–20 |
| Creatinine | 11.4 mg/dL | 0.6–1.3 |
| GFR | 8 mL/min | >60 normal |
| Potassium | 6.2 mEq/L | 3.5–5.0 |
| Sodium | 132 mEq/L | 135–145 |
| Calcium | 7.4 mg/dL | 8.6–10.0 |
| Phosphorus | 7.8 mg/dL | 2.7–4.5 |
| Magnesium | 2.8 mg/dL | 1.6–2.6 |
| Hemoglobin | 8.6 g/dL | 14–16.5 (male) |
| Hematocrit | 26% | 42–52% |
| pH | 7.24 | 7.35–7.45 |
| HCO₃ | 14 mEq/L | 22–26 |
| Albumin | 2.8 g/dL | 3.4–5.0 |

---

### Question 1 — Recognize Cues (Matrix Format)

**Instructions:** Categorize each finding as related to fluid overload, uremia, electrolyte imbalance, or anemia.

| Finding | Fluid Overload | Uremia | Electrolyte Imbalance | Anemia |
|---------|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|
| Bilateral crackles, 3+ edema, periorbital edema, 15 lb weight gain | **X** | | | |
| Orthopnea, O₂ sat 90%, dyspnea | **X** | | | |
| BP 188/104 | **X** | | | |
| Nausea, poor appetite, metallic taste | | **X** | | |
| Asterixis (flapping tremor) | | **X** | | |
| Pruritus with excoriation | | **X** | | |
| BUN 98, Creatinine 11.4 | | **X** | | |
| K⁺ 6.2 | | | **X** | |
| Ca²⁺ 7.4, PO₄ 7.8 | | | **X** | |
| Tingling hands, muscle cramps | | | **X** | |
| pH 7.24, HCO₃ 14 | | | **X** | |
| Hgb 8.6, Hct 26% | | | | **X** |
| Fatigue | **X** | **X** | | **X** |
| Sallow skin | | **X** | | **X** |

**Rationale:**
- **Fluid overload:** Without dialysis for 2 sessions, fluid accumulates. Weight gain of 15 lbs (6.7 kg ≈ 6.7 L fluid). This causes pulmonary congestion (crackles, dyspnea, orthopnea), peripheral edema, periorbital edema, and hypertension.
- **Uremia:** When kidneys fail, urea and other waste products accumulate. This causes the metallic taste, nausea/anorexia (uremic GI effects), pruritus (urea crystals depositing in skin), and asterixis (a neurological sign of metabolic encephalopathy — indicates the uremia is affecting the brain).
- **Electrolyte imbalances:** K⁺ 6.2 is dangerously high — cardiac arrest risk. Hypocalcemia (7.4) and hyperphosphatemia (7.8) reflect the inverse Ca²⁺/PO₄ relationship in renal failure (kidneys can't excrete phosphorus → phosphorus binds calcium → calcium drops). Metabolic acidosis (pH 7.24, HCO₃ 14) occurs because the kidneys can't excrete hydrogen ions or regenerate bicarbonate. Tingling and cramps are from hypocalcemia.
- **Anemia:** Hgb 8.6 is chronically low in ESRD because the kidneys produce less erythropoietin (hence the epoetin alfa prescription). Missing dialysis doesn't directly cause anemia, but the chronic anemia compounds his fatigue and dyspnea.

---

### Question 2 — Prioritize Hypotheses (Cloze/Drop-Down Format)

**The most immediately life-threatening electrolyte abnormality is **[hyperkalemia / hypocalcemia / hyperphosphatemia / metabolic acidosis]** because it can cause **[seizures / cardiac arrhythmia and arrest / bone disease / respiratory failure]**.

**Answer:** hyperkalemia | cardiac arrhythmia and arrest

**The patient's fluid overload symptoms are primarily caused by **[missed dialysis sessions / excessive fluid intake / medication side effects / heart failure]**, and the definitive treatment is **[IV furosemide / fluid restriction / emergent hemodialysis / paracentesis]**.

**Answer:** missed dialysis sessions | emergent hemodialysis

---

### Question 3 — Take Action (Ordered Response Format)

**Instructions:** Prioritize the nursing actions.

| Priority | Action |
|----------|--------|
| **1** | Place on continuous cardiac monitoring and obtain a 12-lead EKG (K⁺ 6.2 — assess for peaked T waves, widened QRS) |
| **2** | Administer IV calcium gluconate as ordered (cardiac membrane stabilizer — protects the heart while other treatments lower K⁺) |
| **3** | Administer IV regular insulin + dextrose as ordered (shifts K⁺ into cells temporarily) |
| **4** | Apply supplemental oxygen and elevate HOB (address dyspnea and O₂ sat 90%) |
| **5** | Notify the dialysis team for emergent hemodialysis |
| **6** | Hold lisinopril (ACE inhibitors raise K⁺ — contraindicated with K⁺ 6.2) |

**Rationale:**
- **Cardiac monitoring first** because K⁺ 6.2 can cause fatal arrhythmias without warning. The EKG tells you HOW the heart is responding to the hyperkalemia right now.
- **Calcium gluconate** doesn't lower K⁺ — it stabilizes the cardiac membrane to prevent arrhythmia while definitive treatment is arranged. Given IV slow push with cardiac monitoring.
- **Insulin + glucose** is a temporizing measure — insulin drives K⁺ into cells, lowering serum K⁺ for 4–6 hours. D50 prevents hypoglycemia (this patient is already on insulin — double risk).
- **Oxygen and positioning** — O₂ sat 90% warrants supplemental O₂. Elevating HOB helps with the fluid-overloaded lungs.
- **Emergent dialysis** is the definitive treatment for ALL of this patient's problems — it removes excess fluid, potassium, phosphorus, urea, and corrects the acidosis.
- **Hold lisinopril** — ACE inhibitors cause potassium retention. With K⁺ already at 6.2, continuing lisinopril is dangerous.

---

### Question 4 — Evaluate Outcomes (Matrix Format)

**Instructions:** After emergent hemodialysis session (4 hours), reassess.

| Post-Dialysis Finding | Expected | Concerning |
|----------------------|:-:|:-:|
| Weight: 196 lbs (removed 11 lbs/5 L fluid) | **X** | |
| BP: 148/86 | **X** | |
| K⁺: 4.8 | **X** | |
| O₂ sat: 95% on 2L NC | **X** | |
| Crackles resolved, edema reduced to 1+ | **X** | |
| BUN: 52 (from 98) | **X** | |
| Patient reports muscle cramps and leg weakness | | **X** |
| Patient reports feeling "much better, can breathe again" | **X** | |
| AV fistula: bruit and thrill present | **X** | |
| BP drops to 88/52 after sitting up | | **X** |

**Rationale:**
- Most findings show excellent response to dialysis — fluid removed, K⁺ normalized, oxygenation improved, uremic symptoms reducing.
- **Muscle cramps and weakness** — Common after dialysis due to rapid fluid/electrolyte shifts. Could also indicate the K⁺ dropped too quickly. Recheck electrolytes. Provide comfort measures.
- **Orthostatic hypotension (188/104 → 148/86 supine → 88/52 sitting)** — Indicates too much fluid was removed or the patient was fluid-depleted. This is a common post-dialysis complication. Hold antihypertensives, position supine with legs elevated, administer small NS bolus if ordered, monitor closely. Reassess dry weight accuracy.
- **AV fistula bruit and thrill** — Always check after dialysis. Presence of both confirms fistula patency. Absent bruit/thrill = clotted fistula = emergency (→ See Section 7: Reduction of Risk Potential).

→ See Section 8: Physiological Adaptation (Renal Failure) and Quick Reference Card 2 (Electrolyte Imbalances)

---

## SCENARIO 10: Post-Stroke Patient with Swallowing Difficulties (Neuro/Rehab)

### Clinical Setting
Acute rehabilitation unit, day shift.

### Patient Information
**Mrs. Rosa Martinez, 69 years old.** Transferred from the neuro ICU 2 days ago. Had an ischemic stroke (left MCA territory) 5 days ago. Received alteplase (tPA) within the treatment window. Residual deficits: right-sided hemiparesis (arm weaker than leg), expressive aphasia (understands commands but has difficulty producing words), and dysphagia (difficulty swallowing). A bedside swallow evaluation by speech-language pathology (SLP) placed her on a dysphagia diet: pureed foods with nectar-thick liquids. NG tube was removed yesterday after she passed a modified barium swallow for pureed textures. Current medications: aspirin 81 mg daily, atorvastatin 80 mg daily, lisinopril 10 mg daily (to be crushed and mixed with applesauce per pharmacy), metoprolol 25 mg BID (to be crushed), enoxaparin 40 mg SubQ daily.

### Nurse's Assessment — 0800

**Neuro Exam:**
- Alert, oriented to person, place, time (nods appropriately to questions, difficulty verbalizing)
- Follows 2-step commands
- Expressive aphasia: uses single words, gestures, and nods effectively. Becomes frustrated when unable to communicate
- Right arm: 2/5 strength (gravity eliminated), right leg: 3/5 strength (antigravity)
- Left side: 5/5 strength
- Facial droop right side present
- Gag reflex present but diminished

**Speech-Language Pathology Notes (from yesterday):**
Modified barium swallow results: silent aspiration observed with thin liquids; mild aspiration with regular solids. Safe for pureed foods and nectar-thick liquids with supervised feeding. Recommend: upright position ≥30 min before and 30–60 min after meals, chin tuck during swallowing, small bites, assess for signs of aspiration during and after meals.

### Incident — 0830
The UAP (certified nursing assistant) assigned to Mrs. Martinez is helping with breakfast. You enter the room and observe the following:
- Patient is reclined at approximately 30 degrees (not upright)
- UAP is offering regular scrambled eggs (not pureed)
- A cup of water with a straw is on the tray table within reach
- Patient is coughing intermittently while eating
- UAP states: "She seemed hungry and the pureed stuff looked gross, so I got her regular eggs from the kitchen. She's doing fine."

---

### Question 1 — Recognize Cues (Highlight Format)

**Instructions:** Identify ALL safety concerns in this situation.

- [x] A. Patient reclined at ~30° instead of upright (90°)
- [x] B. UAP providing regular scrambled eggs instead of pureed diet
- [x] C. Thin water with straw on tray table (patient ordered nectar-thick liquids)
- [x] D. Patient coughing while eating
- [x] E. UAP changed diet order independently
- [x] F. Straw use (not recommended for dysphagia patients)

**All are correct.**

**Rationale:**
- **(A)** SLP ordered upright position for meals. At 30°, gravity doesn't adequately protect the airway. She should be at 90° (high Fowler's) or in a chair.
- **(B)** She was cleared for pureed foods ONLY. Regular scrambled eggs are a standard/soft texture — NOT pureed. This exceeds her swallow evaluation clearance and puts her at risk for aspiration.
- **(C)** She has documented silent aspiration with thin liquids. Water without thickener is thin liquid. This is extremely dangerous — she aspirates thin liquids without coughing (silent aspiration), so neither she nor the UAP would know she's aspirating.
- **(D)** Coughing during eating is a sign of aspiration or aspiration risk. In a patient with known dysphagia, this is a red flag.
- **(E)** A UAP does not have the authority to change a diet order. This is a scope-of-practice violation. Diet modifications require SLP evaluation and a provider order.
- **(F)** Straws deliver liquid to the back of the mouth faster than the patient can control, increasing aspiration risk. Dysphagia patients should drink from a cup.

---

### Question 2 — Take Action (Ordered Response Format)

**Instructions:** Prioritize your immediate actions.

| Priority | Action |
|----------|--------|
| **1** | Stop the meal immediately and remove the tray |
| **2** | Sit the patient fully upright (90°) and assess respiratory status (RR, O₂ sat, lung sounds) |
| **3** | Assess for signs of aspiration (coughing, wet/gurgling voice quality, tachypnea, desaturation) |
| **4** | Suction equipment to bedside if not already present |
| **5** | Educate the UAP on dysphagia diet restrictions and scope of practice |
| **6** | Document the incident and notify the provider and SLP |

**Rationale:**
- **Stop the unsafe activity first.** The patient is actively being fed the wrong food in the wrong position. Stop it.
- **Position and assess** — Sitting upright helps protect the airway. Assess respiratory status to determine if aspiration has already occurred.
- **Aspiration assessment** — Listen for wet/gurgling voice (ask her to say "ah"), auscultate lung bases for new crackles, check O₂ sat. Remember: she has documented SILENT aspiration with thin liquids — she may have aspirated water without anyone noticing.
- **Suction equipment** — Should always be at bedside for dysphagia patients. If aspiration occurred, suctioning may be needed.
- **UAP education** — This is a teaching moment. The UAP needs to understand: (1) dysphagia diet orders are a safety measure, not a preference; (2) changing a diet order is outside UAP scope; (3) aspiration pneumonia is a leading cause of death after stroke. This should be firm but educational, not punitive.
- **Documentation and notification** — This is a near-miss safety event. Document what happened, your assessment findings, and actions taken. The provider needs to know in case a chest X-ray or monitoring is warranted. SLP should reassess if any concern for aspiration.

---

### Question 3 — Generate Solutions (SATA Format)

**Instructions:** For ongoing care, select all appropriate nursing interventions for this patient's dysphagia management.

- [ ] A. Ensure upright position ≥30 minutes before meals and 30–60 minutes after meals
- [ ] B. Teach the patient the chin-tuck swallowing technique
- [ ] C. Place all medications in thin water for swallowing
- [ ] D. Supervise all meals (do not delegate unsupervised feeding to UAP)
- [ ] E. Provide oral care before and after meals
- [ ] F. Offer small bites and allow adequate time between bites
- [ ] G. Place food on the RIGHT side of the mouth (affected side)
- [ ] H. Place food on the LEFT side of the mouth (unaffected side)
- [ ] I. Use a communication board or picture cards during meals
- [ ] J. Monitor for aspiration pneumonia signs: fever, productive cough, dyspnea, crackles
- [ ] K. Keep suction equipment at the bedside
- [ ] L. Encourage the patient to clear her throat or double-swallow after each bite

**Correct Answers:** A, B, D, E, F, H, I, J, K, L

**Rationale:**
- **(A) Correct** — Positioning per SLP orders. Gravity + upright position protects airway. Remaining upright after meals prevents reflux/aspiration.
- **(B) Correct** — Chin tuck positions the epiglottis to better protect the airway during swallowing. This is a compensatory strategy recommended by SLP.
- **(C) Incorrect** — She cannot have thin liquids. Crush medications and mix with applesauce or nectar-thick liquid per pharmacy instructions. Some medications cannot be crushed (check each one).
- **(D) Correct** — Given the UAP's error, direct RN supervision of meals is warranted until the UAP demonstrates understanding of dysphagia precautions. At minimum, meals should be supervised by a trained staff member.
- **(E) Correct** — Oral care reduces bacterial load in the mouth, decreasing the risk of aspiration pneumonia if aspiration occurs. Also stimulates appetite and improves taste sensation.
- **(F) Correct** — Small bites reduce choking risk. Adequate time between bites allows complete swallowing before the next bite.
- **(G) Incorrect** — Placing food on the affected side (right — facial droop, weakness) increases the risk of pocketing food and aspiration. Food should go on the strong/unaffected side.
- **(H) Correct** — Place food on the LEFT (unaffected) side where sensation and motor control are intact.
- **(I) Correct** — Mrs. Martinez has expressive aphasia. A communication board helps her signal hunger, fullness, discomfort, or choking without the frustration of verbal communication.
- **(J) Correct** — Aspiration pneumonia can develop 24–72 hours after aspiration. Monitor for fever, new crackles, productive cough, increased O₂ requirements, and elevated WBC.
- **(K) Correct** — Suction equipment should always be at bedside for dysphagia patients.
- **(L) Correct** — Throat clearing and double-swallowing help ensure the pharynx is clear before the next bite, reducing residual-food aspiration risk.

---

### Question 4 — Evaluate Outcomes (Cloze/Drop-Down Format)

**Instructions:** Three days later, evaluate the patient's progress.

**Mrs. Martinez has been eating pureed meals without coughing episodes. Her O₂ saturation remains 96–98% on room air. She is gaining 1–2 lbs (likely nutritional recovery, not fluid). SLP has advanced her diet trial to **[thin liquids / mechanical soft foods with nectar-thick liquids / regular diet / NPO pending repeat swallow study]** after a repeat swallow evaluation.

**Answer:** mechanical soft foods with nectar-thick liquids

**The fact that she has had no fever, no new crackles on auscultation, and no increased O₂ requirements over the past 3 days suggests **[aspiration pneumonia has developed / the dysphagia precautions are preventing aspiration / she no longer has dysphagia / the stroke is resolving]**.

**Answer:** the dysphagia precautions are preventing aspiration

**The UAP assigned to Mrs. Martinez today correctly sets up her meal tray with pureed food, removes the water pitcher, and brings nectar-thick juice. Before leaving, the UAP **[should begin feeding the patient independently since she knows the diet / should notify the nurse that the meal is ready for supervised feeding / should ask the patient what she wants to eat from the regular menu / can delegate feeding to a family member]**.

**Answer:** should notify the nurse that the meal is ready for supervised feeding

**Rationale:**
- Diet advancement follows SLP evaluation. Progress is typically: NPO → pureed + thickened liquids → mechanical soft + thickened liquids → soft → regular. Each step requires SLP assessment. She's progressing appropriately.
- Absence of pneumonia signs confirms the aspiration prevention strategies are working. Dysphagia precautions are effective.
- Even with the UAP correctly setting up the tray, supervised feeding by trained staff remains the standard for this patient. The UAP appropriately notifying the nurse allows the nurse to supervise or delegate with clear instructions and verification of competency.

→ See Section 8: Physiological Adaptation (Stroke) and Section 5: Basic Care & Comfort (Nutrition, Dysphagia Management)
